(23)

Definition 1.12
(
Ricci curvature scalar
)
–

The Ricci curvature scalar (or Ricci scalar)
R
is the trace or the Ricci tensor, namely

\displaystyle R=g^{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu}}}}=\mathchoice{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}\,.

(24)

Remark 1.4
(
Identities of the curvature tensors
)
–

The Riemann curvature tensor satisfy the following identities

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=-R_{\nu\mu\rho\sigma}=-R_{\mu\nu\sigma\rho}\,,

(25)

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}\,,

(26)

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}+R_{\mu\sigma\nu\rho}+R_{\mu\rho\sigma\nu}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(27)

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma;\alpha}+R_{\mu\nu\alpha\rho;\sigma}+R_{\mu\nu\sigma\alpha;\rho}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(28)

The last two formulas are the Bianchi identities. Moreover, the Ricci curvature tensor is symmetric, i.e.

R_{\mu\nu}=R_{\nu\mu}\,.

(29)

The covariant derivative is very useful and allows us to define the concept of parallel transport.

Definition 1.13
(
Parallel transport
)
–

A vector
\boldsymbol{u}=u^{\mu}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mu}
is parallel transported along a vector
\boldsymbol{v}=v^{\mu}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mu}
if

\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{v}}\boldsymbol{u}=0\,,

(30)

or in components

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{v}}u^{\nu}=v^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}u^{\nu}=0\,.

(31)

A trajectory
x^{\mu}(\tau)
, where
\tau
is an affine parameter, is a geodesics if the 4-velocity
u^{\mu}=\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}/\mathrm{d}\tau
is parallel transported along itself. This concept is central if one works in curved spacetime, as it generalizes the notion of straight line (or the one of the shortest path between two point). It is also a key concept in the theory of
GR
as we will explain below.

2
Einstein Field Equations

We defined in the previous section the metric, and derived various tensors from it. However, one question still remains: For a given physical situation, how can we determine the tensor
\boldsymbol{g}(x)
at any spacetime point?

We know from Newtonian Mechanics that the matter density
\rho
determines the gravitational potential
\Phi
through the Poisson equation. We would like to find the generalisation of this relation in
GR
. This can be done starting with an action and using the variational principle. The Einstein-Hilbert action is

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{EH}}[\boldsymbol{g},\psi]=\int\;\left(\frac{1}{16\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}R+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{m}}[\boldsymbol{g},\psi]\right)\sqrt{-g}\,\mathrm{d}x^{4}x\,,

(32)

where
\psi
denotes collectively all the
matter
fields which we included in the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{m}}[\boldsymbol{g},\psi]
,
R
is the Ricci scalar built from
\boldsymbol{g}
and
g
its determinant, where
\boldsymbol{g}
seen as a matrix. The matter Lagrangian depends on the metric only through minimal coupling with matter (e.g. kinetic terms are expressed with the covariant derivatives). We do not explain here why we take this Ansatz as the correct action describing gravity. Rather, we take it as a
Deus ex machina
, and derive its consequences.

Varying the Einstein-Hilbert action given by Eq. (
32
) with respect to the metric
\boldsymbol{g}
leads to the Einstein Field Equations (or Einstein’s Equations)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{G}

\displaystyle=8\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}\boldsymbol{T}\,,

(33)

\displaystyle G_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=8\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}T_{\mu\nu}\,,

(34)

where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{G}

\displaystyle\equiv\boldsymbol{R}-\frac{1}{2}R\boldsymbol{g}\,,

(35)

\displaystyle G_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\equiv R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Rg_{\mu\nu}\,.

(36)

is the Einstein tensor,
G_{\mathrm{N}}
is Newton’s constant, and

T_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{2}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta(\sqrt{-g}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{m}})}{\delta g^{\mu\nu}}\,,

(37)

is the energy-momentum tensor quantifying the matter and energy content of the spacetime.

Remark 1.5
(
Einstein’s Equations in vacuum
)
–

In vacuum, with
\boldsymbol{T}=0
, the Einstein’s Equations read
\boldsymbol{G}=0
. The trace of the Einstein tensor Eq. (
35
) is
3
3

3

Recall that the Newton constant is
G_{\mathrm{N}}\neq G
.

G=-R\,,

(38)

which implies

R_{\mu\nu}=G_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Gg_{\mu\nu}\,.

(39)

Hence, in vacuum, the condition
\boldsymbol{G}=0
is equivalent to
\boldsymbol{R}=0
.

One last comment regarding the consistency of this equation. The Einstein tensor is identically divergence free

\nabla_{\mu}\mathchoice{G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=0\,.

(40)

This condition is also sometimes called the Bianchi identity. From the Einstein’s Equations, it implies

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}\mathchoice{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(41)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(42)

This last condition is nothing else as the energy-momentum conservation equation! This is a safety check for the validity of the Eintein’s Equations, as we know from Special Relativity and from Electrodynamics that the energy-momentum tensor should satisfy such a condition.

3
Motion of Particles

The Einstein’s Equations are the machinery to determine the metric
\boldsymbol{g}
if the distribution of matter and energy is known. However, the dynamics of the particle is itself governed by the metric structure of spacetime. We would like to derive the equations of motion for particles moving inside a gravitational field.

The starting point is, as usual, an action. We consider a particle whose trajectory is given by
x^{\mu}(\lambda)
, where
\lambda
is an affine parameter. This means that we can reparametrise it as
\tilde{\lambda}=\tilde{\lambda}(\lambda)
, provided the relation is a bijection. From this trajectory, we define the
4
-velocity through

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=u^{\mu}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mu}\,,u^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\,.

(43)

The
4
-velocity together with the metric allows us to compute the infinitesimal time (or distance) between
\lambda
and
\lambda+\mathrm{d}\lambda
as

\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}=g_{\mu\nu}\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}=g_{\mu\nu}u^{\mu}u^{\nu}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{2}=\boldsymbol{u}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{2}\,.

(44)

Moreover, we know from Relativistic Mechanics that this quantity is
0
if the particle is massless, and negative if it is massive. In addition, we can, in the case of a massive particle, adjust the affine parameter such that
\boldsymbol{u}^{2}=-1
, which we will assume from now on.

We claim now that the relativistic action for a particle is simple the total proper time of the trajectory

\mathcal{S}\propto-\int\;\mathrm{d}\tau=-\int\;\sqrt{-g_{\mu\nu}u^{\mu}u^{\nu}}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\,.

(45)

We do not specify here the correct prefactor as it can differ between the massless and the massive case.

Varying the action Eq. (
45
) with respect to the trajectory
x^{\mu}
leads to the equations of motion

\displaystyle\ddot{x}^{\mu}+\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}\dot{x}^{\alpha}\dot{x}^{\alpha}=0\,.

(46)

These equations are equivalent to the geodesics condition

\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\boldsymbol{u}=0

(47)

mentioned in Section
1
which is the final result of this Chapter: Particles, whether they are massive or massless, follow geodesic when moving on a curved spacetime. Note that this does not hold in some theories of Modified Gravity when the action for matter particles can be different (we will see such an example in Part
2
).

Chapter 2
Units and Observables

In this chapter we discuss the concepts of units and observables. Even if this topics can seem boring or not worth your time, I do think that a thorough discussion is always enlightening. We first discuss the natural system of units from a more conceptual point of view as what is usually done, and then we try to give a (qualitative) definition of the notion of observables.

Menu of the Chapter

1
Units

1
Introduction

After replying something like
The speed of the ball is
28
, Many students have heard their Physics teacher shouting
28
what? Oranges? Mangos?
In Science, and often in general in life, it does not make sense to describe quantities without units. The only exception being dimensionless number, when separable objects can be counted. For many people, scientists included, units are a nightmare not worth spending time. I remember the nightmare it was, in middle school, when I was asked to draw tables to convert
119.94\text{\,}\mathrm{dm}
to
0.001\,199\,4\text{\,}\mathrm{dam}
…

Historically speaking, several unit systems have been created, in different epochs and countries, and it is difficult to see any rational behind it. Anyone who has been confronted with the differences between, e.g., the Anglo-Saxon and the SI system is familiar with these issues. The problem comes from the fact that Humans defined units using arbitrary rationales. For example, it seems that the Babylonians divided hours in minutes in
60
as they were counting in this basis. Together with astronomical considerations, this provides an empirical definition of the second. Later, a definition based on some physical process was defined: The second is defined such that the frequency of the emission of the atomic transition between the two hyperfine ground states of Caesium
133
is exactly
9\,192\,631\,770\text{\,}\mathrm{s}
. This number was obviously chosen such that the final result is close enough to the historical value. When the value of one second is defined, the meter can be defined as the distance travelled by light in vacuum in a given amount of time. Finally, the kilogram can be defined imposing the mass of a meter cube of water at its maximal density. Interestingly enough, there was for several decades a standard mass defining the kilogram and kept in Paris. It was clear that this solution was not perfect, as this object had to bee frequently renewed. The other units were defined in a similar fashion, but we will restrict ourselves to these three for the present discussion.

In
2018
basis of the units system were redefined. The philosophy is now different: Rather than defining units with reference objects, the value of various physical constants is defined to be
fixed
in the SI system. This constrains all the other units. For example, the frequency of the caesium
133
, the value of the speed of light
c
and of the reduced Planck constant
\hbar
are kept fixed in the SI system, which uniquely defined the second, the meter and the kilogram. This is the philosophy of the natural system, which we present below.

The point of this Section is to show that units are much more than boring tables. For example, they are the basis of dimensional analysis, which has saved me in the past and will save me in the future. They also provide a natural system of units in Theoretical Physics as we will discuss later. Finally, and more important, they are the first step towards the more general concept of observables, which we present in Section
2
.

2
The Natural System

We want to motivate the definition the natural system of units. As explained before, in this system, the values of some natural constants are kept fixed. We will restrict ourselves to time, lenghts and masses, and their respective SI units, second, meter and kilogram. To define precisely these three units, we need first the speed of light and the Planck constant. We introduce the following notation

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=c_{\mathrm{si}}\,\mathrm{m}\times\mathrm{s}^{-1}\,,

(1)

\displaystyle\hbar

\displaystyle=\hbar_{\mathrm{si}}\,\mathrm{kg}\times\mathrm{m}^{2}\mathrm{s}^{-1}\,.

(2)

Here, the values
c_{\mathrm{si}}
and
\hbar_{\mathrm{si}}
are the numerical dimensionless values of these constants when expressed in the SI system. Using the new definition of
2019
, they read

\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{si}}

\displaystyle\equiv$299\,792\,458$\,,

(4)

\displaystyle\hbar_{\mathrm{si}}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2\pi}$6.626\,070\,15\text{\times}{10}^{-34}$\,.

(5)

We need one more constant: The frequency of the caesium transition. However, this is not what is customarily done in the literature. We take the more usual approach where the proton charge is defined to be constant

e=e_{\mathrm{si}}\,\mathrm{C}\,,

(6)

with

e_{\mathrm{si}}\equiv$1.602\,176\,634\text{\times}{10}^{-19}$\,.

(7)

We stress here that the quantities
c_{\mathrm{si}}
,
\hbar_{\mathrm{si}}
and
e_{\mathrm{si}}
are
exact
and are not subjected to any experimental errors. To close the system, we need a third condition which is generally taken to be the definition of the electronvolt

1\,\mathrm{eV}\equiv e_{\mathrm{si}}\,\mathrm{J}=e_{\mathrm{si}}\,\mathrm{kg}\times\mathrm{m}^{2}\times\mathrm{s}^{-2}\,.

(8)

The three equations Eq. (
1
), Eq. (
2
) and Eq. (
8
) can be inverted to express the SI units as

\displaystyle 1\,\mathrm{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{e_{\mathrm{si}}}{c_{\mathrm{si}}\hbar_{\mathrm{si}}}\,c\hbar\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{eV}}\,,

(9)

\displaystyle 1\,\mathrm{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{e_{\mathrm{si}}}{\hbar_{\mathrm{si}}}\,\hbar\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{eV}}\,,

(10)

\displaystyle 1\,\mathrm{kg}

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{\mathrm{si}}^{2}}{e_{\mathrm{si}}}\,\frac{1}{c^{2}}\,\mathrm{eV}\,.

(11)

These relations form the basis of the natural system of units. They can be used to convert quantities from the SI system to the natural system. For example, Newton’s constant is given by

G_{\mathrm{N}}=G_{N,\mathrm{si}}\,\mathrm{m}^{3}\times\mathrm{kg}^{-1}\times\mathrm{s}^{-2}=\frac{G_{N,\mathrm{si}}e_{\mathrm{si}}^{2}}{c_{\mathrm{si}}^{5}\hbar_{\mathrm{si}}}\,c^{5}\hbar\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{eV}^{2}}\,.

(12)

Usually (if not always), people are lazy and just say
We set
c=\hbar=1
. What they actually mean is that physical quantities are written using powers of
c
,
\hbar
and
\mathrm{eV}
, and that at the end they artificially set
c=\hbar=1
. In the example above, this would give (using
G_{N,\mathrm{si}}\approx 6.67\times 10^{-11}
)

G=6.7\times 10^{-57}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{eV}^{2}}\,.

(13)

We also understand from Eq. (
9
), Eq. (
10
), and Eq. (
11
) why inverse times, inverse lengths and masses are said to have the inverse unit of an energy, as the powers of
\mathrm{eV}
in their expression agree.

Let us show how this formalism simplify the physical equations. We take as an example the equation for the Schwarzschild radius

R=2\frac{G_{\mathrm{N}}M}{c^{2}}\,.

(14)

We stress that this equation, in this form, holds in
every
unit systems. If we apply this in the SI system we get

R_{\mathrm{si}}\,\mathrm{m}=\left(2G_{N,\mathrm{si}}\,\mathrm{m}^{3}\times\mathrm{kg}^{-1}\times\mathrm{s}^{-2}\right)\,\left(M_{\mathrm{si}}\,\mathrm{kg}\,c_{\mathrm{si}}^{-2}\right)\,\left(\mathrm{m}^{-2}\times\mathrm{s}^{2}\right)\,,

(15)

or

R_{\mathrm{si}}=2\frac{G_{N,\mathrm{si}}M_{\mathrm{si}}}{c_{\mathrm{si}}^{2}}\,.

(16)

This is exactly what you would do to compute the Schwarzschild radius with a simple calculator: Plug the dimensionless number and you know that the final result should be expressed in meters.

We describe now how to rewrite this equation in the natural system. First, we want to see what is the dimensionless value for, say,
R
in the natural system. We know that
R
, as a physical quantity, does not depend on the unit system, i.e.

R=R_{\mathrm{si}}\,\mathrm{m}=R_{\mathrm{nat}}\,\frac{c\hbar}{\mathrm{eV}}\,,

(17)

where
R_{\mathrm{nat}}
is the dimensionless value of
R
in the natural system and we used Eq. (
9
) to know the form of a length in this system. Using again Eq. (
9
), we get

R_{\mathrm{nat}}=R_{\mathrm{si}}\frac{e_{\mathrm{si}}}{c_{\mathrm{si}}\hbar_{\mathrm{si}}}\,.

(18)

Similarly, we get for Newton’s constant and the mass

\displaystyle G_{N,\mathrm{nat}}

\displaystyle=G_{N,\mathrm{si}}\frac{e_{\mathrm{si}}^{2}}{c_{\mathrm{si}}^{5}\hbar_{\mathrm{si}}}\,,

(19)

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{nat}}

\displaystyle=M_{\mathrm{si}}\,\frac{c_{\mathrm{si}}^{2}}{e_{\mathrm{si}}}\,.

(20)

Using these relations and the fact that, in the SI system, the relation is given by Eq. (
16
), it is direct to show that

R_{\mathrm{nat}}=2G_{N,\mathrm{nat}}M_{\mathrm{nat}}\,,

(21)

or in other words: In the natural system, the physical equation are obtained setting
c_{\mathrm{nat}}=1
! It is very important however to note that the true physical equation is Eq. (
14
) involving dimensional quantities, while Eq. (
16
) or Eq. (
21
) are simply its translation in one system or another.

Remark 1.6
(
Our convention
)
–

In this work, we will be lazy and simply
set

c=\hbar=1
, without always mentioning it, but we encourage the reader to have the discussion of this Section in mind at all times! Hence, times, length and inverse masses have the same units.

Remark 1.7
(
Planck units
)
–

Using only
c
,
\hbar
and
G
, one can define (the factor of
8\pi
is a mere convention)

\displaystyle L_{\mathrm{Pl}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\hbar G_{\mathrm{N}}}{c^{3}}}\,,

(22)

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{Pl}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\hbar G_{\mathrm{N}}}{c^{5}}}\,,

(23)

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{Pl}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{c\hbar}{8\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}}\,.

(24)

It is direct to check that these quantities are respectively a length, time and mass. They are the Planks units. Their value in the SI system can be computed directly using the SI values of the three constant. The exercise is more interesting in the natural system. Using the definition of Newton’s constant in the natural system Eq (
13
), we get

\displaystyle L_{\mathrm{Pl}}

\displaystyle\approx 8\times 10^{-29}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{eV}}\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{c^{3}}}\,,

(25)

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{Pl}}

\displaystyle\approx 8\times 10^{-29}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{eV}}\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{c^{5}}}\,,

(26)

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{Pl}}

\displaystyle\approx 2\times 10^{27}\,\mathrm{eV}\sqrt{c\hbar}\,.

(27)

And, again, usually the factors
c
and
\hbar
are neglected.

3
The Vector Space of Physical Values

This section is inspired by the excellent reference
[
7
]
. We will follow the ideas presented there, with slight differences in the notations. Similar ideas were presented in
[
197
]
. Again, we only consider here physical quantities which can be expressed as powers of length, time and mass. This includes most of the quantities we want to study in this work (mass and energy density, pressure, frequency, …) and the generalisation to quantities with other units (for example temperature) is straightforward.

Any physical quantity
X
can be expressed as

X=10^{\alpha_{X}}L^{\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}}T^{\alpha_{\mathrm{T}}}M^{\alpha_{\mathrm{M}}}\,.

(28)

where
\alpha_{X}
is a dimensionless number,
L
,
T
and
M
are given units of length, time and mass (such as the meter, the second and the kilogram) and
\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}\,,\alpha_{\mathrm{T}}\,,\alpha_{\mathrm{M}}\in\mathbb{Z}
(or even
\mathbb{Q}
). The dimensionless number is written as a power of
10
for reasons that will become clear.

For example, if I use the SI system, my height
h
corresponds to

\left(\alpha_{h},\alpha_{\mathrm{L}},\alpha_{\mathrm{T}},\alpha_{\mathrm{M}}\right)=(0,1,0,0)

(29)

and my weight
w
corresponds to

(\alpha_{w},\alpha_{\mathrm{L}},\alpha_{\mathrm{T}},\alpha_{\mathrm{M}})=(3,1,-2,1)\,.

(30)

In these two examples, the first number is to be understood as a power of
10
, i.e.
h\sim 10^{0}\,\mathrm{m}
and
w\sim 10^{3}\,\mathrm{N}
. These two examples, yet simple, are very instructive: we understand that any physical quantity, provided a system of units has been chosen, can be fully determined through a
4
-dimensional vector.

In this Vector Space of Physical Values as they call it in the article, the addition and the multiplication of two physical quantities is defined by

\displaystyle(\alpha_{X},\alpha_{\mathrm{L}},\alpha_{\mathrm{T}},\alpha_{\mathrm{M}})\oplus(\alpha_{Y},\alpha_{\mathrm{L}},\alpha_{\mathrm{T}},\alpha_{\mathrm{M}})

\displaystyle\equiv(\log_{10}(10^{\alpha_{X}}+10^{\alpha_{Y}}),\alpha_{\mathrm{L}},\alpha_{\mathrm{T}},\alpha_{\mathrm{M}})\,,

(31)

\displaystyle(\alpha_{X},\alpha_{\mathrm{L}},\alpha_{\mathrm{T}},\alpha_{\mathrm{M}})\odot(\alpha_{Y},\beta_{\mathrm{L}},\beta_{\mathrm{T}},\beta_{\mathrm{M}})

\displaystyle\equiv(\alpha_{X}+\alpha_{Y},\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}+\beta_{\mathrm{L}},\alpha_{\mathrm{T}}+\beta_{\mathrm{T}},\alpha_{\mathrm{M}}+\beta_{\mathrm{M}})\,.

(32)

Note that the first relation holds only if all the
\alpha
’s are equal, or as the old saying goes
You cannot add eggplants to chocolate!
. More details can be found in the article (e.g. the discussion about this space being a vector space).

We want to describe how to go from one system to another. As before, we will consider the International System and the natural system, as describe above. A physical quantity can be written as

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=10^{\alpha_{\mathrm{si}}}\,\mathrm{m}^{\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}}\times\mathrm{s}^{\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}}\times\mathrm{kg}^{\alpha_{\mathrm{kg}}}\,,

(33)

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=10^{\alpha_{\mathrm{nat}}}\,\mathrm{eV}^{\alpha_{\mathrm{eV}}}\times c^{\alpha_{c}}\times\hbar^{\alpha_{\hbar}}\,.

(34)

The knowledge of the four quantities
(x_{\mathrm{si}},\alpha_{m},\alpha_{s},\alpha_{k}g)
or
(x_{\mathrm{nat}},\alpha_{\mathrm{eV}},\alpha_{c},\alpha_{\hbar})
completely the physical quantity
X
, which, itself, does not depend on the coordinate system. Using the results of the previous sections (especially Eq. (
9
), Eq. (
10
), and Eq. (
11
)), the transformation rule is given by

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{\mathrm{nat}}\\
\alpha_{\mathrm{eV}}\\
\alpha_{c}\\
\alpha_{\hbar}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\log_{10}\left(\frac{e_{\mathrm{si}}}{c_{\mathrm{si}}\hbar_{\mathrm{si}}}\right)&\log_{10}\left(\frac{e_{\mathrm{si}}}{\hbar_{\mathrm{si}}}\right)&\log_{10}\left(\frac{c_{\mathrm{si}}^{2}}{e_{\mathrm{si}}}\right)\\
0&-1&-1&1\\
0&1&0&-2\\
0&1&1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{\mathrm{si}}\\
\alpha_{\mathrm{m}}\\
\alpha_{\mathrm{s}}\\
\alpha_{\mathrm{kg}}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(35)

This shows that a change of unit system is nothing else as a usual linear coordinate transformation in the vector space of units!

4
Dimensional Analysis for Tensors

We end this Section by briefly discussing dimensional analysis for tensors. This discussion is based on the nice article by P.G.L. Mana
[
166
]
, which goes much deeper as what we are going to discuss now. In this Section, we do not set
c=\hbar=1
which we keep explicit.

To understand why the question of dimensional analysis, as usual, a simple example is illuminating. We consider the flat metric in the plane, in two coordinates systems

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}s^{2}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}y^{2}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+r^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{2}\,.

(36)

From this example, it is clear that the components of the metric such as
g_{xx}
or
g_{\theta\theta}
do not have the same dimension. Hence, and as usual when working with tensors, working with components is not a good idea. On the other side, it is clear that
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}{d}s^{2}
always has the dimension of a length squared, as it should be as it is the job of the metric tensor. This motivates the following definitions.

Definition 1.14
(
Dimensions of the coordinates
)
–

Let
x^{\mu}
be a coordinate system. We define
[x^{\mu}]
as the unit of the coordinate
x^{\mu}
.

For example, using the usual spherical coordinates
x^{\mu}=(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
, we have

[x^{\mu}]=(\mathrm{T},\mathrm{L},1,1)\,,

(37)

where
\mathrm{T}
and
\mathrm{L}
represent respectively a time and a length.

Definition 1.15
(
Intrinsic dimension of a tensor
)
–

Let
\boldsymbol{T}
be a generic tensor given in coordinates by

\boldsymbol{T}=\mathchoice{T^{{{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\mathchoice{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.95403pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.52086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[14.0909pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.35378pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{q}}}}}{T^{{{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\mathchoice{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.95403pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.52086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[14.0909pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.35378pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{q}}}}}{T^{{{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\mathchoice{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.95403pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.52086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[14.0909pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.35378pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{q}}}}}{T^{{{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\mathchoice{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.95403pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.52086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[14.0909pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.35378pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{q}}}}}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mu_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mu_{p}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{\nu_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{\nu_{q}}\,.

(38)

All the terms in this sum have the same dimension, called the
intrinsic dimension of the tensor
\boldsymbol{T}
and denoted
[\boldsymbol{T}]
. In particular, using

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mu}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{[x^{\mu}]}\,,

(39)

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{\nu}]

\displaystyle=[x^{\nu}]\,,

(40)

we have

[\boldsymbol{T}]=[\mathchoice{T^{{{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\mathchoice{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.95403pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.52086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[14.0909pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.35378pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{q}}}}}{T^{{{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\mathchoice{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.95403pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.52086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[14.0909pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.35378pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{q}}}}}{T^{{{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\mathchoice{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.95403pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.52086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[14.0909pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.35378pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{q}}}}}{T^{{{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\mathchoice{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.08177pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.95403pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.52086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[20.88995pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[14.0909pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[11.35378pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{q}}}}}]\,\frac{[x^{\nu_{1}}]\dots[x^{\nu_{q}}]}{[x^{\mu_{1}}]\dots[x^{\mu_{p}}]}\,.

(41)

From these definitions, it is straightforward to derive the intrinsic dimension of the various tensors presented in Chapter
1
.

We take the convention such that

[\boldsymbol{g}]=\mathrm{L}^{2}\,,

(42)

or in components

g_{\mu\nu}=\frac{\mathrm{L}^{2}}{[x^{\mu}][x^{\nu}]}\,.

(43)

Note that other choices are possible, see
[
166
]
for more details. From this, the units of the components of the inverse metric are

[g^{\mu\nu}]=\frac{[x^{\mu}][x^{\nu}]}{L^{2}}\,

(44)

which ensures that

[g^{\mu\alpha}][g_{\alpha\nu}]=1\,.

(45)

Using the definition of the Christoffel symbols and of the various tensors in Section
1
, we get

\displaystyle[\Gamma^{\mu}_{\nu\rho}]

\displaystyle=\frac{[x^{\mu}]}{[x^{\nu}][x^{\rho}]}\,,

(46)

\displaystyle[\mathchoice{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{[x^{\mu}]}{[x^{\nu}][x^{\rho}][x^{\sigma}]}\,,

(47)

\displaystyle[R_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{[x^{\mu}][x^{\nu}]}\,,

(48)

\displaystyle[R]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}^{2}\,.

(49)

These relations imply

[\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}]=[\boldsymbol{R}]=[\boldsymbol{G}]=1\,.

(50)

We want now to determine the dimension of the energy-momentum tensor. Again, there is some freedom in the exact definition is the speed of light is not set to
1
. Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be a
4
-velocity with intrinsic dimension

[\boldsymbol{u}]=\mathrm{T}\,.

(51)

We postulate that the quantity

[\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}]=\frac{\mathrm{M}}{\mathrm{L}\mathrm{T}^{2}}\,,

(52)

which is a spatial energy density. This implies

\displaystyle[T_{\mu\nu}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{M}}{\mathrm{L}}\frac{1}{[x^{\mu}][x^{\nu}]}\,,

(53)

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{T}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{M}}{\mathrm{L}}\,.

(54)

As mentioned, several conventions are possible, each of them with a different power of
c
in the final Einstein’s Equations. Recalling that
[\boldsymbol{G}]=1
and using the usual dimensions for the Newton’s constant, the full Einstein’s equations read

\boldsymbol{G}=\frac{8\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}{c^{2}}\boldsymbol{T}\,.

(55)

We could also make this analysis from the definition of the energy-momentum tensor Eq. (
37
), but we would need in this case to divide by an extra factor of
c
. Finally, note that the Einstein-Hilbert Lagrangian reads, with our convention,

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EH}}=\frac{c^{3}R}{16\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}\,.

(56)

2
Observables

1
General considerations

We discussed in the previous Section the important concept of units and presented two different systems used in Physics. A natural question arises: Which system is
the best
? This somehow implies that there is one choice of units which is better than any other. The answer is that such a system does not exist: Any problem can be solved in any unit system. You
could
study General Relativity using miles, quarantines and ounces as basic units of length, time and masses and you
could
use the Planck system in your daily life.

The only thing that matters, in the end, is that physical observable should not depend on your choice. What is a physical observable is a more subtle issue. We will come back to this below. The important point is that there is no good choice for the units. Some of them may make computations more convenient, as it was the case in the Section
2
, where the expression for the Schwarzschild radius is simpler in the natural system. However, the physical observable, in this case
R
, does
not
depend on this choice.

In the next Section, we will show that the choice of units is not the only choice on which physical observables should not depend, and we will give a (non-exhaustive) list of transformations under which physical observables should not change.

2
Examples

We know already that in Electrodynamics and Quantum Mechanics there is the freedom to choose the gauge or the phase of the wave function. Final results, e.g. the force felt by a charged particle in an electromagnetic field or the probability to observe a particle, should not depend on this choice. We present in this Section other of these examples which will be relevant in the present work.

Choice of units:

As explained in the previous Sections, the description of physical systems should not, in fine , depend on the system of units. In practise, final answers do contain units, but an equality as
L=1.5\,\mathrm{m}
is indeed unit-independent, as both the number
1.5
and the unit (meter here) both change under a unit transformation. We will study in more details in Part
2
how this invariance can actually be even more general to include
spacetime dependent
unit transformations, and we will apply this to the Galaxy Number Counts, which is expected to be a genuine physical observable.

Choice of coordinates:

Undergraduate students are allowed to solve problems in the coordinate system of their choice, but advised to work in the most convenient one. This reflects the fact that physical observables should not depend on this choice. We will discuss this in Part
3
, where we will define several observables which are independent on the choice on coordinates (in this example spherical coordinates on the celestial sphere). We will encounter a more interesting example in Part
4
where we will see that even if the Master Equation describing waves around Black Holes is not unique and depend on a choice of coordinates, physical quantities as the speed of the gravitational waves or the frequencies of such waves do not depend on this choice.

Rescaling in Cosmology:

In Chapter
3
we will see that in Cosmology, the scale factor, the radial coordinate and the curvature are not uniquely defined, and hence are not physical observables. We will still be able to build physical observables from these quantities, such as the Hubble factor.

Choice of gauge:

Also in Chapter
3
, we will discuss the perturbations in Cosmology and around a
SSSS
(
SSSS
) and see that there is again some freedom to parametrise these quantities, on which final observables should not depend. Note that this gauge choices are actually related to a freedom in the choice of coordinates.

3
Definition

We are now able to define physical observables in physics (or more generally in science).

Definition 1.16
(
Physical Observables
)
–

We consider a set of physical quantities
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}=\{\mathcal{Q}_{i}\}
defined on a manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
. We define a set of transformations
\{\mathcal{S}\}
from
{\mathcal{M}}
to itself. Let
\mathcal{O}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}})
be a quantity built from the physical quantities. This quantity is a physical observable if

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}})=\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}))

(57)

for every
\mathcal{T}\in\mathcal{S}
. In other words, the value of a physical observable should be invariant under all the transformations.

There is one caveat with this definition: It is (purposely) very vague. Defining precisely what is a physical quantity, or making an exhaustive list of all the transformations in the set
\mathcal{S}
is a difficult task that I am not pretending to perform. Moreover, there is a slight loophole: We can, first, impose which quantities should be physical observables, and then only keep the transformations
\mathcal{T}
which leaves the observables invariant, or vice-versa (first choosing the transformations and the defining the observables). We do not have a fully satisfactory answer for now, and we will just keep this ideas in mind in this work. Note also that, these details being said, we will not mention them all the time. We will also sometimes work with quantities that are, stricto sensu, not physical observables, but the context should be always clear to avoid any ambiguity.

Chapter 3
The Standard Cosmological Model

In this Chapter, we present in the first Section the Standard Cosmological Model. In Cosmology, the Universe is assumed to be homogeneous and isotropic on large scales. Moreover, we also know from observations that the Universe is expanding. We present several important concepts, particularly the scale factor, the Hubble constant and the redshift, and we briefly discuss the Cosmic Microwave Background. For a more thorough introduction to Cosmology, see the book by Jean-Philippe Uzan et Patrick Peter
[
164
]
. Obviously, much more references can be found for an introduction to Cosmology.

In the second Section, we discuss the theory of perturbations applied to Cosmology. The model of a homogeneous and isotropic Universe is not realistic: The Universe contains inhomogeneities, for example the galaxies, and this should be taken into account. For example, I found the Lecture Notes of Hannu Kurki-Suonio
[
130
]
and the review of Malik
[
144
]
very useful to learn the basis of perturbation theory.

The last Section of the Chapter is devoted to lensing, the general theory of light deflection as it travels through the Universe. We discuss the lens map and the Jacobi formalism which are the mathematical tools to describe quantitatively lensing of bundles of light rays. We briefly present the Mathematics of the Spherical Harmonics, and their generalisation to Spin Weighted Spherical Harmonics and discuss the statistics of lensing. The main reference for this part is the book about the
CMB
written by Ruth Durrer
[
66
]
.

Menu of the Chapter

1
The FRLW Universe

1
The FRLW Metric

We know that General Relativity describes gravitational phenomena extremely well. Examples of such include trajectories of planets around stars, behaviour of galaxies and even Black Holes. So let us be bold and ask the following question: Why do not we apply the machinery of General Relativity to describe the behaviour of the entire Universe? This is the main task of Cosmology!

We want to find, in fine, the equations governing the evolution of the Universe at large scales. To do so, we need to make some assumptions about the general form of the metric.

First, we require the Universe to be static, which means that the metric cannot depend on time and that the time-space elements
g_{0i}
vanish. Second, we want the Universe to be, at any instant in time, homogeneous and isotropic. This assumptions is the cosmological principle. Based on this assumptions, we can build the
FRLW
metric.

Definition 1.17
(
FRLW metric
)
–

The
FRLW
metric is defined, using spherical coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
, as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}=-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}+a(t)^{2}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}}{1-kr^{2}}+r^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\theta^{2}+r^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\varphi^{2}\right)\,,

(1)

where
a(t)
is the scale factor depending on time only,
k
the spatial curvature of the constant-time hypersurfaces, whose sign can be either positive, negative or zero.

Remark 1.8
(
Normalisation of time
)
–

A prefactor in front of the time-time part of the metric would be possible. It would then read

\boldsymbol{g}=-N(\tilde{t})^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}{\tilde{t}}^{2}+\dots\,.

(2)

However, this can be removed defining the cosmic time
t
via (provided
N(\tilde{t})>0
)

N(\tilde{t})\mathrm{d}\tilde{t}=\mathrm{d}t\,.

(3)

Another more important comment: There is some freedom in the definitions of the radial coordinate. Indeed, requiring the line-element to be invariant, we define

\displaystyle\tilde{a}

\displaystyle=\lambda a\,,

(4)

\displaystyle\tilde{r}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}r\,,

(5)

\displaystyle\tilde{k}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}k\,.

(6)

It can easily be checked that the line-element is invariant is we use the tildes quantities. This freedom in the definition of the quantities tells us that
r
,
a
and
k
are not observable per se. One should consider invariant quantities such as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{1}

\displaystyle=ar\,,

(7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{2}

\displaystyle=ka^{-2\,.}

(8)

The first quantity
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
is a physical distance and has the units of a length, while
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
is a physical measure of the spatial curvature and has the units of a inverse length squared, as it should be.

Remark 1.9
(
Spatial curvature
)
–

In this thesis, we will only consider flat spatial surfaces with
k=0
.

Remark 1.10
(
Conformal time
)
–

Sometimes, it is more convenient to work in conformal time
\eta
defined implicitly as
\mathrm{d}t=a(t)\mathrm{d}\eta
. Using this time coordinate, the
FRLW
metric reads

\boldsymbol{g}=a(\eta)^{2}(-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\eta^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+r^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\Omega^{2})\,,

(9)

where we set
k=0
. Moreover, under the transformation Eq. (
4
), the conformal time becomes

\mathrm{d}\tilde{\eta}=\lambda^{-1}\mathrm{d}\eta\,,

(10)

which implies that any product of the form
\mathcal{Q}_{3}=a\Delta\eta
is invariant.

Remark 1.11
(
Today
)
–

By convention, quantities today are denoted with the index
0
, e.g.
t_{0}
or
a_{0}
. It is common to set
a_{0}=1
in the literature, but we will never do this in this thesis.

2
The Hubble factor

In this Section, we want to define physical quantities relevant in Cosmology. The first choice would be the scale factor
a(t)
. However, as discussed above, it is not a physical observable per se. Rather, we should consider for a physical quantity a typical physical distance
D_{\mathrm{p}}(t)=a(t)R
, where
R
is a fixed radial coordinates.

The second quantity one would like to measure is the speed of expansion of the Universe. A naive answer would be to consider simply
v_{\mathrm{exp}}=\dot{a}
. This is however a very bad choice… Why? The reason is again that this is not an observable per se: The value of the scale factor
a(t)
can be rescaled arbitrarily by a positive constant
\lambda
, as explained in the previous section. A better definition of the expansion rate of the Universe is the Hubble factor defined as

\displaystyle H(t)=\frac{\dot{a}(t)}{a(t)}\,.

(11)

Indeed, if one defines
\tilde{a}=\lambda a
(while keeping the physical time
t
unchanged), the Hubble factor is not modified. Hence, this definition is a good candidate to quantify the expansion rate of the Universe.

Remark 1.12
(
Hubble factor in conformal time
)
–

Using the conformal time defined in Rem.
1.10
, we define the Hubble factor in conformal time as

\mathcal{H}=\frac{1}{a}\frac{\mathrm{d}a}{\mathrm{d}\eta}=aH\,.

(12)

Note that the Hubble factor is not constant! Its value today is the Hubble constant
H_{0}
given by

H_{0}=100h\,\frac{\mathrm{km}}{\mathrm{s}}\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}\,,

(13)

with
h\approx 0.6/0.7
, see Section
4
for more details about the time evolution of the Hubble factor. The physical interpretation of the Hubble factor goes as follows. Consider two comoving friends, one located at coordinate
r=0
and one located at constant coordinate
r\neq 0
(and at a fixed values of the angles
(\theta,\varphi)
which is not relevant here). The physical distance
D_{\mathrm{p}}
between these two friends can be computed using the
FRLW
metric given by Eq. (
1
) and is

D_{\mathrm{p}}(t)=a(t)r\,.

(14)

Note that this distance depends on time precisely because of the expansion of the Universe! The recession speed of the second friend (with respect to the first one) is the time derivative of this quantity given by

\dot{D}_{\mathrm{p}}=\dot{a}r=H(t)ar=H(t)D_{\mathrm{p}}\,.

(15)

This is the
Hubble’s law
! It gives an interpretation of the Hubble constant: It quantifies the relative recession speed of any distance object in the Universe! This also explains its interesting units: An object (say a galaxy) located at
1\,\mathrm{Mpc}
away from us will recede with a speed of order
100\,\mathrm{km\times s^{-1}}
.

Let us finish this section by briefly discuss the Hubble tension. Today, we have two different experimental ways to measure the Hubble constant.

The first method is the
close method
: Roughly speaking, it relies on the principle described by the Hubble’s law Eq. (
15
). The general idea is to observe several galaxies, and measure together their receding speed and their distance. Fitting those two quantities together can provide an estimation of the Hubble constant.

The second method is the
far method
: Without going into the details, the actual value of the Hubble constant can be deduced from the observed power spectrum of the
CMB
.

The story could end now if these two methods did not give different values (taking into account the error bars). This difference is called the Hubble tension and we do not have any satisfactory answer yet, even if several hypothesis have been done, see for example
[
56
]
.

The two estimable videos (in French)
[
179
,
180
]
of the Channel
ScienceEtonnante
present an excellent description of the problem, even for Physicists!

3
Redshift in Cosmology

In this Section, we introduce another major concept in Cosmology (and in general in Physics): The redshift. Let us give first a qualitative explanation. When a photon travels in the Universe, it is somehow
attached
to the expanding space, which makes it expand too, or more precisely, which increases its wavelength. We know from Electrodynamics and Quantum Mechanics that the energy of such a photon will decrease. Hence, in the Universe, travelling photons lose energy. If those photons would be in the visible range, they would then start
blue-ish
with a high energy, and then become
red-ish
, with less energy. This is the origin of the term
redshift
.

We want to show this in a more formal way. We consider a radially travelling photon whose 4-velocity is given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}

\displaystyle=k^{\mu}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\mu}\,,

(16)

\displaystyle k^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\omega(t)(1,a(t)^{-1},0,0)\,.

(17)

The vector is chosen such that
\boldsymbol{k}^{2}=0
, but the prefactor outside can in principle depend on time. Photon, as any particle, must follow geodesics, hence the 4-vector
\boldsymbol{k}
must satisfy the parallel transport condition given by Eq. (
30
) (with
\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{b}=\boldsymbol{k}
). Using the
FRLW
metric and the formalism presented in Section
1
, we find that the only condition is

(a(t)\omega(t))^{\bullet}=0\,,

(18)

or in other words

\omega(t)=\omega_{0}\frac{a_{0}}{a(t)}\,,

(19)

where
\omega_{0}
and
a_{0}
are some initial values.

We need now to explain the interpretation of
\omega(t)
. In order to do so, we introduce a stationary observer, for example on Earth. Her 4-velocity is given by

\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}\,,

(20)

which satisfies
\boldsymbol{u}^{2}=-1
. She observes the photon arriving at her with 4-velocity
\boldsymbol{k}
. The frequency she receives is given by

\omega_{\mathrm{r}}\equiv-\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=\omega(t)\,.

(21)

This definition is motivated by the fact that it is a coordinate invariant quantity and it leads to the correct answer in Special Relativity, e.g. by taking into account the Doppler effect.

Then, if she knows the physical process from which the photon was emitted, she can reproduce this experiment in her lab and will measure the emitted frequency
\omega_{\mathrm{e}}
, that she will compare with the observed frequency
\omega_{\mathrm{r}}
. This is done by defining the redshift

\displaystyle 1+z\equiv\frac{\omega_{\mathrm{e}}}{\omega_{\mathrm{r}}}=\frac{a(t_{\mathrm{r}})}{a(t_{\mathrm{e}})}\,,

(22)

where the subscripts means
emission
and
reception
. This relation is fundamental in Cosmology: Assuming that
a(t_{\mathrm{r}})=a_{0}
is the scale factor today (and hence is constant), this relation translates the scale factor at emission into a redshift. Hence, the redshift can be used as a
clock
for the history of the Universe (assuming that the scale factor is an increasing function of
t
). One should note that this clock goes backward: at the Big-Bang, we had
a(t_{\mathrm{e}})=0
and
z=\infty
, and today we have
a(t_{\mathrm{e}})=a_{0}
and
z=0
.

4
The Friedmann Equations

In this Section, we derive the Friedmann equations. These equations describe the dynamics of the
FRLW
Universe. They are obtained through the Enstein’s Equations Eq. (
34
). The left hand side of the equations, the Einstein tensor, can be computed using the machinery of Differential Geometry and the
FRLW
metric. However, we have not defined yetthe right hand side. We explain here how to define a reasonable energy-momentum tensor in Cosmology.

We assume that the Universe is filled with several different fluids, with total energy density
\rho
and pressure
P
. These quantities, because of the homogeneity assumption, can only depend on time. Moreover, we assume that the fluid has a constant 4-velocity
\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}
, which is the only possible choice because of the isotropy assumption. From this 4-velocity, we can build the spatial metric

\boldsymbol{h}=\boldsymbol{g}+\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}\,,

(23)

satisfying
\boldsymbol{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=0
(as
\boldsymbol{u}^{2}=-1
). With these considerations, we can introduce the energy-momentum tensor.

Definition 1.18
(
Energy-momentum tensor
)
–

The energy-momentum tensor for a perfect fluid is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{T}

\displaystyle=P\boldsymbol{h}+\rho\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}\,,

(24)

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=P(g_{\mu\nu}+u_{\mu}u_{\nu})+\rho u_{\mu}u_{\nu}\,,

(25)

where
\rho
is its energy density,
P
its pressure and
\boldsymbol{u}
its
4
-velocity.

This definition can be justified recalling that the energy density
\rho
is defined to be (minus) the eigenvalue of the energy-momentum tensor associated with the timelike eigenvector
\boldsymbol{u}
, i.e.
\rho=-u^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}u^{\nu}
. This definition fixes the second term. The first term can be interpreted as the definition of pressure, as being the spatial part of the energy-momentum tensor.

In Definition
1.18
, we assumed that the fluid has only one component. We could consider several fluids, and the total energy-momentum tensor would read

\boldsymbol{T}_{\mathrm{tot.}}=\sum_{i}\boldsymbol{T}_{i}\,,

(26)

where the fluid
i
has energy density
\rho_{i}
and pressure
P_{i}
. Moreover, we will assume that the pressure is proportional to the energy density

P_{i}=w_{i}\rho_{i}\,,

(27)

where
w_{i}
is the barotropic index of the fluid. The three most common examples are
w_{\mathrm{m}}=0
for cold matter,
w_{\mathrm{r}}=1/3
for radiation and
w_{\Lambda}=-1
for a cosmological constant.

We have now all the ingredients to derive the Friedmann equations. Using the energy-momentum tensor given by Eq. (
24
), and computing the Einstein tensor from the
FRLW
metric given by Eq. (
1
), we get two independent Friedmann equations : One corresponding to
G_{00}
and one to the spatial trace
\mathchoice{G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{G^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}

\displaystyle H^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{8\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}{3}\rho-\frac{k}{a^{2}}\,,

(28)

\displaystyle\frac{\ddot{a}}{a}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}{3}(\rho+3P)\,.

(29)

From these two equations, or equivalently using the conservation equation given by Eq. (
41
), we get

\dot{\rho}+3H(\rho+P)=0\,.

(30)

If we assume that the fluids are not interacting with each other, this relation holds for each of them independently

\dot{\rho}_{i}+3H(\rho_{i}+P_{i})=0\,.

(31)

Moreover, if we set
P_{i}=w_{i}\rho_{i}
, and using Eq. (
31
), we can relate the energy density
\rho_{i}(t)
with the scale factor
a(t)as

\rho_{i}(t)=\rho_{i0}\left(\frac{a(t)}{a_{0}}\right)^{-3(1+w_{i})}\,,

(32)

where
\rho_{i0}
and
a_{0}
are arbitrary initial conditions. We can then rewrite the first Friedmann Equation Eq. (
28
), getting

\displaystyle H^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{8\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}{3}\rho(t)

(33)

\displaystyle=\frac{8\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}{3}\sum_{i}\rho_{i}(t)

(34)

\displaystyle=\frac{8\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}{3}\rho_{0}\sum_{i}\frac{\rho_{i}(t)}{\rho_{0}}

(35)

\displaystyle=H_{0}^{2}\sum_{i}\frac{\rho_{i0}}{\rho_{0}}\left(\frac{a(t)}{a_{0}}\right)^{-3(1+w_{i})}\,,

(36)

where we used the first Friedmann Equation applied today (represented by the index
0
) and the solution for each species given by Eq. (
32
). We introduce the cosmological parameters today as

\Omega_{i}\equiv\frac{\rho_{i0}}{\rho_{0}}\,,

(37)

satisfying
\sum_{i}\Omega_{i}=1
. The Friedmann equation reads then

\displaystyle\frac{H^{2}}{H_{0}^{2}}=\sum_{i}\Omega_{i}\left(\frac{a(t)}{a_{0}}\right)^{-3(1+w_{i})}\,.

(38)

Using the definition of the redshift given by Eq. (
22
), we can also write this relation as

\frac{H^{2}}{H_{0}^{2}}=\sum_{i}\Omega_{i}(1+z)^{3(1+w_{i})}\,.

(39)

Defining the function

E(z)=\sqrt{\sum_{i}\Omega_{i}(1+z)^{3(1+w_{i})}}\,,

(40)

we get

\displaystyle H(z)=H_{0}E(z)\,,

(41)

which relates the Hubble factor at arbitrary redshift
z
with the Hubble constant today.

5
Timeline of the Universe

In this Section, we want to present the
biography
of the Universe. In other words, we would like to describe several events, together with the time at which they happened. We assume that the Universe was born during the Big-Bang at
t=0
, and we denote the present time
t_{0}
. We consider an event which happened at time
t
such that
0\leq t<t_{0}
. The time separating us from this event can be computed as

\displaystyle t_{0}-t

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\;\mathrm{d}t

(42)

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{a_{0}}\;\frac{1}{\dot{a}}\,\mathrm{d}a

(43)

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{a_{0}}\;\frac{1}{Ha}\,\mathrm{d}a

(44)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{z}\;\frac{1}{H(\tilde{z})(1+\tilde{z})}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}

(45)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{H_{0}}\int_{0}^{z}\;\frac{1}{E(\tilde{z})(1+\tilde{z})}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}\,,

(46)

where we used the relation between the redshift and the scale factor given by Eq. (
22
) and the expressed Hubble factor as function of the redshift with Eq. (
41
). We can integrate this relation numerically to obtain pairs
(t,z)
linking between time and redshift. In particular, setting
z=\infty
, we can compute the age of the Universe. Using the Cosmological
LCDM
parameters (Planck
[
5
]
)

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\approx 0.7\,,

(47)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathrm{m}}

\displaystyle\approx 0.31\,,

(48)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\approx 0.69\,,

(49)

and neglecting radiation, we find that age of the Universe is

t_{\mathrm{U}}\approx 13.8\,\mathrm{Gyrs}\,.

(50)

In Fig.
1
, we show this relationship between time and redshift, where the time is computed since the Big-Bang until the considered event. For example,
Homo Sapiens
appeared roughly
500^{\prime}000
years ago. A photon emitted by the first Human would be seen today (by an alien) with a redshift of
z\approx 0.003
, and a photon emitted by a galaxy
6\text{\,}\mathrm{Gyrs}
after the Big-Bang would be seen today with a redshift of
z\approx 1
.

Figure 1:

Timeline of the Universe

Relationship between the redshift of an event and the
age
of the Universe when this event happened. We can read off, for example, the age of the Universe by looking at
z=0
.

Event

z

Anyone studying Cosmology should always remember the typical time scales, which are in general much longer that any time scale a human can think of. To give you a little anecdote, when I was taking my first Cosmology class, the professor said that dark energy started to dominate
recently
. I thought that the first Humans witnessed this transition! As one can see in Tab.
1
, I was quite far-off, as humans appeared almost
3
billion years after the transition…

6
Distances

In Cosmology, because of the large scales involved, the concept of distance is not well-defined. We present here various definitions of distance with different interpretations. Let us consider a photon received on Earth with redshift
z
. Its time of emission is
t
, where the explicit relation with the redshift is given by Eq. (
46
). We assume that the observer is located at
r=0
.

1.

First, let us compute the comoving distance
r
of the source. As the photons travels along null geodesic, using the
FRLW
metric yields

\mathrm{d}r=-\frac{1}{a}\mathrm{d}t\,,

(51)

where the negative sign comes from the fact that the photon travels inwards. Using the same steps as before, we get

\displaystyle r=\frac{1}{a_{0}H_{0}}\int_{0}^{z}\;\frac{1}{E(\tilde{z})}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}\,.

(52)

Note that this distance does
not
depend on time: The source is located at fixed
r
and does not move. Moreover, using the definition of the conformal time, it is clear that
\mathrm{d}r=-\mathrm{d}\eta
, which implies

\eta_{0}-\eta=r\,,

(53)

where
\eta_{0}
(resp.
\eta
) is the conformal time today (resp. at emission).

Definition 1.19
(
Conformal distance between two sources
)
–

We can generalise this to show that the conformal distance between two sources observed at redshift
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
(assuming the sources are aligned and
z_{1}<z_{2}
) as

\chi(z_{1},z_{2})\equiv\frac{1}{a_{0}H_{0}}\int_{z_{1}}^{z_{2}}\;\frac{1}{E(\tilde{z})}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}\,.

(54)

2.

We can also compute the distance today
D_{0}
. This distance would correspond to an
instantaneous
measurement, which is indeed not possible. However, this distance can be mathematically computed. The scale factor being
a_{0}
today and the comoving distance being
r
given above, the distance today is

\displaystyle D_{0}=a_{0}r=\frac{1}{H_{0}}\int_{0}^{z}\;\frac{1}{E(\tilde{z})}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}\,.

(55)

3.

We can perform the same computation, but at the time of emission to get the instantaneous distance when the photon was emitted
D_{{\mathrm{e}}}
. As the conformal distance is constant, and using the definition of the redshift, we get

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{e}}=a_{\mathrm{e}}r=\frac{a_{0}}{1+z}r=\frac{1}{(1+z)H_{0}}\int_{0}^{z}\;\frac{1}{E(\tilde{z})}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}\,.

(56)

4.

The penultimate distance we consider is the angular diameter distance
D_{\mathrm{A}}
. If we observe an object under a solid angle
\Delta\Omega
, and we know its physical area
A_{\mathrm{p}}
, we define the angular diameter distance
D_{\mathrm{A}}
through the usual trigonometric relation

A_{\mathrm{p}}=\Delta\Omega D_{\mathrm{A}}^{2}\,.

(57)

The solid angle is not modified when the photon travels (
\theta
and
\varphi
are constant). Hence, at the emission time and position, the physical area of the object was given by

A_{\mathrm{p}}=a^{2}r^{2}\Delta\Omega\,,

(58)

from which we infer

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{A}}=ar=\frac{a_{0}}{1+z}r=\frac{1}{(1+z)H_{0}}\int_{0}^{z}\;\frac{1}{E(\tilde{z})}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}\,,

(59)

which is actually equal to
D_{\mathrm{e}}
.

5.

Finally, we define the luminosity distance. If an object with intrinsic luminosity
L_{\mathrm{int.}}
emits light, and a flux of energy
F
is received on Earth, we define the luminosity distance
D_{\mathrm{L}}
via
1
1

1

We consider here that we receive the full flux, i.e. from all around the source. To be more realistic, we would need to consider the flux received in a small solid angle
\Delta\Omega
, but the final result would not be different.

F=\frac{L_{\mathrm{int.}}}{4\pi D_{\mathrm{L}}^{2}}\,.

(60)

The light of the source has been redshifted, so the energy we observe is

E_{{\mathrm{o}}}=\frac{E_{\mathrm{int.}}}{1+z}\,,

(61)

where
E_{\mathrm{int.}}
is the intrinsic energy emitted by the source. Moreover, there is a time dilatation. Indeed, imagine that the source emits light during a small interval of time
\Delta t_{\mathrm{e}}
. During this time, the light travels a conformal distance
\Delta r
given by
\Delta t_{\mathrm{e}}=a\Delta r
, where
a
is the scale factor as emission. The conformal distance
\Delta r
is constant during the light’s journey. As the scale factor expands, the time it takes for this light ray to pass increases. More precisely, the time at the observer position is given by

\frac{\Delta t_{\mathrm{o}}}{a_{0}}=\frac{\Delta t_{\mathrm{e}}}{a}\,,

(62)

or equivalently

\Delta t_{\mathrm{o}}=(1+z)\Delta t_{\mathrm{e}}\,.

(63)

The observed luminosity is then given by

L_{\mathrm{e}}=\frac{E_{{\mathrm{o}}}}{\Delta t_{\mathrm{o}}}=\frac{1}{(1+z)^{2}}L_{\mathrm{int.}}\,.

(64)

This energy is spread on a sphere of radius
a_{0}r
, hence the observed flux is

F_{\mathrm{e}}=\frac{L_{\mathrm{e}}}{4\pi a_{0}^{2}r^{2}}=\frac{1}{(1+z)^{2}}\frac{L_{\mathrm{int.}}}{4\pi a_{0}^{2}r^{2}}\,.

(65)

From this, we infer that the luminosity distance is given by

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{L}}=(1+z)a_{0}r=\frac{1+z}{H_{0}}\int_{0}^{z}\;\frac{1}{E(\tilde{z})}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}

(66)

We defined
5
different distances. In practice, only two are observables: The angular diameter distance
D_{\mathrm{A}}
and the luminosity distance
D_{\mathrm{L}}
. The first former is an observable is the angular size of the object is known, we call such objects
standard rulers
. The latter is an observable is the intrinsic luminosity of the object is known, which we call
standard candels
.

Note that from these definitions Eq. (
59
) and Eq. (
66
), we get the relation

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{L}}=(1+z)^{2}D_{\mathrm{A}}\,.

(67)

This relation is very interesting as it only rely on a few assumptions. We assumed that photons follow null geodesics and travel across a
FRLW
Universe. Moreover, in the derivation of the luminosity distance, we implicitly assumed that the number of photons is conserved during the process.

7
The Cosmic Microwave Background

Right after the Big Bang, the Universe was a mere hot soup of elementary particles. Within the first half hour, the quarks combined to form protons and neutrons, which themselves merged to form deuterium and helium nuclei. However, at that time, the photons travelling in the Universe were energetic enough to break any neutral atom that would have dared to form. Another consequence is that light in this primordial Universe could not travel freely. The Universe is said to be opaque, with a thick
mist
penetrating it.

This situation lasted for quite a long time. The Universe had to wait no more than
380^{\prime}000
years to form neutral hydrogen atoms. By that time, because of the expansion of the Universe, a majority of the photons were not hot enough anymore and could not
break
neutral atoms. Hence, light was not constantly stopped and could travel freely in the Universe. These light is the origin of the Cosmic Microwave Background which we still observe today!

Figure 2:

Formation of the CMB

Left:
Before decoupling, electrons and protons are not bounded. The free photons scatter against them and cannot travel freely.

Right:
After decoupling, neutral atom are formed. The photos do not scatter and can travel freely across the Universe.

The
CMB
is a relic of this primordial light which was in thermal equilibrium with the other particles. Hence, the energy distribution of these photons should follow a perfect black body law, whose temperature would be
T_{0}=2.7\,\mathrm{K}
today. This is exactly what we observe! The observed
CMB
is then a very good evidence for the Big Bang model used in Cosmology.

2
Perturbation Theory

1
General Idea

In this Section, we present perturbation theory. This is a mathematical and physical topic in its own right, and one could definitely write a full thesis about it. I do not have the pretension to be thorough and complete here. Our goal is to present the idea from a general point of view, and then describe the relevant cases for the present work: Cosmological perturbations and perturbations around
SSSS
. The basic idea of perturbation theory can be summarized as follows:

Small problems require small solutions.

This is a general advice to follow in life. For example, if your noodles are slightly hot, and if you slightly blow on them, I’m quite sure you can eat them. Sometimes, however, small problems require big solutions: If you have a small spiky rock in your shoes, you should not slightly shake your shoes to solve the issue, rather you should stop walking and take care of it.

The Mathematical idea is similar: Assume you have a problem, say an equation (the heat in the example), that is very close from a problem whose solution is known. You can fairly expect the solution you are seeking to be very close from the known solution. The small difference between the two (the slight blow in the example) can be found using perturbation theory.

However, there are problems that seem to be very close from each other, but whose solutions are far apart, as in the example of the rock. For such examples, perturbation theory does not work and more advanced methods should be used.

Perturbation theory is actually very useful in Physics: In general, we always consider situations that are close to ideal models, from which the solutions can be easily computed. Perturbation theory helps us to compute approximation of the solutions. In Cosmology, for example, we assume that the Universe is isotropic and homogeneous. This is an idealized situation from which several things can be computed. The reality is a bit different: The Universe is not fully homogeneous, it contains some small
ripples
here and there, e.g. galaxies or dark matter halos. Cosmological Perturbation Theory is extremely useful to describe those so-called inhomogeneities and extract very interesting predictions therefrom.

2
Lie Derivative

Motivation

There is one caveat one should be careful about when using perturbation theory in General Relativity. We start with a simple example to exhibit the problem and generalize it to General Relativity in the next section.

Suppose we study a
2D
scalar function
f(t,x)
. We will assume a Cosmological background, where the
0
th
-order only depends on
t
, and consider a
1
st
-order generic perturbation, i.e.

f(t,x)=\bar{f}(t)+\varepsilon\,\delta f(t,x)\,,

(68)

where
\varepsilon
is a parameter representing the perturbation and considered to be small. We will work only at first order in
\varepsilon
.

Suppose we perform a change of coordinates given by

\left\{\begin{matrix}\tilde{t}&=&t-\varepsilon\,x\\
\tilde{x}&=&x\,.\end{matrix}\right.

(69)

This transformation is also called a
gauge transformation
. The function
f
being a scalar, its transformation rule is given by

\tilde{f}\left(\tilde{t},\tilde{x}\right)=f\left(t,x\right)\,,

(70)

where it is implied that
t
and
x
depend on
\tilde{t}
and
\tilde{x}
. This yields

\tilde{f}(\tilde{t},\tilde{x})=f\left(\tilde{t}+\varepsilon\,\tilde{x},\tilde{x}\right)=\bar{f}\left(\tilde{t}\right)+\varepsilon\left[\delta f(\tilde{t},\tilde{x})+x\dot{\bar{f}}(\tilde{t})\right]\,.

(71)

This means that if we use the
(\tilde{t},\tilde{x})
system of coordinates, we could think that the new perturbation is

\delta\tilde{f}(\tilde{t},\tilde{x})=\delta f(\tilde{t},\tilde{x})+x\dot{\bar{f}}(\tilde{t})\,.

(72)

However, the new term only comes from the fact that we perform a slight coordinate transformations, and does not have any physical meaning. Hence, we need a way to determine the true physical perturbation. Note also that this problem only arises at the perturbed level: We want to keep the Cosmological background
\bar{f}
in all coordinate systems, which prevents us from doing a change of coordinate at this order.

Scalar Field

We now present the relevant mathematical tool to formalise this issue: The Lie Derivative. It describes quantitatively the transformation of perturbations under change of coordinates. We will explicitly explain how to define it for scalar functions, vectors and forms, and will generalize it to tensors afterwards. In this Section, we consider generic coordinates
x^{\mu}
and
\tilde{x}^{\mu}
related by (at first order)

\displaystyle\tilde{x}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=x^{\mu}+\varepsilon\,\xi^{\mu}(x)\,,

(73)

\displaystyle x^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\tilde{x}^{\mu}-\varepsilon\,\xi^{\mu}(\tilde{x})\,.

(74)

Note that here the function
\boldsymbol{\xi}({x})
is the same for both transformations (up to its sign), as we work only at first order in
\varepsilon
. Under this assumption, it is straightforward to show that the transformation rules Eq. (
73
) and Eq. (
74
) are inverse of each other.

We consider a scalar function
F(x)
separated into a background part and a perturbation as

F(x)=\bar{F}(x)+\varepsilon\,\delta F(x)\,,

(75)

where we do not make any assumption about the background function, e.g. we do not impose a Cosmological setup. The transformation rule for scalar functions is given by

\tilde{F}(\tilde{x})=F(x)\,,

(76)

where it is implied here that
x^{\mu}
is given in terms of
\tilde{x}^{\mu}
through Eq. (
74
) and vice-versa. This yields

\displaystyle\tilde{F}(\tilde{x})

\displaystyle=F(x-\varepsilon\,\boldsymbol{\xi})

(77)

\displaystyle=\bar{F}(\tilde{x})+\varepsilon\left[\delta F(\tilde{x})-\xi^{\mu}(\tilde{x})\partial_{\mu}\bar{F}(\tilde{x})\right]\,,

(78)

where in the last term we replaced
F
by
\bar{F}
as this quantities are equal at first order. We would like now to define a way to compare the functions
F
and
\tilde{F}
. There are two ways to do this. The first one would be to compute this difference at the same physical point, i.e. between
F(x)
and
\tilde{F}(\tilde{x})
. However, as the transformation rule for a scalar function given by Eq. (
76
), we know that this difference would be
0
, and hence would not be very interesting. What we could do instead is choose a given coordinate
\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}
and compute the difference between
F(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}})
and
\tilde{F}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}})
, which can be done directly through Eq. (
75
) and Eq. (
77
)

\tilde{F}(\tilde{x})-F(\tilde{x})=-\varepsilon\xi^{\mu}(\tilde{x})\partial_{\mu}\bar{F}(\tilde{x})\,.

(79)

Moreover, we define the perturbations in the new system as

\tilde{F}(\tilde{x})=\bar{F}(\tilde{x})+\varepsilon\,\delta\tilde{F}(\tilde{x})

(80)

In other words, we assume that the background function is the same in both coordinate systems. From the definition of the scalar function Eq. (
75
) and Eq. (
80
), it is clear that

\tilde{F}(\tilde{x})-F(\tilde{x})=\delta\tilde{F}(\tilde{x})-\delta F(\tilde{x})\,.

(81)

Combining now Eq. (
77
) and Eq. (
81
) gives

\delta\tilde{F}(\tilde{x})-\delta F(\tilde{x})=-\xi^{\mu}(\tilde{x})\partial_{\mu}\bar{F}(\tilde{x})\,.

(82)

From this, we can define the Lie Derivative of a scalar field.

Definition 1.20
(
Lie Derivative - scalar field
)
–

The Lie derivative of a scalar field
{F}(x)
under the change of coordinates given by Eq. (
73
) is defined as

\displaystyle\pounds_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}{F}=-\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\partial}F\,,

(83)

or in coordinates

\displaystyle\pounds_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}{F}=-\xi^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}{F}\,,

(84)

In other words, the Lie Derivative of the background function
\bar{F}
gives the variation of the perturbations
\delta\tilde{F}(\tilde{x})-\delta F(\tilde{x})
under the infinitesimal change of coordinates Eq. (
73
).

Remark 1.13
(
Coordinate dependence
)
–

As this expression is at first order, it can be expressed at the coordinate
x
or
\tilde{x}
. Moreover, the
\varepsilon
term is implicit as the function
\boldsymbol{\xi}
is perturbative.

Remark 1.14
(
Convention
)
–

Some references define the Lie Derivative with the opposite sign. Here we use the rule
New field minus the Old field
to define the Lie Derivative.

Vector Field

We now turn our attention to vector fields. Let
\boldsymbol{V}(x)
be a vector field decomposed into a background part and a perturbation as

\boldsymbol{V}(\boldsymbol{x})=\bar{\boldsymbol{V}}(\boldsymbol{x})+\varepsilon\,\delta\boldsymbol{V}(\boldsymbol{x})\,.

(85)

Under the change of coordinates given by Eq. (
73
), and using the transformation rule for vectors given by Eq. (
3
), we get

\displaystyle\tilde{V}^{\mu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle=V^{\nu}(\boldsymbol{x})\frac{\partial\tilde{x}^{\mu}}{\partial x^{\nu}}

(86)
