\displaystyle=\nu_{\ell}\;\;{{}_{-2}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}}\,,

(200)

\displaystyle\nu_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{(\ell+2)!}{(\ell-2)!}}\,.

(201)

The Spin Weighted Spherical Harmonics satisfy the orthogonality condition

\int\;{{}_{s}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}}\,{{}_{s}Y^{\star}_{\ell_{2},m_{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}=\delta_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\delta_{m_{1},m_{2}}\,,

(202)

and the conjugation relation

{{}_{-s}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.22272pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.22272pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.33838pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.67026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.22272pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.22272pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.33838pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.67026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.22272pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.22272pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.33838pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.67026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.22272pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.22272pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.33838pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.67026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-m}}}}}=(-1)^{s+m}{{}_{m}Y^{\star}_{s,\ell}}\,.

(203)

Again, explicit expressions of the Spin Weighted Spherical Harmonics for
s=0,1,2
and
\ell=0,1
are given in Appendix.
1
.

5
Statistics

In this Section, we want to introduce a very important tool in Cosmology, and in Physics in general: The power spectrum. We recall the definition of the lensing potential

\phi(z,\boldsymbol{n})=-2\int_{\eta_{\mathrm{e}}}^{\eta_{\mathrm{o}}}\;\frac{\eta-\eta_{\mathrm{e}}}{\left(\eta_{\mathrm{o}}-\eta_{\mathrm{e}}\right)(\eta_{\mathrm{o}}-\eta)}\Psi_{\mathrm{W}}(\eta,\eta_{\mathrm{o}}-\eta,\boldsymbol{n})\,\mathrm{d}\eta\,,

(204)

where we expressed it as a function of the observed redshift of a given source
z
, the explicit relation between
z
and
\eta
being given by Eq. (
52
) and Eq. (
53
). Hence it is understood that
\eta_{\mathrm{o}}
and
\eta
stand for
\eta_{\mathrm{o}}(z)
and
\eta(z)
respectively, and that

\mathrm{d}\eta=-\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{a_{0}H(z)}\,.

(205)

Moreover, it is understood that
\boldsymbol{n}
is a given direction in the sky.
The lensing potential depends on the Weyl potential
\Psi_{\mathrm{W}}
, which depends, through the Einstein’s Equation, on the fluctuations of matter distribution. Without going into much details, the distribution of homogeneities is a random distribution. Hence, one cannot predict, say, the exact value of
\phi(z,\boldsymbol{n})
for a given value of
z
and
\boldsymbol{n}
. However, we can study statistical properties of this random field.

To do so, we introduce the decomposition of the lensing potential in terms of the spherical harmonics

\phi(z,\boldsymbol{n})=\sum_{\ell,m}\phi_{\ell,m}(z)\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}(\boldsymbol{n})\,.

(206)

The complex coefficients
\phi_{\ell,m}
are equivalent to the Fourier coefficients of a
usual
function
f(\boldsymbol{x})
. Using the conjugation property of the Spherical Harmonics Eq. (
194
), and the fact that the lensing potential
\phi
is real, we obtain the same kind of condition for these coefficients

\phi^{\star}_{\ell,m}(z)=(-1)^{m}\phi_{\ell,-m}(z)\,.

(207)

The main idea is the following. We assume that these coefficients have zero mean and follow a Gaussian distribution, i.e.

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{\ell,m}(z)\rangle

\displaystyle=0\,,

(208)

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{\ell_{1},m_{1}}(z_{1})\phi^{\star}_{\ell_{2},m_{2}}(z_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=C^{\phi}_{\ell_{1}}(z_{1},z_{2})\delta_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\delta_{m_{1},m_{2}}\,.

(209)

Here, the expectation value is to be understood as an average over all the
random realisation of the Universe
. Of course, this is not possible in principle: One would need to be a God-like creature to create several realisations of the Universe and compute these expectation values. We will set this comment aside for now, and later we will explain how in practise one can use these relations. In the second condition, the numbers
C^{\phi}_{\ell}
are the power spectrum of the lensing potential and are fundamental in Cosmology: Their values can be predicted from various cosmological models and observations allow us to confront these different models. This second condition enforces that the random field
\phi
is statistically isotropic. This is also seen computing the correlation function given by

\displaystyle\zeta^{\phi}(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1};z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})

\displaystyle\equiv\langle\phi(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\phi(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle

(210)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{1},m_{1}}\sum_{\ell_{1},m_{2}}\langle\phi_{\ell_{1},m_{1}}\phi_{\ell_{2},m_{2}}\rangle\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}(\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{2},m_{2}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{2},m_{2}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{2},m_{2}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{2},m_{2}}}}}(\boldsymbol{n}_{2})

(211)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{1},m_{1}}\sum_{\ell_{1},m_{2}}\langle\phi_{\ell_{1},m_{1}}\phi^{\star}_{\ell_{2},-m_{2}}\rangle(-1)^{m_{2}}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}(\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{2},m_{2}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{2},m_{2}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{2},m_{2}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{2},m_{2}}}}}(\boldsymbol{n}_{2})

(212)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{1},m_{1}}\sum_{\ell_{1},m_{2}}C^{\phi}_{\ell_{1}}(z_{1},z_{2})\delta_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\delta_{m_{1},m_{2}}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[19.973pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[12.20505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell_{1},m_{1}}}}}(\boldsymbol{n}_{1})Y^{\star}_{\ell_{2},-m_{2}}(\boldsymbol{n}_{2})

(213)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell}C^{\phi}_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})\left(\sum_{m}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}(\boldsymbol{n}_{1})Y^{\star}_{\ell,-m}(\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\right)

(214)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell}\frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi}C^{\phi}_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})P_{\ell}(\mu)\,,

(215)

where
P_{\ell}
are the Legendre polynomials. We used the conjugation properties of the Spherical Harmonics and the coefficients
\phi_{\ell,m}
and the addition property of the Spherical Harmonics, see Appendix
2
. Moreover, we defined

\mu\equiv\boldsymbol{n}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{2}=\cos\varphi\,,

(216)

where
\varphi
is the angle between
\boldsymbol{n}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{n}_{2}
. In the end, we have that the correlation function of the lensing potential is given by

\displaystyle\zeta^{\phi}(z_{1},z_{2};\mu)=\sum_{\ell}\frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi}C^{\phi}_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})P_{\ell}(\mu)\,.

(217)

This relation shows that the correlation function only depends on the angle between the two direction
\boldsymbol{n}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{n}_{2}
, which is the definition of the statistical isotropy. This justifies, afterwards, the assumption that the coefficients
\phi_{\ell,m}
follow a Gaussian distribution.

Finally, we want to decompose the shear as a tensor field on the sphere. Recall that the relationship between the components of the shear and the lensing potential are given by Eq. (
188
) and Eq. (
189
). We define the shear in the
(+,-)
basis as

\gamma^{\pm}=\gamma_{1}\pm\mathrm{i}\gamma_{2}

(218)

From these relations, it is straightforward to show that

\displaystyle\gamma^{+}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}{\not{\partial}}^{2}\phi\,,

(219)

\displaystyle\gamma^{-}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}{{\not{\partial}^{\star}}}^{2}\phi\,.

(220)

More details can be found for example in
[
81
,
66
]
. Using the decomposition of the lensing potential in Spherical Harmonics Eq. (
206
) and the properties of the slashed derivatives Eq. (
199
) and Eq. (
200
), we can show that the decomposition of the components of the shear is

\displaystyle\gamma^{+}(z,\boldsymbol{n})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=2,m}\phi_{\ell,m}(z)\nu_{\ell}\;{{}_{+2}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}}(\boldsymbol{n})\,,

(221)

\displaystyle\gamma^{-}(z,\boldsymbol{n})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=2,m}\phi_{\ell,m}(z)\nu_{\ell}\;{{}_{-2}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}}(\boldsymbol{n})\,.

(222)

Note that the terms corresponding to
\ell=0,1
vanish because of the definitions of the slashed derivatives Eq. (
195
) and Eq. (
196
).

Remark 1.21
(
Monopole and dipole
)
–

In the context of Spherical Harmonics expansion, terms corresponding to
\ell=0
and
\ell=1
are called respectively
monopole
and
dipole
terms. Generally, these terms are not used or neglected. We discuss here the reason.

A monopole term, as
Y_{0,0}(\theta,\varphi)
is constant, corresponds to

\phi(z,\boldsymbol{n})=\phi_{0}(z)\,,

(223)

using the deflection relation Eq (
181
), this implies

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{e}}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}\,.

(224)

In other words, a monopole term does not lead to any lensing and is hence not observable.

To understand why a dipole term is not observable, let us consider an observer moving at constant speed
v
in direction
\tilde{z}
in a pure
FRLW
spacetime. Her
4
-velocity is (in cosmic time)

\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-a^{2}v^{2}}}(\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}+v\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\tilde{z}})\,,

(225)

and the metric is

\boldsymbol{g}=-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}+a^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}^{2}\,.

(226)

Defining the coordinates

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=\tilde{t}\,,

(227)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=\tilde{x}\,,

(228)

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=\tilde{y}\,,

(229)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=\tilde{z}-vt\,,

(230)

the
4
-velocity becomes

\boldsymbol{u}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-a^{2}v^{2}}}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}\,,

(231)

i.e. the observer is now at rest.
The metric in the new coordinate system reads

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}

\displaystyle=-(1-a^{2}v^{2})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}+a^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{x}^{2}+2a^{2}v\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}z

(232)

\displaystyle=a^{2}\big(-(1-a^{2}v^{2})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\eta^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{x}^{2}+2av\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}z\big)\,,

(233)

where the last equality is expressed in conformal time. Using the formalism presented in Section
2
, we get

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}v^{2}\,,

(234)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle=0\,,

(235)

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=avz\,,

(236)

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=0\,,

(237)

and the Weyl potential is given by

\Psi_{\mathrm{W}}=-\frac{1}{2}v^{2}+a^{\prime}vz=-\frac{1}{2}v^{2}+a^{\prime}vr\cos\theta\,,

(238)

where we converted
z
in Spherical Coordinates. The first term leads to a monopole term in the lensing potential and does not contribute as we discussed above. The contribution of the second term to the lensing potential is of the form

\phi(z,\boldsymbol{n})=\phi_{1}(z)\cos\theta\,,

(239)

which corresponds to a term with
\ell=1

m=0
(terms with
m=\pm 1
can be obtained the same way with a velocity in different directions). The bottom line is the following:Even in a non-perturbed Universe, the motion of the observer with respect to the background Universe leads to a
fake
dipole term with
\ell=1
. Hence, there is a degeneracy between an intrinsic dipole coming from the cosmological perturbations and from the velocity of the observer. Moreover, the shear (the quantity we want to study) is insensitive to any dipole term. This can be seen directly using the explicit formula Eq. (
188
) and Eq. (
189
) with
\phi\propto\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.11337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.79526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.11337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.79526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.11337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.79526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.11337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.79526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1,m}}}}
or noting that the sums in Eq. (
221
) and Eq. (
222
) start at
\ell=2
.

PART 2 Galaxy Number Counts in Conformal Frames

The Einstein and Jordan Frames
, Juan Manuel García Arcos

Chapter 4
General Introduction

In this Part, I present the article Basundhara, Ruth and I wrote
[
82
]
. This was the first article of my PhD, and I really enjoyed working on the topic with Basundhara. I remember the day I managed to prove the main relations, I was in a Unicorn-themed café in Paris, and I messaged Basundhara straight away!

One big puzzle in modern Cosmology is Dark Energy. Large scale observations suggest that the Universe has been recently accelerating, since roughly
5
billions years ago. The problem is that the measured value of the cosmological constant, or equivalently, of the vacuum energy, is approximately
10^{-120}
times smaller than the naive value expected from Quantum Field Theory. Tremendous attempts have been made to reconcile these two values. Modified Theories of Gravity form a broad topic in Cosmology. A famous example is Horndeski’s theory, see for example
[
118
,
52
,
49
,
156
,
23
]
for generic reviews.

The simplest models of Modified Gravity are the so-called
ST
(
ST
) theories, which we will consider here. In such theories, a scalar field is coupled to gravity (hence the name), see for example
[
84
,
188
]
for a nice introduction. Horndeski’s theory mentioned above is, under some assumptions, the most general
ST
theory.

ST
theories can be studied in different frames, the two most important ones being the Einstein and the Jordan frames. In the Jordan frame, matter is minimally coupled to gravity in the action, but there is an extra coupling term between the curvature and the scalar field, which introduces new terms in the Einstein’s Equations. In the Einstein frame, there is a non-minimal coupling between matter and the metric. The matter particles do not follow geodesics in this frame. However, the scalar field is not directly coupled to the curvature, and the Einstein’s Equations take their usual form.

People have long debated about the equivalence between these two frames, and if physical observables should be frame-independent, see for example
[
48
,
42
,
77
,
76
,
175
,
37
,
57
,
53
,
53
,
175
,
152
,
119
]
for different discussions. However, the answer is not consensual yet. Some authors claim that the equivalence is not established
[
12
]
and that observables depend on the frame
[
115
,
116
]
. It has also been proposed that the equivalence breaks down at the quantum level
[
78
]
.

The objective of our work is to consider the
GNC
, an observable introduced by Bonvin in
[
24
]
which quantifies the angular fluctuations of the number of observed galaxy. This quantity has been studied extensively recently, see
[
54
,
191
,
93
,
67
]
. As the
GNC
is indeed an observable that astronomers can measure, we expect it to be frame-independent, which we show explicitly.

The Part is structured as follows. Chapter
4
is this Introduction. In Chapter
5
we discuss general concepts about Conformal Frames. In particular, we give a vivid physical interpretation to conformally related frames. We also present the main ingredients to work with
ST
theories, and discuss the specific example of the
FRLW
spacetime. In Chapter
6
, we define the
GNC
and show explicitly that this observable is frame-independent. We conclude in Chapter
7
.

Chapter 5
Conformal Frames

In this Chapter, we present the theory of conformal transformations and conformal frames. In the first Section, we present the definition of conformal transformations and discuss their physical interpretation. Conformal transformations were introduced by Brans and Dicke, see for example
[
25
,
57
,
57
,
141
]
. Physics of conformal frames has since then been broadly studied, for example by Catena
[
42
]
or by Chiba
[
48
]
.

In the second Section, we introduce a coupling between gravity and a scalar field. We discuss the conformal transformations in this context and present our first
dictionary
: A generic method to derive equivalent theories from one frame to another. The study of a scalar field coupled to conformally related metrics has been studied extensively, and has plenty of applications, e.g. multifield inflation theories, see
[
119
]
for a good example.

In the third Section, we discuss the specific Cosmological example of the
FRLW
metric in two conformally related metrics. Again, this topic has been studied in great details in the past, for example by Faraoni and Rondeau
[
77
,
76
,
78
,
175
]
.

Menu of the Chapter

1
Definition

1
A Tale of Two Frames

We present in this Section the concept of conformally related frames, old friends we already encountered in Theorem
1.1
. We recall here the definition.

Definition 2.1
(
Conformally related frames
)
–

Let
\boldsymbol{g}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}
two metric on the same manifold (with the same signature) with coordinates
x^{\mu}
. They are said to be conformally related if there is a positive and smooth function
\Omega(x)
such that

\boldsymbol{g}(x)=\Omega^{2}(x)\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}(x)\,.

(1)

This relation is also called a conformal transformation, and the function
\Omega^{2}(x)
is the conformal factor.

In other words, the two metrics differ only by a coordinate-dependent multiplication. We also stress the fact that a conformal transformation is not a coordinate transformation. The manifold on which both metrics act, together with its set of coordinates
x^{\mu}
, is not modified. Hence, a conformal transformation only changes the value of the metric tensor. We will call a manifold together with a specific metric
a frame
. Hence, as we consider two metrics here, we have two different frames in our hands.

Definition 2.2
(
Jordan and Einstein frames
)
–

The frame associated with
\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}
is the
Jordan frame
while the frame associated with
\boldsymbol{g}
is the
Einstein frame
.

Under the transformation Eq. (
1
), the various tensors of General Relativity in both frames can be related . The exact expressions for these transformations can be found e.g. in
[
77
,
74
,
59
]
. To only give one example, the Ricci scalars are related as (in
4
dimensions)

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Omega^{2}}\left(\tilde{R}-6\frac{\tilde{\Box}\Omega}{\Omega}\right)\,,

(2)

\displaystyle\tilde{R}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{2}\left(R+6\frac{\Box\Omega}{\Omega}-12\frac{g^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\Omega\nabla_{\nu}\Omega)}{\Omega^{2}}\right)\,.

(3)

Note that in these relations, the covariant derivatives (and the d’Alembertian) are not the same: The connection
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
(resp.
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}
) is associated with
\boldsymbol{g}
(resp.
\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}
). The first term of these relations can be interpreted as follows: The relationship between the frames Eq. (
1
) means that dimensionless lengths are bigger in the Einstein frame (assuming
\Omega>1
). The curvature being the inverse of a length squared, it is smaller in the Einstein frame, hence the presence of
\Omega^{2}
in the denominator.

2
Physical Interpretation

The definition of a conformal transformation Eq. (
1
) together with the various transformation (e.g. Eq. (
2
) and Eq. (
3
)) do not provide much more physical insight. In this Section, we give a nice physical interpretation to better understand conformal transformations. This interpretation will follow us through this journey and we want to make it clear from the beginning. The discussion is inspired from
[
48
]
and
[
57
]
.

The relationship between the two frames Eq. (
1
) is nothing else as a proxy describing how lengths and times are measured in both frames. More precisely, let us assume that we are interested to measure a given length, say your own height, in both frames. To do so, we need to define a
ruler
: An object (or a physical process) with which the length of another object will be compared. When this is done, the length of any given object can be measured in units of the ruler.

In the Jordan frame, one specific ruler is used to define
1\text{\,}\mathrm{\tilde{m}_{J}}
, a
Jordan-meter
. With this ruler, any given length can be expressed in Jordan-meter as

L=$\tilde{\ell}\text{\,}\mathrm{\tilde{m}_{J}}$\,,

(4)

meaning that this objects measures
\ell
units of length in this frame. Note that here,
L
is the physical length of the object, as an invariant and abstract quantity, while
\ell
is a dimensionless number measuring this length in a given frame with given units, see Section
1
. The same reasoning can be done in the Einstein frame, defining
1\text{\,}\mathrm{m_{E}}
, a
Einstein-meter
and measuring the length of the same object as

L=$\ell\text{\,}\mathrm{m_{E}}$\,.

(5)

We can now give a nice interpretation of the definition of conformal frames Eq.(
1
). It simply relates the two dimensionless numbers as (recall that
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}{s}^{2}={g}_{\mu\nu}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}{x}^{\mu}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}{x}^{\nu}
measures a length
squared
)

\displaystyle\ell(x)=\Omega(x)\tilde{\ell}(x)\,.

(6)

Remark 2.1
(
Relation between the rulers
)
–

The physical length of an object appearing in Eq. (
4
) and Eq. (
5
) should be the same in both frames, which means that the units are related as

\frac{\ell(x)}{\tilde{\ell}(x)}=\frac{$1\text{\,}\mathrm{\tilde{m}_{J}}$(x)}{$1\text{\,}\mathrm{m_{E}}$(x)}=\Omega(x)\,.

(7)

We can generalize the fundamental relation Eq. (
6
) to other units. For example (setting
c=\hbar=1
), times follow the same relations, while the inverse relation holds for masses or energies.
This justifies the fact that the rulers (and the units) are also coordinate-dependent! To phrase it nicely: In the theory of conformal frames, you are allowed to change your ruler over time as well as when you travel! Note also that if the intrinsic value for
1\text{\,}\mathrm{m_{E}}
increases, then
\ell
has to decrease. In other words, my size in meters (roughly
1.7
) is much smaller than my size in centimeters (roughly
170
)!

In this context, it can seem difficult to do Physics
1
1

1

But also very convenient: If, a couple of years ago, you would had added a little bit of mass to the reference kilogram in the
Bureau international des poids et mesures
, you would have lost weight without any effort!
: How can I make precise experiments if every two minutes I change my ruler (and my clocks, scales, thermometers, and so on)? The answer is that one should define a
reference length
by considering a given physical process (which can be of any kind: The wavelength of a given atomic transition, the distance between the Earth and the Sun, the mean size of an animal,…). In both frames, this reference process has dimensionless length
\tilde{\ell}_{\mathrm{R}}
and
{\ell}_{\mathrm{R}}
. When measuring another physical length, the key is two compare its dimensionless length
\tilde{\ell}
and
{\ell}
with the reference length of the considered frame, which is nothing else to say that in Physics, ratios are more important than absolute numbers, which is the essence of what we stated in Section
1
!

3
An Example: FRLW and Minkowski

We present here a toy-model of a conformal transformation: The
FRLW
metric. This will be explained in more details in Section
3
, but we find it insightful to provide a qualitative explanation first.

Let us consider the two metrics
2
2

2

As there is only one scale factor, we omit the tilde for clarity.

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\eta^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{x}^{2}\,,

(8)

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}

\displaystyle=a(\eta)^{2}(-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\eta^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{x}^{2})\,.

(9)

They are good friends of us: The Minkowski and the
FRLW
metric, respectively. They are obviously related with the conformal factor
\Omega(x)=a(\eta)^{-1}
.

We will assume that the Jordan frame is our usual metric system, where
$1\text{\,}\mathrm{\tilde{m}_{J}}$=$1\text{\,}\mathrm{m}$
, while in the Einstein frame, people take as ruler the distance between the Milky Way and another given far away galaxy, let us call it the Rooibos Galaxy. They use then
$1\text{\,}\mathrm{m_{E}}$=$10^{23}\text{\,}\mathrm{m}$
. In this frame, usual distances are really tiny, a trip between Geneva and St. Gallen back and forth is about
10^{-17}\text{\,}\mathrm{m_{E}}
…

Using the property that physical distances are frame independent Eq. (
7
), we get

\frac{\ell}{\tilde{\ell}}=\Omega=10^{-23}\,,

(10)

in other words, distances in the Einstein frames appear much smaller as the ruler is much bigger. Note however that this relation holds only at the present time: As in the Einstein frame, the ruler is a distance between the Milky Way and the Rooibos Galaxy, the physical value of
1\text{\,}\mathrm{m_{E}}
will change over time due to the expansion, and hence the dimensionless length
{\ell}
of any measurement in this frame will decrease!

The question arising now is the following: How to make measurements in the Einstein frame? In the Jordan frame, it is
business as usual
: The Universe is in expansion and everything goes as explained in Chapter
3
. In the Einstein frame, things are different: Astronomers measuring the distance between us and the Rooibos Galaxy always find
1\text{\,}\mathrm{m_{E}}
, by definition, and conclude, for example, that the Hubble constant
H(t)
vanishes at all times, which is indeed the case in the Minkowski metric. The key is to consider, as explained in Section
2
, a given physical process, say the wavelength of a given atomic transition, which defines
{\ell}_{\mathrm{R}}
. As the ruler in the Einstein frame keeps getting bigger, the dimensionless value of
{\ell}_{\mathrm{R}}
keeps decreasing! This will let the astronomers understand that the Universe is indeed in expansion.

In practice, the expansion of the Universe is inferred through redshift measurements. Assume again that an astronomer receives some light signal, from the Rooibos Galaxy, produced by an atomic transition. The astronomer will reproduce this transition in the lab, measure its wavelength, and compare it with the received wavelength. In this context, the lab wavelength takes the role of the reference length and there is in fine no need to use a ruler whatsoever, the received wavelength is bigger than the reference one anyway.

One last important comment: Assume that this measurement has been performed, and that the received wavelength is indeed smaller than the wavelength produced in the laboratory. Does this necessarily mean that the Universe is expanding? The wavelength of a given atomic transition is given by
\lambda=Ca_{\mathrm{B}}\alpha
, where
a_{\mathrm{B}}
is the Bohr radius and
\alpha
is the fine structure constant, and
C
is an irrelevant constant number. If the Bohr radius decreases over time, or if the value fine structure constant gets smaller, the wavelength will intrinsically become smaller. In this case, the observer will measure a redshift, which won’t be due to the expansion of the Universe! We will discuss this point in more details in Section
5
.

4
Disformal Transformations

In this Section, we briefly discuss disformal transformations, an more general version of conformal transformations. They were first introduced by Bekenstein
[
16
]
, and were later developed by several authors, see for example
[
47
,
150
,
85
,
58
]
for various discussions and applications to Cosmology. We present here the main ideas and more details can be found in
[
95
]
, an extension of the reference we present in this Part. Under a disformal transformation, both frames as related as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{2}\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}+\Lambda\boldsymbol{\nabla}\phi\otimes\boldsymbol{\nabla}\phi\,,

(11)

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{2}\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}+\Lambda\nabla_{\mu}\phi\nabla_{\nu}\phi\,.

(12)

Here,
\Omega
and
\Lambda
are smooth function of the scalar field
\phi
. A dependence on
(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\phi)^{2}
would also be possible but we do not consider this case here. As in the case of conformal transformations, the metric
\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}
(resp.
\boldsymbol{g}
) is associated with the Jordan frame (resp. Einstein).

We assume that the metric in the Jordan frame is
FRLW
(in cosmic time)

\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}=-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}+a^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{x}^{2}\,.

(13)

Using the transformation rule Eq. (
11
), the metric in the Einstein frame is given by

\boldsymbol{g}=-(\Omega^{2}-\Lambda\dot{\phi}^{2})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}+\Omega^{2}a^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{x}^{2}\,,

(14)

where we assumed a cosmological setup, i.e. the scalar field only depends on time. The main difference with conformal transformations is that times and lengths are not affected in a similar way, as can be seen directly from Eq. (
14
), as both coefficients are different. In this context, lengths
\ell
and times
\tau
are related as

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle=\Omega\tilde{\ell}\,,

(15)

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Omega^{2}-\Lambda\dot{\phi}^{2}}\tilde{\tau}\,.

(16)

From these rules, other transformation rules can be derived. Let us detail the example for a mass. If we assume that the unit for action (e.g.
\hbar
is kept constant in both frames, see Chapter
1
for more details), a mass is given by

M=\frac{AT}{L}\,,

(17)

where
A
,
T
and
L
respectively represent an action, a time and a length. The transformation rule is given by

m=\frac{\sqrt{\Omega^{2}-\Lambda\dot{\phi}^{2}}}{\Omega}\tilde{m}\,.

(18)

More examples are provided in
[
95
]
(Section
2.1
).

The bottom line is that, even in the case of disformal transformations, both frames are still equivalent. This should not be a surprise: If one is allowed to change lengths over space and time, why not changing time too? We will not go further into the details, as the logic is the same as for conformal transformations.

5
A Philosophiconomical Reflexion

A varying reference length, the only explanation?

We discussed the possibility of varying physical laws at the end of Section
3
. This topic has been discussed extensively in
[
198
,
161
]
, and experimental tests have been mentioned in
[
17
,
89
,
26
,
120
]
and I am not claiming to state anything revolutionary. The goal of the present section is to warn the reader about other possibilities.

We stated that in Physics, one should consider dimensionless ratio, and avoid using absolute or dimensionful quantities. We also argued why the use of a reference length is important: A different reference length over time is a smoking gun for a change of the ruler over time. Note that we will here only discuss lengths (and rulers) and time variations, but the logic applies for any other physical quantity and spatial variations.

However, the physical interpretation of a varying reference length can be different: Maybe the ruler stays the same but the reference length itself varies over time! This would be a signature that the law of Physics themselves change over time. Assume that you take as reference length the wavelength of the first atomic transition of the hydrogen atom (the Lyman series with
n=2
). For the sake of the argument, let us also assume that you use some mechanical device to measure this wavelength, hence the gravity plays a role in the measurement. You make this measurement today and find
\lambda_{0}=$121.57\text{\,}\mathrm{nm}$
, and when you redo it tomorrow, you obtain this time
\lambda_{0}=$124.3\text{\,}\mathrm{nm}$
. Assuming your errors are well-controlled, what happened?

According to what we said above, we would have assumed that your ruler changed, maybe you modified the gravitational device: Did you change the length of one pendulum? Or the oscillating mass? We would like to investigate the other solutions to the problem, so we will assume that, as a diligent scientist, you did not change your setup between the different experiments.

The other assumption would be that the law of Physics are changing over time. For example, what if the energy of the photon is given by
E=\hbar\omega+\delta E(t)
, where
\delta E(t)
is a function of time, starting at
0
and slowly increasing? Or what if the value of
\hbar
is changing over time? Or even better, what if the dimensionless fine structure constant changed? All these effects would modify the wavelength of the transition. Another possibility is that the value of
G_{\mathrm{N}}
changes over time, which would modify the action of your mechanical device. In all these scenarios, the reference length would be modified, not because your ruler has changed but because the laws of Physics are different (see Section
2
where we will see a simple example where this is due to the scalar field).

The question is now: In this context, how can we be sure that Physics is not changing over time? Checking every day the wavelength of all the atomic transition is not realistic, and this actually would not even help. Indeed, if Physics is changing you may not even notice it if the ruler you’re using is changing at a specific rate, cancelling the effect of the varying laws.

A very good is example is the
FRLW
Universe: As we saw in Section
3
, we cannot distinguish between and expanding Universe and the Minkowski spacetime, where the rulers is varying and a scenario in which physical laws (e.g. the value of the fine structure constant) change over time. Both interpretations lead to the same observables and should be considered as equivalent (at least as long as we only consider observations at the background level).

The problem is clear: There is somehow a loophole, and it is very difficult (if not impossible) to prove that Physics does not vary with time. It is however reasonable to assume that this is the case: From a Human point of view, the Universe seems to be the same over time. I have never seen my cat getting suddenly bigger than me, and I can check every day when I bike that the laws of Mechanics and Gravity are the same (luckily for my safety!).

To conclude this discussion in a broader context, let me share maybe my personal experience. When I try to solve a Physics problem, I often find myself using tools and equations I know without questioning their use or relevance enough. The ideas presented in this Section should, according to my opinion, always accompany Physicists, especially theoreticians who may get lost in their ideas. What I discussed here can seem very basic but is actually very deep if one tries to think about it. I hope these words are not just a bottle in the sea but will resonate in some minds!

Money money money

I would like to give a short example taken from economics and daily life to show how the concepts presented in this section can be applied outside Physics. Before going into details, I will simplify things and only consider two countries without considering fees of any kind or other subtleties.

In economics, it is also meaningless to deal with
dimensional
variables. For example, what is the intrinsic value of one Swiss Franc, or of one Euro? This question alone does not have any quantitative answer. However, the ratio between those two quantities is in general well-defined :
1\ \mathrm{EUR}/1\ \mathrm{CHF}
has a specific value (in general between
1
and
1.5
in this example) which can vary in time. The money units
\mathrm{CHF}
and
\mathrm{EUR}
act as the rulers in both frames we discussed above!

However, one could still argue that this ratio is meaningless. Indeed, there is plenty of currencies in the world and they do not mean anything per se. What makes much more sense for a human is to know what they can get with such financial units, for example how many Unconstructed Mokas or Noodles plates they can buy. In this sense, the meaningful ratios are of the form

\frac{1\ \mathrm{Noodles\ plate}}{1\ \mathrm{CHF}}\,.

(19)

In the framework we described above, this ratio correspond exactly to the
reference length
, which in this case is a
reference price
, more generally called
consumer price index
, indicating the price of a pool of goods. The fact that Physics should be the same in different frames translates here into the fact that you cannot get richer by changing your Swiss Francs to Euros (again, neglecting differences between countries, fees, speculation, …). For a physical distance
L=$\ell\text{\,}\mathrm{m}$
, the comparison is shown in Tab.
1

Noodles plate

\rightarrow

L

Currency

\rightarrow

1\mathrm{m}

Price

\rightarrow

\ell

Table 1:
Comparison between the physical formalism and an example taken from Economics.

One last comment on this topic: When I was given my first Cosmology class, the professor mentioned inflation. By that time, I was still a kid and had a vague idea that inflation was something bad related to money. At first, I did not understand why one would talk about this in a theoretical Physics class, until I discovered what it really was. Since then, I put this idea I had inflation somewhere in my brain and never looked at it until the middle of my PhD, when I was working on the article with Basundhara and Ruth. It’s only by that time that I understood that inflation is the same concept in Cosmology and in Economics: In both cases, the reference length/price suddenly increases very fast over time!

Much more can be discussed on this topic, and I did put too many elements under the carpet, as I do not want to crowd this thesis with unnecessary details… Let me just point out, to close this interlude, that I find very interesting and actually very insightful to think about Economics or any other field using physical concepts and I can only encourage everyone to do the same!

2
Coupling with a Scalar Field

1
Action of a Scalar Field

In this Section, we introduce
ST
theories: How can we study General Relativity together with a scalar field (as matter fields)? This has been done extensively in the past. The book by Fujii
[
84
]
is a very good introduction to this topic and goes much further than this thesis.

Theoretical physicists love actions. Let us make them happy and build an action for a scalar field. First, we would like to reproduce something similar to the classical formula:
Kinetic minus Potential
. The kinetic energy is proportional to
\dot{\phi}^{2}
. Moreover, if you imagine a metal spring, the spatial deformation will act as a potential energy, so
\nabla_{i}\phi\nabla^{i}\phi
is a good candidate. In a tensorial notation, and taking into account our sign convention, the Lagrangian for a scalar field is

\mathcal{L}_{\phi}=-\frac{1}{2}k(\phi)(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\phi)^{2}-V(\phi)\,,

(20)

where we allow a generic kinetic term
k(\phi)
and we consider an external potential
V(\phi)
. The full action of a
ST
is obtained using the standard prescription and reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ST}}[\boldsymbol{g},\phi]=\int\;\left(\frac{1}{16\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}R-\frac{1}{2}k(\phi)(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\phi)^{2}-V(\phi)\right)\sqrt{-g}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,.

(21)

The scalar field is said to be
minimally coupled
to gravity as they only
interact
through the square root of the metric determinant and the covariant derivative.

Much more can be said about
ST
theories. More details are given by Sotiriou in
[
188
]
, where the general theory is presented together with implications for Cosmology

2
Dictionary for the Scalar Field

The action

We would like to study how the action given by Eq. (
21
) transforms under a conformal transformation of the metric. More details can be found in the very nice thesis of Giammarco Fabiani
[
74
]
where they present the
ST
theories in much details, in particular they consider the possibility of field redefinition. Any interested reader is encouraged to have a look at this work! The work of Morris provided also more details in this direction, see
[
152
]
.

Let us consider a slightly more general version of the
ST
action Eq. (
21
) by adding a non-minimal coupling term to the curvature
A(\phi)
and an interaction with the scalar field
\alpha(\phi)
in the matter Lagrangian

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ST}}[{\boldsymbol{g}},{\phi}]=\int\;\left(\frac{A(\phi)}{16\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}R-\frac{1}{2}k(\phi)(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\phi)^{2}-V(\phi)+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{m}}[\alpha(\phi)\boldsymbol{g},\psi]\right)\sqrt{-g}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,,

(22)

where
\psi
represent the matter fields, e.g. a cosmological fluid.
We perform the usual change of frame given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{2}(\phi)\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}\,,

(23)

where we slightly changed the definition of the conformal factor. Indeed, we assumed that it only depends on the coordinates through the scalar field. Under these transformations the action can be written in the same functional form as

\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{ST}}\left[\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}},{\phi}\right]=\int\;\left(\frac{\tilde{A}(\phi)}{16\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}\tilde{R}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{k}(\phi)(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}\phi)^{2}-\tilde{V}(\phi)+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{m}}[\tilde{\alpha}(\phi)\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}},\psi]\right)\sqrt{-\tilde{g}}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,.

(24)

Using that the action is invariant, i.e.

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ST}}[{\boldsymbol{g}},{\phi}]=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{ST}}\left[\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}},{\phi}\right]\,,

(25)

the functions
\tilde{A}
,
\tilde{k}
and
\tilde{V}
are defined as

\displaystyle\tilde{A}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\Omega^{2}A(\phi)\,,

(26)

\displaystyle\tilde{V}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\Omega^{4}V(\phi)\,,

(27)

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\alpha(\phi)\Omega^{2}\,,

(28)

\displaystyle\tilde{k}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\Omega^{2}\left(k(\phi)-\frac{3}{16\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(\frac{\Omega^{\prime}}{2\Omega}\right)^{2}\right)\,.

(29)

With this transformation, we can define classes of equivalent theories: For each quadruplet of functions
(A,k,V,\alpha)
, we perform the change of frame parametrised by
\Omega
to obtain the same theory expressed with the tilde quantities. As this transformation is nothing but a mere change of frame, we expect the law of Physics to be the same in both frames, which is what interests us in this article, with the
GNC
discussed hereafter.

Let us turn now to our specific example. We make the following assumptions.

•

In the Einstein frame, the scalar field is minimally coupled to gravity:
{A}(\phi)=1
.

•

In the Jordan frame, the fluid is not coupled to the matter fields:
\tilde{\alpha}=1

Under these assumptions, the transformations Eq.(
26
), Eq. (
27
), Eq. (
28
) and Eq. (
29
) become

\displaystyle\tilde{A}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{2}\,,

(30)

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\Omega^{-4}\tilde{V}\,,

(31)

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\Omega^{-2}\,,

(32)

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{k}}{\Omega^{2}}+\frac{3}{16\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(\frac{\Omega^{\prime}}{2\Omega}\right)^{2}\,.

(33)

and the actions in both frames read

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{ST}}[{\boldsymbol{g}},{\phi}]

\displaystyle=\int\;\left(\frac{1}{16\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}R-\frac{1}{2}k(\phi)(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\phi)^{2}-V(\phi)+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{m}}[\Omega^{-2}(\phi){\boldsymbol{g}},\psi]\right)\sqrt{-g}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,,

(34)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ST}}\left[\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}},{\phi}\right]

\displaystyle=\int\;\left(\frac{\Omega^{2}}{16\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}\tilde{R}-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{k}(\phi)(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}\phi)^{2}-\tilde{V}(\phi)+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{m}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}},\psi]\right)\sqrt{-\tilde{g}}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,,

(35)

with
k
,
\tilde{k}
,
V
and
\tilde{V}
arbitrary functions related by Eq. (
33
) and Eq. (
31
). Note that the condition
k,\tilde{k}>0
ensure the stability of the theory.

The energy-momentum tensor

To conclude this section, we discuss the transformation rules of the energy-momentum tensor under the conformal transformation. This transformation being a (local) change of units, we expect the energy-momentum tensor to be modified accordingly, as it quantifies the energy density and pressure. Moreover, as there is a factor
\Omega^{2}
in the matter Lagrangian in Eq. (
35
), we expect the usual conservation equation to be modified.

We present here the final results, but more explanations and derivations can be found in the article
[
82
]
or in the work of Santiago
[
176
]
, with a thorough discussion about this topic.

Using the definition of the energy-momentum tensor given by Eq. (
37
), we get the dictionary for the energy-momentum tensor

\displaystyle\tilde{T}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{2}T_{\mu\nu}\,,

(36)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\tilde{T}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{\tilde{T}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{\tilde{T}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{\tilde{T}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{4}\mathchoice{{T}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{{T}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{{T}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{{T}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}\,,

(37)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\tilde{T}^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\tilde{T}^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\tilde{T}^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\tilde{T}^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{6}\mathchoice{{T}^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{{T}^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{{T}^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{{T}^{{{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\,.

(38)

In the Jordan frame, matter is not coupled to the scalar field, hence the conservation equation still holds, while it is modified in the Einstein frame because of the non trivial interaction
\alpha\neq 1
in Eq. (
32
)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{\nabla}\phi\frac{\partial_{\phi}\Omega}{\Omega}T\,,

(39)

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{T}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(40)

or in components

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{\nu}\phi\frac{\partial_{\phi}\Omega}{\Omega}T\,,

(41)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nabla}^{\mu}\tilde{T}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(42)

with
T=\mathchoice{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{T^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}
. The last term represents a non-conservation term for the fluid, which we will explain below.

3
FRLW in Two Frames

1
Background Universe

Scale factor, Hubble constant and Friedmann equations

In this Section, we apply the
ST
theory to a
FRLW
background. Again, we present the results of the
[
82
]
. We consider two
FRLW
metrics given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}

\displaystyle=a^{2}\left(-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\eta^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{x}^{2}\right)\,,

(43)

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{a}^{2}\left(-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\eta^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{x}^{2}\right)\,.

(44)

Both metrics are related by the conformal transformation Eq. (
23
), which implies

\displaystyle a=\Omega_{0}\tilde{a}\,.

(45)

Remark 2.2
(
Background variables
)
–

Here and in what follows, we will denote background variables with the subscript
0
. It is then understood that all these variables only depend on the conformal time, e.g.

\Omega_{0}=\Omega(\phi_{0}(\eta))\,,

(46)

or

\Omega_{0}^{\prime}=\partial_{\phi}\Omega\phi_{0}^{\prime}\,.

(47)

Remark 2.3
(
Reference lengths
)
–

As explained in Section
2
, the study of conformal frames is easily understood if one define references lengths, associated with a given physical process. Here, we will assume that the reference length does not change in the Jordan frame and equals
\tilde{\ell}=1
(which is somehow equivalent to say that the ruler does not change in this frame). The conformal transformation Eq. (
23
) implies that the reference length in the Einstein frame is

\ell=\Omega\,,

(48)

where we drop the subscript
\mathrm{R}
for clarity.

The relationship between the scale factors allows us to relate both Hubble constants as

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}=\frac{\tilde{a}^{\prime}}{\tilde{a}}=\frac{\Omega}{a}\left(\frac{a}{\Omega}\right)^{\prime}={\mathcal{H}}-\frac{\Omega_{0}^{\prime}}{\Omega_{0}}={\mathcal{H}}-\frac{\ell_{0}^{\prime}}{\ell_{0}}\,.

(49)

This relation can be easily understood. Recall that in the
usual
Jordan frame, the ruler is not modified while it is in the Einstein frame. Assume for example that
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}=0
and
\ell_{0}^{\prime}>0
, which means that the Universe is not expanding and that the ruler in Einstein is shrinking (hence the lengths are artificially increasing). Then we have

{\mathcal{H}}=\frac{\ell_{0}^{\prime}}{\ell_{0}}>0\,

(50)

which is coherent with the assumptions: The lengths seem to be growing, which mimics an expanding Universe.

We present now the Einstein equations in both frames for completeness. Again, more details can be found in
[
152
]
or in the article. We only present the final results here. In the Einstein frame, the Friedmann equations read

\displaystyle 3{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=8\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}\left(a^{2}\rho_{0}+\frac{{(\phi_{0}^{\prime})}^{2}k}{2}+a^{2}V\right)\,,

(51)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-2\frac{{a^{\prime\prime}}}{a}

\displaystyle=8\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}\left(a^{2}P_{0}+\frac{(\phi_{0}^{\prime})^{2}k}{2}-a^{2}V\right)\,.

(52)

They are simply the Friedmann equations with the usual energy density and pressure of a scalar field (and matter). In this frame, the scalar field is considered as matter like the fluid. In the Jordan frame, these equations become

\displaystyle 3\tilde{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{8\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}{\Omega_{0}^{2}}\left(\tilde{a}^{2}\tilde{\rho}_{0}+\frac{(\phi_{0}^{\prime})^{2}\tilde{k}}{2}+\tilde{a}^{2}\tilde{V}\right)-6\tilde{\mathcal{H}}\frac{\phi^{\prime}_{0}\Omega_{0}^{\prime}}{\Omega_{0}}\,,

(53)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-2\frac{\tilde{a}^{\prime\prime}}{\tilde{a}}

\displaystyle=\frac{8\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}{\Omega_{0}^{2}}\left(\tilde{a}^{2}\tilde{P}_{0}+\frac{(\phi_{0}^{\prime})^{2}\tilde{k}}{2}-\tilde{a}^{2}\tilde{V}\right)

(54)

\displaystyle+2\tilde{\mathcal{H}}\frac{\dot{\phi}_{0}\Omega_{0}^{\prime}}{\Omega_{0}}+2\frac{\phi^{\prime\prime}_{0}\Omega_{0}^{\prime}}{\Omega_{0}}+2(\phi_{0}^{\prime})^{2}\left(\left(\frac{\Omega_{0}^{\prime}}{\Omega_{0}}\right)^{2}+\frac{\Omega_{0}^{\prime\prime}}{\Omega_{0}}\right)\,,

where we notice a modification with respect to their usual form: The scalar field is not considered as matter, and the non-minimal coupling introduces new term, which we can interpret as
modified gravity
.

The energy-momentum tenosr

We now turn our attention to the energy-momentum tensor and to the energy conservation equation. Using its transformation rule Eq. (
37
), and the definition of the fluid energy-momentum tensor Eq. (
24
), we get

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho_{0}}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{0}^{4}\rho_{0}\,,

(55)

\displaystyle\tilde{P}_{0}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{0}^{4}P_{0}\,.

(56)

These relations are easily understood considering that pressure and energy density have mass dimension
+4
, and hence their transformation rule must be the opposite one as for lengths. Using the conservation equations Eq. (
40
) and Eq. (
39
), we get the energy conservation equations

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-3\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}(\tilde{\rho}_{0}+\tilde{P}_{0})\,,

(57)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-3{{\mathcal{H}}}(\rho_{0}+P_{0})+\frac{\ell_{0}^{\prime}}{\ell_{0}}(3P_{0}-\rho_{0})\,.

(58)

It is easy to derive the last relation either using the general form Eq. (
39
) or the explicit transformations of the energy density Eq. (
55
), the pressure Eq. (
56
) and the Hubble constant Eq. (
49
). Again, we can understand the last relationship considering two interesting examples. We will assume that
\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}>0
is constant, which represents a physical expansion in the Jordan frame, and
{\mathcal{H}}=0
which means that the Einstein frame is artificially static. The relationship between those two quantities Eq. (
49
) implies
\ell_{0}^{\prime}=-\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}\ell_{0}
: In the Einstein frame, dimensionless lengths are (exponentially) decreasing, the ruler is expanding and dimensionless masses are (exponentially) growing. We consider two specific examples.

1.

If the Universe is filled with cold matter with
P_{0}=0
, we get in the Einstein frame

\rho_{0}^{\prime}=+\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}\rho_{0}\,.

(59)

Moreover, the energy density in a given volume
V_{0}
is defined as

\rho_{0}=\frac{m_{0}}{V_{0}}\,,

(60)

where
m_{0}
is the mass inside the volume. To compute the evolution of the energy, we follow a given mass with time. In the physical Jordan frame, the physical volume
V
becomes, after a time
\mathrm{d}t
, due to the expansion of the Universe as

V_{\mathrm{p}}=V(1+3\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}\mathrm{d}t)\,.

(61)

However, in the Einstein frame, the ruler is expanding so dimensionless volume are decreasing with the rate

V^{\prime}=-3\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}V\,,

(62)

the factor
3
coming from the fact that a volume is a length to the third power. Those two effects cancel exactly, and it appears in the Einstein frame that the dimensionless volume is kept constant. On the other side, dimensionless masses in the Einstein are increasing as

m^{\prime}=\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}m\,,

(63)

which is the only relevant effect whose consequence is that
\rho_{0}
seems to be increasing in this frame.

2.

If the Universe is filled with Dark Energy with
P_{0}=-\rho_{0}
, we get

\rho_{0}^{\prime}=4\tilde{{\mathcal{H}}}\rho_{0}\,.

(64)

Here, the dilution effect does not take place, but dimensionless masses are still growing while dimensionless volumes are still decreasing: The first effect brings a factor
1
while the second a factor of
3
, which makes
1+3=4
in total.

The redshift

We end this discussion on the background
FRLW
metric by presenting the correct expression for the redshift. Again, in the Jordan frame the ruler is kept constant (
\ell_{\mathrm{R}}=1
) the basis expression Eq. (
22
) still holds
3
3

3

Recall that the indices mean
emission
and
reception
.

1+z=\frac{\tilde{\omega}_{\mathrm{e}}}{\tilde{\omega}_{\mathrm{r}}}=\frac{\tilde{a}_{\mathrm{r}}}{\tilde{a}_{\mathrm{e}}}\,,

(65)

where the quantities are measured in the Jordan frame. To compute the redshift in the Einstein frame, we recall that frequencies scale as inverse times, and hence their transformation rules read

\omega=\frac{1}{\ell_{0}}\tilde{\omega}\,.

(66)

Combining those elements, we obtain the redshift expressed in the Einstein frame as

1+z=\frac{\ell_{0,{\mathrm{e}}}\ \omega_{\mathrm{e}}}{\ell_{0,{\mathrm{r}}}\ \omega_{\mathrm{r}}}=\frac{\ell_{0,{\mathrm{e}}}\ {a}_{\mathrm{r}}}{\ell_{0,{\mathrm{r}}}\ {a}_{\mathrm{e}}}\,.

(67)

Again, we can understand this relation with a simple example. Say that
\ell_{0,{\mathrm{r}}}<\ell_{0,{\mathrm{e}}}
and
\omega_{\mathrm{r}}=\omega_{\mathrm{e}}
. This means that dimensionless lengths are getting smaller, so that the ruler is expanding exactly at the same rate as the expansion of the Universe: The dimensionless wavelengths and frequencies are not modified. Thanks to the correction in the formula, we still obtain
z>1
, which is coherent with a physical, frame-independent, redshift.

2
Perturbed Universe

Bardeen potentials

We would like to consider now perturbations to the
FRLW
background. We use the same notations as in Section
2
. We consider the perturbed metrics in the longitudinal gauge

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}

\displaystyle=a^{2}\left(-(1+2\Psi)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\eta^{2}+(1-2\Phi)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{x}^{2}\right)\,,

(68)

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{a}^{2}\left(-(1+2\tilde{\Psi})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\eta^{2}+(1-2\tilde{\Phi})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{x}^{2}\right)\,,

(69)

as well as the perturbed scalar field

\phi(\eta,\boldsymbol{x})=\phi_{0}(\eta)+\delta\phi(\eta,\boldsymbol{x})\,,

(70)

and the perturbed reference length (in the Einstein frame)

\ell(\eta,\boldsymbol{x})=\ell_{0}(\eta)+\delta\ell(\eta,\boldsymbol{x})\,,

(71)

and recall that
\Omega=\ell
.
The interpretation of
\delta\ell
is the following: Consider one constant time slice where
\ell_{0}(\eta)
has some fixed value. If at some given spatial point
\boldsymbol{x}
we have
\delta\ell(\eta,\boldsymbol{x})>0
. This means that at this specific point, dimensionless are slightly bigger than on average for this time slice, and equivalently that the ruler is slightly smaller. We will use this interpretation later on when we consider perturbed equations.

The dictionary for the Bardeen potentials can be easily found using the definitions and reads

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Phi}-\frac{\delta\ell}{\ell_{0}}\,,

(72)

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Psi}+\frac{\delta\ell}{\ell_{0}}\,.

(73)

This can be easily understood as follows. Consider a small coordinate displacement
\delta x
. In the Jordan frame, the measured distance is

L_{\mathrm{J}}=\tilde{a}\delta x(1-2\tilde{\Phi})\,,

(74)

while in the Einstein frame, the measured distance is

L_{\mathrm{E}}=a\delta x(1-2\Phi)\,.

(75)

Recalling that those two distances are related thanks to the conformal transformation via

L_{\mathrm{E}}=\ell L_{\mathrm{J}}=(\ell_{0}+\delta\ell)L_{\mathrm{J}}\,,

(76)

it is straightforward to show that the relation Eq. (
72
) holds. The same logic can be applied for a time measurement, justifying the relation for
\Psi
given by Eq. (
73
).

A direct consequence of Eq. (
72
) and Eq. (
73
) is that Weyl potential is frame independent

\Psi_{\mathrm{W}}=\Psi+\Phi=\tilde{\Psi}+\tilde{\Phi}=\tilde{\Psi}_{\mathrm{W}}\,.

(77)

This is not a surprise as the Weyl potential is used to compute the lensing potential, and we know from Theorem
1.1
that lensing should not be affected by a conformal transformation. More details about invariance under conformal transformations in Cosmology can be found e.g. in
[
139
]
.

Energy-momentum tensor

We want now to describe the perturbations to the energy-momentum tensor. We use the same convention as in Eq. (
117
), Eq. (
118
) and Eq. (
119
) to describe this perturbations in both frames. We want to relate the relative perturbations

\delta(\eta,\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{\rho(\eta,\boldsymbol{x})-\rho_{0}(\eta)}{\rho_{0}(\eta)}\,.

(78)

Using that

\rho=\ell^{-4}\tilde{\rho}\,,

(79)

it is easy to see that at first order, both perturbations are related as

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=\tilde{\delta}-4\frac{\delta\ell}{\ell}\,,

(80)

and the same holds for the relative Pressure perturbation. Again, we can get a nice understanding of this formula considering a simple example: Assume that
\tilde{\delta}(\eta,x)=0
at some spacetime point. This means that in the Jordan frame, there is no energy perturbation at this specific point. If
\delta\ell>0
, lengths tend to be measured slightly bigger at this point, and masses slightly lower in the Einstein frame, which implies that the energy density are also measured slightly lower, with the usual factor of
4
, as explained in the previous section.

The perturbed
4
-velocity in both frame read

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a}\left((1-\Psi)\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}+v^{i}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{i}\right)\,,

(81)

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tilde{a}}\left((1-\tilde{\Psi})\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}+v^{i}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{i}\right)\,,

(82)

where the velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
is the same in both frames, which could be expected as it is a dimensionless quantity (and hence there is no need for a tilded version).

Redshift

We end this Section about perturbations with an important point: The perturbation of the redshift. Let us stress an important point: When observing a photon coming from a galaxy, we measure its direction in the sky
\boldsymbol{n}
and its redshift
z
. Both quantities are in a sense
perturbed
as the photon traveled across the Universe which is not homogeneous and isotropic. Mathematically speaking, we formalise this by assuming that the photon was emitted on some constant time slice corresponding to a background redshift
z_{0}\neq z
. This would be the observed redshift if the Universe was truly
FRLW
, and the formula would be given by Eq. (
22
). In reality, this background redshift and the observed one do not coincide and we define the redshift difference as

\delta z\equiv z-z_{0}\,.

(84)

We need to stress here that
z_{0}
and
\delta z
are
not
observables! They are mathematical tools we use in our computations, but any final, physical, observable results should not depend on it. The background redshift
z_{0}
is not physical as, at the perturbed level, the background spacetime is not well-defined and can be redefined through an infinitesimal coordinate transformation, see Section
2
where we discussed this effect in more details. The situation is shown in Fig.
1
, where a photon is emitted and observed in a perturbed Universe.

Figure 1:

Redshift perturbation

The photon is emitted at a time slice corresponding to
z_{0}
, but the observed redshift is
z
. The difference betweeen these two quantities is
\delta z
. In this specific example, we have
z>z_{0}
so
\delta z>0
. Note that the observed direction is also perturbed because of lensing.

The expression for
\delta z
can be found e.g. in
[
24
]
and reads in the Jordan frame

\displaystyle\delta z(z,\boldsymbol{n})=-(1+z_{0})\left(\tilde{\Psi}(z_{0},\boldsymbol{n})-\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+\int_{0}^{r(z_{0})}\;(\tilde{\Psi}^{\prime}+\tilde{\Phi}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}r\right)\,.

(85)

We can give a physical interpretation for each term in Eq. (
85
).

1.

The first term is a usual potential term: Let us assume that the Bardeen potential
\tilde{\Psi}>0
at the emission position. While travelling towards us, the photon goes down this potential, and as any biker knows, its energy increases. Hence, the observed redshift is reduced and this implies
\delta z<0
.

2.

The second term is a Doppler effect, where
\boldsymbol{n}
is the observed direction and
\boldsymbol{v}
is the peculiar velocity of the galaxy. If
\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}>0
, the galaxy is receding away from us, which increases the redshift because of the Doppler effect, hence
\delta z>0
with this term. This effect is sometimes called the simple Sachs Wolfe effect, see
[
117
]
for more explanations.

3.

The last term is the Integrated Sachs Wolfe effect. It can be understood as follows: If you are biking up hill and the ground is helping you by going up while you are biking, you will lose less energy that if the ground was not moving. This is what happens if the photons travel through a time-dependent potential field, and this reduces their observed redshift. Note that for this term, the
spatial
potential
\Phi
also contributes.

Remark 2.4
(
Monopole terms
)
–

In Eq. (
85
), the terms depending only on the observer position were neglected. These terms are constant and, for example, only contribute to the monopole term of the power spectrum, see Remark
1.21
.

The expression for the redshift perturbation given by Eq. (
85
) is valid in the Jordan frame, as there is no correction to make. In the Einstein frame, the only new term at the source position is
\ell_{{\mathrm{e}}}
, see Eq. (
67
). Using the definition of the perturbation of the reference length, the redshift perturbation in the Einstein frame reads

\displaystyle\delta z(z,\boldsymbol{n})=-(1+z_{0})\left(\Psi(z_{0},\boldsymbol{n})-\frac{\delta\ell(z_{0},\boldsymbol{n})}{\ell_{0}(z_{0})}-\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+\int_{0}^{r(z_{0})}\;(\Psi^{\prime}+\Phi^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}r\right)\,.

(86)

Note that, using the dictionary for the Bardeen potentials Eq. (
73
), it is direct to show that
\delta z
is frame invariant, i.e. that Eq. (
85
) and Eq. (
86
) are equal.

Chapter 6
Galaxy Number Counts

In this Chapter, we present the concept of
GNC
. In the first Section, we introduce the
GNC
as a Cosmological observable introduced by Bonvin et al. in
[
24
]
. The key idea is that, when we perform galaxy surveys, we observe galaxies, together with their angular position and observed redshift. The
GNC
is a physical observable, contrary to, for example, the energy perturbation density. Hence, we expect its value to be frame-independent. In the second Section, we prove this explicitly, at the perturbation level, in a second section.

Menu of the Chapter

1
Introduction

1
General Idea

We present here the concept of
GNC
