. This quantity answers to the question asked by Bonvin et al.
[
24
]
. To rephrase them,

What do galaxy surveys really measure?

When astronomers observe a galaxy, as mentioned in the previous Section, they measure two quantities: Its redshift
z
and its direction on the celestial sphere
\boldsymbol{n}
. Both quantities cannot be measured exactly, hence the galaxy are grouped together into redshift and angular
bins
. If you are provided with a galaxy survey (assuming there is sufficiently enough galaxies), you can perform the following steps.

1.

Choose a redshift bin around
z
and an angular bin around the direction
\boldsymbol{n}
.

2.

Count
all
the galaxies observed in the redshift bin
z
(considering the full sky).

3.

Compute the average number of galaxies
\langle N\rangle(z)
per angular bin.

4.

Count the number of galaxies
N(z,\boldsymbol{n})
in the specific bin around
z
and
\boldsymbol{n}
.

5.

Compute
N(z,\boldsymbol{n})-\langle N\rangle(z)
and normalize it by
\langle N\rangle(z)
to determine if, in this specific direction, there is an over- or under- galaxy density, compared with the average galaxy density at constant redshift
z
.

This process makes sense from an observational point of view: In fine, we only observe galaxies, not energy density nor the full matter distribution.

Following the steps above, we define the
Galaxy Number Counts
(
GNC
)

\displaystyle\Delta(z,\boldsymbol{n})\equiv\frac{N(z,\boldsymbol{n})-\langle N\rangle(z)}{\langle N\rangle(z)}\,.

(1)

Compter les étoiles des deux côtés
, Jessica Mascellino

Remark 2.5
(
Bias
)
–

If you really want to be picky, you could argue that we do not observe galaxies, but the photons they emit. There is a bias problem: Only bright galaxies can be observed, or some galaxies will be hidden by some foreground matter. We neglect these subtleties here and consider the
GNC
from a theoretical perspective, assuming that we are able to observe all the galaxies.

2
Expression of the Galaxy Number Counts

In this Section, we present the mathematical expression of the
GNC
. The detailed derivation can be found in
[
24
]
and we show only the important results here. The
GNC
can be decomposed into two parts as

\displaystyle\Delta(z,\boldsymbol{n})=\delta_{z}(z,\boldsymbol{n})+\frac{\delta V(z,\boldsymbol{n})}{V(z)}\,.

(2)

The first term is the
redshift density perturbation
defined as

\delta_{z}(z,\boldsymbol{n})\equiv\frac{\rho(z,\boldsymbol{n})-\rho(z)}{\rho(z)}\,,

(3)

where
\rho(z)
is the mean energy density at constant observed redshift
z
. The second term is the
volume perturbation
and it quantifies the fact that the physical volume of a redshift bin may be different. It is defined as

V(z,\boldsymbol{n})=V(z)+\delta V(z,\boldsymbol{n})\,,

(4)

where
V(z)
is the average physical volume per bin, at fixed redshift
z
, see Fig
1
.

Figure 1:

Volume perturbation

Both bins appear to have the same angular size (the apparent angular aperture is the same). However, the bin on the right undergoes more lensing and corresponds to a bigger physical volume, and hence contains more galaxies (assuming the physical densities are equal).

Remark 2.6
(
Two different energy perturbations
)
–

The quantity
\delta_{z}
is
not
the usual relative energy perturbation of the energy-momentum tensor. To understand that, let us have another look at Fig.
1
. The photon was emitted on the time slice corresponding to
z_{0}
. Hence, the energy density at the emission position should be expanded around the background quantity at this specific redshift

\rho(z,\boldsymbol{n})=\rho_{0}(z_{0})+\delta\rho(z,\boldsymbol{n})\,.

(5)

This last term
\delta\rho(z,\boldsymbol{n})
is the perturbation to the energy density appearing in the expression of the energy-momentum tensor discussed in Section
3
. On the other side, the redshift density perturbation
\delta_{z}(z,\boldsymbol{n})
is computed with respect to a given observed redshift which, depending on the chosen gauge, may not correspond to a constant time slice.

As usual, we can try to give a nice interpretation of Eq. (
2
). The first term is quite intuitive: In a given pixel, the
GNC
is the same as the redshift density perturbation, quantifying the over- or under- energy density in this pixel. However, a redshift bin should be converted to a physical bin to really estimate the number of galaxies it contains. This is why it is also important to consider the volume perturbation. Indeed, because of lensing, the physical volume of a redshift bin may be slightly bigger or slightly smaller. If the physical volume is, say, slightly bigger than the average, we have
\delta V>0
, see Fig.
1
. In this case, the bin we are observing contains more galaxy simply because it represents a bigger portion of space, which enhances the
GNC
.

For completeness, we provide the full expression for the
GNC
, taken from
[
24
]
. For a photon observed in direction
\boldsymbol{n}
and emitted at comoving distance
r_{\mathrm{s}}
, with comoving (gauge-invariant) velocity
\boldsymbol{V}
the
GNC
reads

\displaystyle\Delta(z,\boldsymbol{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{D_{\rho}}{\bar{\rho}}-3(1+w)\Phi+\Phi+\Psi+{\mathcal{H}}\left(\Phi^{\prime}-\partial_{r}(\boldsymbol{V}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\right)

(6)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}{{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}+\frac{2}{r_{\mathrm{s}}{\mathcal{H}}}\right)\left(\Psi-\boldsymbol{V}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\int_{0}^{r_{\mathrm{s}}}\;\Phi^{\prime}+\Psi^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\right)

(7)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{r_{\mathrm{s}}}\int_{0}^{r_{\mathrm{s}}}\;\left(2-\frac{r_{\mathrm{s}}-r}{r}\Delta_{\Omega}\right)(\Phi+\Psi)\,,

(8)

where the integrals are performed over the unperturbed path and
w
is the barotropic index of the background fluid.

2
Invariance of the Number Counts

1
Jordan frame

The expression given by Eq. (
2
) holds in the Jordan frame, as again value of the reference length is constant, hence it is not necessary to
convert
any quantities. The
GNC
in the Jordan frames reads

\displaystyle\tilde{\Delta}(z,\boldsymbol{n})=\tilde{\delta}_{z}(z,\boldsymbol{n})+\frac{\tilde{\delta V}(z,\boldsymbol{n})}{\tilde{V}(z)}\,.

(9)

2
Einstein frame

Expression for the Number Counts

Things get a little bit more complicated in the Einstein frame, where the reference length does not have a constant value. We want to compute the redshift energy perturbation in this frame. To evaluate
\rho(z,\boldsymbol{n})
, we need to think about the definition of the energy density. In theory, one should count the total mass of the galaxies and divide it by the physical volume. However, the mass having the dimension of an inverse length, their value has to be computed using the reference length as

M(z,\boldsymbol{n})=N(z,\boldsymbol{n})\ell(z,\boldsymbol{n})^{-1}m_{0}\,,

(10)

where we assumed that all the galaxies have constant mass
m_{0}
is a constant mass in the Jordan frame. This assumption can be relaxed and does not change the final result. Then, in the Einstein frame, the redshift perturbation density reads

\displaystyle\delta_{z}(z,\boldsymbol{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho(z,\boldsymbol{n})-\rho_{0}(z)}{\rho_{0}(z)}=\frac{\frac{N(z,\boldsymbol{n})\ell(z,\boldsymbol{n})^{-1}}{V(z,\boldsymbol{n})}-\frac{N_{0}(z)\ell_{0}(z)^{-1}}{V(z)}}{\frac{N_{0}(z)\ell_{0}(z)^{-1}}{V(z)}}\,.

(11)

Now, as pointed out in Remark
2.6
and shown in Fig.
1
, we can write the reference length as

\ell(z,\boldsymbol{n})=\ell_{0}(z_{0})+\delta\ell(z,\boldsymbol{n})=\ell_{0}(z)-\delta z\frac{\mathrm{d}\ell_{0}}{\mathrm{d}z_{0}}+\delta\ell(z,\boldsymbol{n})\,,

(12)

where we used the definition of the redshift perturbation Eq. (
84
). The next steps follow the same logic and the final result is

\displaystyle\Delta(z,\boldsymbol{n})=\delta_{z}(z,\boldsymbol{n})+\frac{\delta V(z,\boldsymbol{n})}{V(z)}-\frac{\delta z(z,\boldsymbol{n})}{\ell_{0}(z)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\ell_{0}}{\mathrm{d}z_{0}}+\frac{\delta\ell(z,\boldsymbol{n})}{\ell_{0}(z)}

(13)

Now that we obtained the expression for the
GNC
in the Einstein frame, what should be done is to show that it is indeed equal to the
GNC
in the Jordan frame, by computing explicitly the relationship between the redshift energy perturbation and the volume perturbation in both frames. Before doing so, we state and proof a cute theorem which will prove valuable for these tasks.

A little theorem

Theorem 2.1
(
Relative perturbations in the Einstein frame
)
–

Let us consider a quantity
f(z,\boldsymbol{n})
whose length dimension is
n
. We define its redshift perturbation in the Einstein frame as

\frac{\delta f}{f}\equiv\frac{\delta f(z,\boldsymbol{n})}{f_{0}(z)}=\frac{f(z,\boldsymbol{n})-f_{0}(z)}{f_{0}(z)}\,,

(14)

where
f_{0}(z)
is the average value of the function at redshift
z
, and equivalently in the Jordan frame.

Then, the quantities in both frames are related as

\frac{\delta f}{f}=\frac{\delta\tilde{f}}{\tilde{f}}-n\frac{\delta z}{\ell_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\ell_{0}}{\mathrm{d}z_{0}}+n\frac{\delta\ell}{\ell_{0}}\,.

(15)

Proof.

The proof follows the same lines as the computation of the redshift density perturbation
\delta_{z}(z,\boldsymbol{n})
above. The value of the function in both frames are related as

\displaystyle f(z,\boldsymbol{n})

\displaystyle=\tilde{f}(z,\boldsymbol{n})\ell(z,\boldsymbol{n})^{n}\,,

(16)

\displaystyle f_{0}(z)

\displaystyle=\tilde{f}_{0}(z_{0})\ell_{0}(z)^{n}\,.

(17)

Using again that

\ell(z,\boldsymbol{n})=\ell_{0}(z_{0})+\delta\ell(z,\boldsymbol{n})

(18)

and

\delta z=z-z_{0}\,,

(19)

the result can be shown directly.

∎

Redshift density perturbation

The redshift density perturbation corresponds to a quantity with length dimension
n=-4
. Using the Theorem
2.1
, we obtain

\delta_{z}=\tilde{\delta}_{z}+4\frac{\delta z}{\ell_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\ell_{0}}{\mathrm{d}z_{0}}-4\frac{\delta\ell}{\ell_{0}}\,.

(20)

This relation can also be derived explicitly relating
\delta_{z}
to
\delta\rho
and using the dictionary for the perturbations given by Eq. (
80
), the definition of the redshift involving the reference lengths Eq. (
67
) and the dictionary for the Hubble constant Eq. (
49
). This derivation does not contain any major difficulties, and more details can be found in the article.

Volume perturbation

The physical volume has length dimension
n=3
and the Theorem
2.1
reads in this case

\frac{\delta V}{V}=\frac{\delta\tilde{V}}{\tilde{V}}-3\frac{\delta z}{\ell_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\ell_{0}}{\mathrm{d}z_{0}}+3\frac{\delta\ell}{\ell_{0}}\,.

(21)

Again, this relation can be derived in a more detailed fashion which explained in the article and in
[
24
]
(mostly in Section II B.). We again encourage the interested reader to have a look at the specific parts and we won’t go into more details.

3
Comparison

We now have all the ingredients in our hands to compare the expressions of the
GNC
in both frames. Combining the dictionary for the redshift perturbation Eq. (
20
) and for the volume perturbation Eq. (
21
), we get

\delta_{z}+\frac{\delta V}{V}=\tilde{\delta}_{z}V+\frac{\delta\tilde{V}}{V}+\frac{\delta z}{\ell_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\ell_{0}}{\mathrm{d}z_{0}}-\frac{\delta\ell}{\ell_{0}}=\tilde{\Delta}(z,\boldsymbol{n})+\frac{\delta z}{\ell_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\ell_{0}}{\mathrm{d}z_{0}}-\frac{\delta\ell}{\ell_{0}}\,,

(22)

where we used the definition of the
GNC
in the Jordan frame Eq. (
2
). Comparing this result with the expression of the
GNC
in the Einstein frame Eq. (
13
), we see directly that

\displaystyle\tilde{\Delta}(z,\boldsymbol{n})={\Delta}(z,\boldsymbol{n})\,,

(23)

or in other words, the value of the
GNC
is frame-independent! To make a link with the definition of physical observables presented in Chapter
2
, here the
GNC
is an observable, i.e.
\Delta=\mathcal{O}
, while a change of frame is one of the transformations
\mathcal{T}
we introduced. The physical quantity such as
\delta V
or
\rho_{z}
are elements of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}
which do transform under
\mathcal{T}
.

Remark 2.7
(
Disformal transformations
)
–

Regarding disformal transformations (see Section
4
), we showed in
[
95
]
that the number count is also invariant in these cases. The proof goes along the same lines, the only difference is that time is also modified, and new terms should be considered. In the end, as we did here, all the new terms cancel out in the final expression.

Chapter 7
Conclusion

In this Part, we introduced the central notion of conformally related frames. These are metrics who differ only by a position-dependent global multiplication. We argue that a change of frame is equivalent to a local change of units, where the reference length of a given physical process depends on space and time, as was already done in
[
48
,
57
]
.

We defined the two most important frames, the Einstein frames in which gravity is not coupled to the scalar field, but it non minimally coupled to the matter fields, and the Jordan frame, where the gravity and the scalar field are coupled, but where the matter is minimally coupled to gravity. We frames the discussion in the more general context of
ST
theories, inspired for instance by
[
84
,
188
]
.

In a second part, we studied the Number Counts, first introduced in
[
24
]
. We presented this observable and provided some physical interpretation. The most important part of this work is the proof that the Number Counts does not depend on the frame. This was expected, but this work is a safety check to convince the community that the Einstein and the Jordan frames are indeed equivalent.

Additional works were made on the topic. For example, a new observable is defined in
[
147
]
where the Number Counts observable is defined with a redshift-dependent weight. In
[
87
,
86
]
, they proceed to a Hamiltonian analysis in conformal frames in a Cosmological context. Finally, in
[
95
]
, we showed that the invariance of the Number Counts also holds if one considers the more general disformal transformations. Applying a disformal transformation to a Cosmological background modify not only the length scale, but also the time scale. The derivation is slightly different, but the logic behind is actually very fun.

In a future work, one should be able to show the invariance under conformal transformations of more physical observables. Even better would be a convincing proof that the frames are equivalent (or, why not, a counter example to show that they are not). Even if the debate does not seem to take a lot of attention, it is still worth noting that it is not closed yet.

PART 3 Rotation from Lensing

Black Holes Tormenting Galaxies
, Juan Manuel García Arcos

Chapter 8
General Introduction

In this Part, I present two articles that Giulia, Ruth and I wrote
[
80
,
81
]
. We started this project in August 2020, after the first lockdown. It somehow refueled my motivation after this weird semester.

This part is about gravitational weak lensing. Lensing is one of the main effects in General Relativity and Cosmology. It occurs as light travels through a curved spacetime, e.g. due to the presence of matter (luminous and dark). Being able to quantify the lensing undergone by the signals we measure on Earth is a very powerful tool to probe the content of the Universe, e.g. between us and a far away galaxy. In practice, lensing has a very small effect on individual sources. A statistical approach is then necessary to extract meaningful quantities, and reduce the noise. For instance, estimating angular correlation functions on various physical quantities is a powerful tool to estimate the values of Cosmological parameters, and in fine to constrain various Cosmological models, in particular
LCDM
.

Lensing is mathematically described by the Jacobi map which itself depends on the second derivative of the lensing potential
\phi
, a redshift-dependent function defined on the celestial sphere. More explicitly, it is the integral of the Weyl potential along the line of sight between the observer and a given source of light. Its value is parametrised by
3
quantities: The convergence, the net rotation and the shear. Lensing is a broad field of research and it would not be possible to give a thorough list of references. Nice reviews are for instance
[
178
,
163
,
100
]
. For more specific examples, see
[
135
]
for a numerical simulation (including vector perturbations),
[
55
]
for an application to
CMB
lensing,
[
206
]
for an application to cosmic strings and
[
4
,
51
]
where lensing of gravitational waves is considered.

The convergence is related to the diagonal part of the Jacobi map. Theoretical predictions estimating the convergence from the Number Counts are presented in
[
151
,
134
,
155
]
and numerical simulations are found in
[
181
,
140
]
. On the other side, the net rotation is the antisymmetric part of the Jacobi map. Typically, it is a second order effect and is neglected in the present work, see for example
[
202
,
75
]
.

Shear measurements are in general difficult. Their effect on the shape of the galaxy is small and these correlation functions are affected by intrinsic alignment of galaxies
[
14
,
104
,
124
]
. However, various large-scale projects, e.g. KiDs and DES, have already been done
[
8
,
63
,
3
,
64
]
. These surveys will provide data, which motivate the necessity to study shear correlation functions from a theoretical point of view. Several authors have presented methods to compute shear correlation functions, see for example
[
103
,
122
,
14
,
177
]
. In this work, the goal is to present a new method to build an estimator for shear correlation functions using galaxy orientation and light polarisation.

It is a well-known result that light polarisation is parallel transported, while galaxy shape is Lie transported
[
163
]
, which is correctly described by the Jacobi formalism. It has been shown that the emitted polarisation at the galaxy location is generally aligned with the intrinsic shape of the galaxy (in particular, as it will be explained, it is aligned with the semi-minor axis), see
[
91
]
. In our first paper
[
80
]
, we showed that if the main axes of the shear are not aligned with the main axes of the galaxy, lensing will induce a slight misalignment between the polarisation vector and the shape of the galaxy. This angle difference is a genuine signature of lensing. In particular, it is a probe of cosmic shear.

Polarisation has already been used in the past to probe gravitational lensing. For example, in
[
127
,
129
,
32
]
, the angle between the polarisation vector and the main axes of a galaxy is used to estimate the mass of a foreground galaxy using light emitted by distant quasars. In
[
27
]
, they propose a method to use polarisation to build maps of Dark Matter distribution. In
[
194
]
, they use polarisation to estimate the net rotation part of the Jacobi map. In
[
28
,
33
]
, it is argued that polarisation measurements can mitigate the shot-noise, while in the series or articles
[
205
,
203
,
204
,
202
,
194
]
, a method to estimate cosmic shear using the intrinsic alignment of galaxy shapes is proposed. Finally, in
[
101
]
, the authors propose to use radio surveys to study weak lensing, but the polarisation is not really considered.

Our approach is similar to what has been done, yet it brings some novelties. We consider the rotation angle described above to build an estimator for the shear correlation functions, see our second article
[
81
]
. This has not been done so far. Using polarisation information, together with observed galaxy orientation and morphology, and considering two couples of galaxies, we show how to build an estimator of the coordinate-independent shear correlation functions.

This Part is structured as follows. This introduction is Chapter
8
. We present the main theoretical tools in Chapter
9
, the most important being the derivation of the rotation angle. In Chapter
10
we discuss two applications of our formalism: A toy-model where lensing is only due to Schwarzschild lenses, and the Cosmological setup where we build an estimator for the shear correlation functions. We conclude the discussion in Chapter
11
.

Chapter 9
Theoretical tools

In this Chapter we present the theoretical framework to study lensing. In the first Section, we discuss two ways to transport vectors from one spacetime point to another: The Parallel Transport and the Lie Transport. These concepts are closely related to the Jacobi formalism presented in Section
3
. Much more can be found on the topic, for example in the reviews of Bartelmann
[
14
]
or Perlick
[
163
]
as well as the thesis of Pierre Fleury
[
79
]
(especially Chapter 2), a work of excellent overall quality!

In the second Section, we present the physical assumptions of our model regarding the properties of the galaxy, especially their shape and the orientation of the polarisation of the light they emit.

In the third Section, we define a genuine observable to quantify the rotation of the shape of a galaxy due to lensing (and in particular to shear).

Menu of the Chapter

1
How to Transport Vectors?

1
The Screen

We discuss here in more details the parallel transport of vectors, whose formal definition is given by Eq. (
30
). The parallel transport is of great relevance in General Relativity as the
4
-velocity of any particle (could it be massive or massless) is parallel transported along itself. In other words, in this theory, trajectories of particles follow geodesics. In the context of lensing, the vector we consider is the photon’s
4
-velocity
\boldsymbol{k}
. The parallel transport condition implies

\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{k}=0\,.

(1)

We want however to be able to describe lensing as modification of the shape of a bundle of light (corresponding for example to a galaxy). In order to do so, we need a way to
print
this shape on a
screen
seen by an
observer
. We would like to define those concepts precisely, but before doing so it is insightful to give a simple example.

Let us consider an observer at rest in Minkowski spacetime in Cartesian coordinates. Their
4
-velocity is given by

\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathrm{o}}=\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}\,.

(2)

They observe a galaxy, whose image is made of several photons. Among them, they chose a reference one (say at the center of the galaxy). This photon comes from the
-\boldsymbol{z}
direction and its trajectory and
4
-velocity are

\displaystyle x^{\mu}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\omega(\lambda,0,0,\lambda)\,,

(3)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}

\displaystyle=\omega(\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\partial}_{z})\,,

(4)

where
\omega
is the frequency of the photon as measured by the observer and
\lambda
an affine parameter such that
x(\lambda_{\mathrm{o}})
is the position of the observer. Along this trajectory, we define a set of fictitious observers with
4
-velocity

\boldsymbol{u}(\lambda)=\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}\,.

(5)

Note that, at the observer position, the
4
-velocity
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathrm{o}}
and the fictitious velocity are equal, i.e.

\boldsymbol{u}(\lambda_{\mathrm{o}})=\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}=\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathrm{o}}\,.

(6)

At each point, we would like to define a screen on which the set of fictitious observers could print the shape of the galaxy centered on the reference photon. Which properties should such screens have? Relevant elements are the following.

•

The screen should be a
2
-dimensional spatial surface.

•

It should be in the
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
-plane to be orthogonal to the photon’s trajectory.

•

It should be spanned by basis vectors whose norm does not change along the trajectory.

The first and second conditions agree with the intuitive definition of a screen (How is the screen placed when you go to the movie theater?). The third definition can be intuitively understood. Indeed, if we want to make comparisons between the shapes on the different screens, it makes sense to ensure that the basis vectors do not change size. From these conditions, the only possibility is to assume that the screen is spanned by the two basis vectors (up to a global rotation)

(\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2})=(\boldsymbol{\partial}_{x},\boldsymbol{\partial}_{y})\,.

(7)

At each event of the photon’s trajectory
x^{\mu}(\lambda)
, a point on the screen (for example the position of a given photon) is given by its coordinates
(\alpha^{1},\alpha^{2})
in the
(\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2})
basis. Using this specific example, we are now able to give a more general definition of a screen.

Definition 3.1
(
Screen
)
–

Given a photon’s trajectory
x(\lambda)
with its
4
-velocity
\boldsymbol{k}
and an observer at
x^{\mu}(\lambda_{\mathrm{o}})
with
4
-velocity
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathrm{o}}
, a
screen
is a set of fictitious observers with
4
-velocity
\boldsymbol{u}(\lambda)
along the photon trajectory such that
\boldsymbol{u}(\lambda_{\mathrm{o}})=\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathrm{o}}
together with a set of spatial orthonormal basis
(\boldsymbol{e}_{1}(\lambda),\boldsymbol{e}_{2}(\lambda))
. Moreover, each orthonormal basis must be defined such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(8)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{k}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(9)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{e}_{a}

\displaystyle\propto\boldsymbol{k}\,,

(10)

where it is understood that these three conditions should hold at each point of the trajectory.

The first condition ensures that the screen is a spatial surface (as the vector
\boldsymbol{u}
somehow represents the time coordinate of the observers). The third condition guarantees that the norm of the basis vectors do not change (the proof is direct using the second relation).

Remark 3.1
(
Quasi-parallel transport
)
–

In Definition
3.1
, the last condition defines the
quasi parallel transport
. It is not possible, in general, for the vectors
\boldsymbol{e}_{a}
to be purely parallel transported along
\boldsymbol{k}
. However, such a strong condition is not necessary and we present the most general definition. Note also that the
4
-velocities of the fictitious observes
\boldsymbol{u}(\lambda)
must not be parallel transported. More on these details are found in
[
79
]
.

Remark 3.2
(
Sachs basis
)
–

The orthonormal basis
(\boldsymbol{e}_{1}(\lambda),\boldsymbol{e}_{2}(\lambda))
is called the
Sachs basis
and the screen it spans is called the
Sachs screen
.

The condition on the basis vector to be quasi-parallel transported is very useful. Indeed, recall that the polarisation vector of light is parallel transported along the trajectory, see for example
[
55
]
. This implies that, when studying lensing of galaxy shape, using the polarisation vectors as basis vectors is a natural choice as their transport properties are known. We represent schematically in Fig.
1
the trajectory of a photon and the Sachs formalism.

Figure 1:

The Sachs screen

The trajectory of the photon is shown in black. At two positions, the screen is represented, together with the orthonormal Sachs basis, represented by the arrows. On the screen, one can
print
the shape of the galaxy as it would be seen by a fictitious observer. The images of, for example, a galaxy are
printed
in orange on the screens, see next Section for more details.

2
Lie Transport

We discussed the Sachs formalism which allows us to define a screen, together with a parallel transported basis. We also defined fictitious observers at each point on the trajectory, together with such a screen. Let us assume that the light was emitted by a galaxy. At each point, those observers could project the shape of the galaxy on the screen. We would like to understand how this shape changes along the photon’s trajectory.

The light emitted by the galaxy is made from a bundle of photons. Each of these photons follows a null geodesic. However, because all of these photons are travelling on different spacetime points, we expect the deflection of these trajectories to be different. We can describe this qualitatively using the theory of
geodesic deviation
.

Let us consider a bundle of travelling neighbouring photons. One of them is arbitrarily chosen to be the reference one, with trajectory
x_{\mathrm{ref}}^{\mu}(\lambda)
and
4
-velocity
\boldsymbol{k}_{\mathrm{ref}}
. For another fixed ray with trajectory
x^{\mu}(\lambda)
and trajectory
\boldsymbol{k}
, we define the deviation vector as

\xi^{\mu}=x^{\mu}-x_{\mathrm{ref}}^{\mu}\,.

(11)

Note that
\boldsymbol{\xi}
can indeed be seen as a
4
-vector on the tangent space at
x_{\mathrm{ref}}^{\mu}(\lambda)
as the two rays are considered very close to each other, see Fig
2
. We can determine the acceleration of the vector
\boldsymbol{\xi}
, see
[
80
]
for more details. The final result is

\displaystyle\ddot{\xi}^{\alpha}=-\mathchoice{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.406pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.406pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.14456pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81754pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\beta\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.406pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.406pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.14456pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81754pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\beta\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.406pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.406pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.14456pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81754pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\beta\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.406pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.406pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.14456pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81754pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\beta\nu}}}}k^{\mu}\xi^{\beta}k^{\nu}\,,

(12)

where a dot indicates here a derivative with respect to the affine parameter
\lambda
. This relation is the geodesics deviation equation and it provides a vivid interpretation of the Riemann tensor. We can also show directly that it implies that the Lie Derivative (see Section
2
) of the deviation vector vanishes along the geodesics, i.e.

\displaystyle\pounds_{\boldsymbol{k}}{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=0\,.

(13)

In other words, the deviation vector is Lie transported along the geodesics. This relation is very important for us as it describes how the shape of a light bundle is deformed when it travels through a curved spacetime.

Figure 2:

Deviation vector

The reference ray is shown in black and its
4
-velocity in blue, while an arbitrary ray is shown in red. The Sachs screen are shown in pink. The deviation vector for two different affine parameters is shown in purple. Image inspired from
[
79
]
.

3
Comparison with the Jacobi Formalism

In the two previous Sections, we have seen two important concepts. First, we defined a screen along the trajectory of light. Second, we stated the geodesics deviation equation describing how the shape of a bundle of light is deformed as it travels through spacetime. We would like to relate those two concepts together and relate them with the Jacobi formalism presented in Section
3
.

Recall the Jacobi map
\boldsymbol{A}
was defined through

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{e}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}\,,

(14)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1-\kappa-\gamma_{1}&-\gamma_{2}-\omega\\
-\gamma_{2}+\omega&1-\kappa+\gamma_{1}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(15)

Note that this is the more general expression of the Jacobi matrix (at linear order), where a global (counterclockwise) rotation by an angle
\omega
is also included. In the relation given by Eq. (
14
),
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{e}}
(resp.
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}
) quantifies the position of a given ray at the emission (resp. observer) position. Those positions are defined with respect to the Screen basis.

For example, in the Cosmological setup of Section
3
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}_{1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\theta}\,,

(16)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}_{2}

\displaystyle=\sin^{-1}\bar{\theta}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\varphi}\,,

(17)

where
(\bar{\theta},\bar{\varphi})
is a reference position, corresponding to the trajectory of the reference light ray. If we consider an observer at rest, it is easy to check that all the conditions in the Definition
3.1
are satisfied. This is nothing else as saying that the
2
-dimensional space spanned by
(\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2})
is a Sachs screen.

However, the choice given by Eq. (
16
) and Eq. (
17
) is just one possibility in this specific Cosmological context. In general, the expression for the Sachs basis can be anything as long as it satisfies the conditions of Definition
3.1
.

In other words, the lensing map given by its most general linear expression Eq. (
15
) together with the relation given by Eq. (
14
) describe how the shape of images is deformed with respect to a parallel transported basis. This transformation can also, as we described in the previous Section, be quantified by the Lie transport of the images. We will explain how the Jacobi map can be useful in Section
1
, but first we would like to give a physical interpretation to the vectors
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{e}}
.

4
Mapping Angles to Distances

In the definition of the Jacobi map Eq. (
14
), the vectors
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{e}}
do not have a clear physical interpretation. We want to discuss here how to give them a better signification.

The vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}
lies on the plane at the observer’s position. A sensible way to define it goes as follows. Again, we should choose a reference light ray. For any other ray, the norm of the vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}
is the angle it forms with the reference ray, and its orientation is simply given by the relative position of the ray with respect to the reference ray. This defines the vectors
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}
without the ambiguity of a global rescaling.

Regarding the vector at the emission
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{e}}
, we define
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathrm{e}}
to be the physical distance between the ray and the reference ray as

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathrm{e}}=(1+\kappa)D_{\mathrm{A}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{e}}\,,

(18)

where
D_{\mathrm{A}}
is a quantity with units of length we would like to determine. Combining the definition of Jacobi map Eq. (
14
) and the definition of
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathrm{e}}
, we get

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathrm{e}}=(1+\kappa)D_{\mathrm{A}}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{e}}\,.

(19)

We assume that, at the observer, the bundle of vectors
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}
form a solid angle
\Omega_{\mathrm{o}}
and that at the emission, the rays form a physical surface of area
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{e}}
. Using the relation between
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathrm{e}}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{e}}
, we obtain at linear order

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{e}}=(1+2\kappa)D_{\mathrm{A}}^{2}|\boldsymbol{A}|\Omega_{\mathrm{o}}\,.

(20)

Using the definition of the Jacobi map given by Eq. (
14
), we have
|\boldsymbol{A}|=1-2\kappa
, which implies

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{e}}=D_{\mathrm{A}}^{2}\Omega_{\mathrm{o}}\,.

(21)

This is exactly the definition for
D_{\mathrm{A}}
of the angular diameter distance presented in Section
6
. This gives a physical interpretation to the length
D_{\mathrm{A}}
. Combining these results yields

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathrm{e}}=\boldsymbol{D}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}\,,

(22)

where we defined the dimensional Jacobi matrix

\boldsymbol{D}=(1+\kappa)D_{\mathrm{A}}\boldsymbol{A}\equiv\bar{D}_{\mathrm{A}}\boldsymbol{A}\,,

(23)

and where
\bar{D}_{\mathrm{A}}
is the non-perturbed angular diameter distance of the source.

Remark 3.3
(
Vocabulary
)
–

In general, the matrix
\boldsymbol{D}
is what people called the
Jacobi matrix
. However, here, we will only need its dimensionless counterpart
\boldsymbol{A}
, which we call the
Jacobi matrix
with a slight vocabulary abuse.

The evolution of the dimensional Jacobi equation (more details can be found in
[
79
,
80
,
163
]
for example) is given by

\displaystyle\ddot{\boldsymbol{D}}=\boldsymbol{R}{\boldsymbol{D}}\,,

(24)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{\mathrm{o}}=0\,,

(25)

\displaystyle\dot{\boldsymbol{D}}_{\mathrm{o}}=-\mathbbm{1}\,,

(26)

where the two last conditions are evaluated at the observer position and
\mathbbm{1}
is the identity matrix. A dot denotes derivation with respect to the future-oriented affine parameter
\lambda
. Recall that
\lambda_{\mathrm{s}}<0
at the source position (and
\lambda_{\mathrm{o}}=0
at the observer), hence the negative sign, as we intuitively want
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}
and
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathrm{e}}
to be in the same direction. The matrix
\boldsymbol{R}
is given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Phi_{00}&0\\
0&\Phi_{00}\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\operatorname{Re}(\Psi_{0})&\mathrm{Im}(\Psi_{0})\\
\mathrm{Im}(\Psi_{0})&\mathrm{Re}(\Psi_{0})\end{array}\right)\,,

with

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}

\displaystyle=-C_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}k^{\nu}k^{\rho}\overline{m}^{\mu}\overline{m}^{\sigma}\,,

(31)

\displaystyle\Phi_{00}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}R_{\mu\nu}k^{\mu}k^{\nu}\,,

(32)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{e}_{1}+\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{e}_{2}}{\sqrt{2}}\,.

(33)

where an overbar indicates complex conjugation,
\boldsymbol{k}
is the photon
4
-momentum,
\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}}}}
is the Ricci tensor and
C_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
the Weyl tensor. The relations Eq. (
24
), Eq. (
25
) and Eq. (
26
) allow in principle to determine completely the Jacobi matrix (if the trajectory of the photon and the Sachs basis are known).

2
Physical Assumptions

1
Shape of Galaxies

We would like to use the shape of galaxies to study lensing (particularly the shear). To this aim, we need to make a few assumptions about the shape of the galaxies.

Our main assumption is that galaxies have an elliptical shape in
3
dimensions. When we observe a galaxy, we actually see its
2
-dimensional projection on the Sachs screen at our position. However, it it intuitively understood that the projection of a
3
-dimensional ellipse on a
2
-dimensional plane is still an ellipse. If the semi-major, respectively semi-minor, axis has size
R
, respectively
r
, the eccentricity of the ellipse is

\varepsilon=\sqrt{1-\frac{1}{a^{2}}}\,,

(34)

where

a=\frac{R}{r}

(35)

is the aspect ratio of the ellipse.

Remark 3.4
(
Eccentricity
)
–

Note that the eccentricity of a given ellipse is invariant under a scaling of the ellipse given by
(r,R)=\lambda(\tilde{r},\tilde{R})
with
\lambda>0
. This will prove useful as we can simply use the observed shape on a screen whose size is much smaller than the size of a galaxy! Moreover, note that
\varepsilon=0
corresponds to a circle while for
\varepsilon\rightarrow\infty
, the ellipse becomes a straight line.

2
Polarisation of Light

We also would like to use the direction of the polarisation of light to extract information about the true shape of the galaxy. This information has already been proposed or used to be an interesting observable, see for example
[
28
,
32
,
143
,
184
,
183
,
203
]
. More precisely, it has been already been mentioned that light polarisation is aligned with the principal axes of the galaxy, see
[
114
,
28
,
27
]
. The explanation is that the dominant source of polarisation is the synchrotron polarisation emitted by the electrons moving because of the magnetic field of the galaxy. The dominant component of this field is in the galactic plane and is in general aligned with the galaxy semi-major axis, see
[
91
]
. The polarisation of the light emitted by the rotating electrons is orthogonal to the magnetic field and to the line of sight, hence it is aligned with the semi-minor axis. Note that we can only consider galaxies where the emitted frequency is around
50\text{\,}\mathrm{GHz}
, as lower frequency light is depolarised due to Faraday rotation
[
128
]
. In
[
189
]
, the observable correlation is explicitly shown, with a typical difference of
5\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
between the two vectors.

Figure 3:

Polarisation of galaxies

The black ellipse represents the galaxy. The magnetic field
\boldsymbol{B}
is aligned with the semi-major axis, while the polarisation vector
\boldsymbol{p}
of the emitted light is aligned with the semi-minor axis.

From now on, we take this observation as granted, and also assume that it holds at the observed level, i.e. that the projected polarisation on the screen is aligned with the projected principal axes of the galaxy shape. Moreover, note that, strictly speaking, the polarisation is defined to be the direction of the electric field. This direction is actually aligned with the semi-minor axis, but this is not relevant as it can be defined up to a rotation of
90\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
.

3
Rotation of Ellipses

1
General idea

We now have everything to explain the main idea of this Chapter. Let us summarise what we learned. First, the shape that we observe in the sky can be mathematically described as a set of points on a
2
-dimensional screen. Second, the axes of this screen have to be parallel transported. Third, the deformation of the shape on this screen with respect to this basis is described by the Jacobi formalism, presented in Section
3
. Fourth, the polarisation of the light is parallel transported and is aligned with the main axes of the galaxy at the emission position.

Lumière propagée et déviée
, Jessica Mascellino

In other words, the polarisation vectors (e.g. the electric and magnetic field) are parallel transported along the light trajectory and can be considered as basis vectors for the screen. At the emission position, these vectors are aligned with the main axes of the galaxy. If there were no lensing, say there is only you and this far away galaxy, the shape of the galaxy will not be modified and will stay the same with respects to the Sachs basis. Hence, at the observed position, the polarisation vectors would still be aligned with the main axes of the galaxy. In a more realistic situation, the Universe is filled with matter between this far away galaxy and the Earth. The shape of the galaxy is Lie transported, which is different from the parallel transported undergone by the polarisation vectors. Hence, at the observed position, the polarisation vector does not coincide anymore with the main axes! Such a discrepancy is a true signature of lensing and this is what we would like to quantitatively study in the next Section.

2
Rotation of the Principal Axis

We would like to study the rotation of the main axes of an ellipse under the Jacobi transformation given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{e}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}\,,

(36)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathrm{e}}

\displaystyle=(1+\kappa)D_{\mathrm{A}}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}\quad\mathrm{with}

(37)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1-\kappa-\gamma_{1}&-\gamma_{2}-\omega\\
-\gamma_{2}+\omega&1-\kappa+\gamma_{1}\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\mathrm{and}

(38)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathrm{e}}

\displaystyle=(1+\kappa)D_{\mathrm{A}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{e}}\,.

(39)

where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{e}}
(resp.
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}
) represent the position of one light on the screen at the emission (resp. observer) position, and
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\mathrm{e}}
is the physical distance vector at the emission position.

Let us take a step back and describe qualitatively what we want. The parameters at hand are the convergence
\kappa
, and the shear
\gamma
together with the angle
\chi
it forms with the Sachs basis, or equivalently its components defined as

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=\gamma\cos 2\chi\,,

(40)

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=\gamma\sin 2\chi\,.

(41)

When we started the project, with Giulia and Ruth, we thought that the relevant quantities was the net rotation
\omega
. However, we soon realised that it vanishes in the Schwarzschild case, and that it is of second order in the Cosmological setup. We were a bit puzzled. Indeed, if there is no net rotation in the Jacobi matrix, does this mean that the image of the galaxy does not rotate neither? If this were true, there would be no hope to observe a rotation of the principal axes with respect to the polarisation direction.

However, we realised soon after that an ellipse can undergo a rotation because of the shear, provided its direction do not coincide with the axes of the ellipse. Indeed, in this situation, the ellipse would be twisted and the position of its axes would rotate! This is shown schematically in Fig.
4
.

Figure 4:

Rotation from shear

The principal axes of the shear do not coincide with the main directions of the observed ellipse (in cyan, full line). The source ellipse (in blue, dashed) is obtained applying the Jacobi map. The semi-major axis is rotated under this process!

We want to describe this quantitatively. We assume that the galaxy shape forms an ellipse at the observer. In other words, the vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}
satisfies

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}^{\mathrm{T}}\boldsymbol{M}_{\mathrm{o}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{o}}=1\,,

(42)

with

\boldsymbol{M}_{\mathrm{o}}=\begin{pmatrix}r_{\mathrm{o}}^{-2}&0\\
0&R_{\mathrm{o}}^{-2}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(43)

It is direct to show that the condition Eq. (
42
) together with Eq. (
43
) represent an ellipse with semi-minor (dimensionless) axis
r_{\mathrm{o}}
in the
\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
direction and semi-major (dimensionless) axis
R_{\mathrm{o}}
in the
\boldsymbol{e}_{2}
direction.

To study the shape of the galaxy at the emission position, we use Eq. (
36
) and plug it into Eq. (
42
) to get

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{T}}\boldsymbol{M}_{\mathrm{e}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathrm{e}}=1\,,

(44)

with

\boldsymbol{M}_{\mathrm{e}}=(\boldsymbol{A}^{-1})^{\mathrm{T}}\boldsymbol{M}_{\mathrm{o}}\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\,.

(45)

The eigensystem of the matrix
\boldsymbol{M}_{\mathrm{e}}
contains all the information about the shape and the orientation of the ellipse at the emission. Generalising the case of a diagonal matrix given by
\boldsymbol{M}_{\mathrm{o}}
, we infer that the eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{M}_{\mathrm{e}}
correspond to the direction of the main axes while the eigenvalues
\lambda_{\pm}
are related to their length via
\lambda_{+}=R_{\mathrm{e}}^{-2}
and
\lambda_{-}=r_{\mathrm{e}}^{-2}
. A more formal proof can be given considering the usual diagonal decomposition of a symmetric matrix under an orthogonal change of coordinates.

At linear order in
\kappa
,
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
, the computations can be done using the standard techniques to determine eigenvalues and eigenvectors. We present only the results here. At the emission, we get

\displaystyle R_{{\mathrm{e}}}

\displaystyle=D_{\mathrm{A}}R_{{\mathrm{o}}}\left(1+\gamma_{1}\right)\,,

(46)

\displaystyle r_{{\mathrm{e}}}

\displaystyle=D_{\mathrm{A}}r_{{\mathrm{o}}}\left(1-\gamma_{1}\right)\,,

(47)

\displaystyle\delta\alpha

\displaystyle=\gamma_{2}\left(\frac{2-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}^{2}}\right)\,,

(48)

where
R_{{\mathrm{e}}}
and
r_{{\mathrm{e}}}
are the main axes of the ellipse tilted with an angle
\delta\alpha
with respect to the Sachs basis, and

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}=\sqrt{1-\frac{r_{\mathrm{o}}^{2}}{R_{\mathrm{o}}^{2}}}

(49)

is the eccentricity at the observer. Moreover, the eccentricity at the emission is

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{e}}=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}+2\gamma_{1}\frac{1-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}}\,.

(50)

Here, we considered an ellipse at the observer whose main axes coincide with the Sachs basis. We can be even more general and assume that the semi-minor axis forms an angle
\beta_{\mathrm{o}}
with the horizontal axes. The formulas can be easily adapted. In this general case, the formulas are

\displaystyle R_{\mathrm{e}}

\displaystyle=D_{\mathrm{A}}R_{\mathrm{o}}(1+\gamma\cos(2\chi-2\beta_{\mathrm{o}}))\,,

(51)

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{e}}

\displaystyle=D_{\mathrm{A}}r_{\mathrm{o}}(1-\gamma\cos(2\chi-2\beta_{\mathrm{o}}))\,,

(52)

\displaystyle\delta\alpha

\displaystyle=\gamma\sin(2\chi-2\beta_{\mathrm{o}})\frac{2-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}^{2}}\,,

(53)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{e}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}+2\gamma\cos(2\chi-2\beta_{\mathrm{o}})\frac{1-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}}\,.

(54)

3
Physical Interpretation

We can give a physical interpretation to the formulas given by Eq. (
51
), Eq. (
52
), Eq. (
53
) and Eq. (
54
).
The first thing to note is that all the formulas only depend on the difference
\chi-\beta_{\mathrm{o}}
. Recall that
\chi
parametrises the orientation of the shear, while
\beta_{\mathrm{o}}
quantifies the orientation of the semi-minor axis with respect to
\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
on the Screen space. When one set of Sachs screen has been chosen along the light trajectory, it is always possible to rotate all the screens by a constant angle. By doing so, the orientation of the shear
\chi
and the position of the observed galaxy
\beta_{\mathrm{o}}
will both be modified, however their difference will be kept constant. Hence, we expect all the observables, such as the eccentricity and the rotation of the main axes, to be invariant under such a change.

Second, we note that if

\chi-\beta_{\mathrm{o}}=n\frac{\pi}{2}\,,

(55)

with
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
\delta\alpha=0
. This condition simply means that the axes of the shear are aligned with the main axes of the galaxy. In such a situation, the galaxy is only distorted, but its direction is not affected, as discussed previously.

The opposite case happens if

\chi-\beta_{\mathrm{o}}=\frac{\pi}{4}+n\frac{\pi}{2}\,.

(56)

In this case, the eccentricity is not affected: The ellipse undergoes a pure rotation and its size is not modified.

One last comment can be made about the area of the ellipse. At the emission, it is given by

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{e}}=\pi R_{\mathrm{e}}r_{\mathrm{e}}=D_{\mathrm{A}}^{2}\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{o}}\,,

(57)

with
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{o}}=\pi R_{\mathrm{o}}r_{\mathrm{o}}
, as expected from the definition of the angular diameter distance.

To conclude this Section, we want to stress that among all the formulas, we will only be interested about the rotation of the main axis of the ellipse
\delta\alpha
. Indeed, as explained above, the polarisation vector is aligned with the shape of the galaxy at the emission. Hence, the difference angle
\delta\alpha
is indeed a physical observable at the observer, provided the polarisation direction can be measured. However, the rotation angle has a non-trivial dependence on the eccentricity which is not well-defined around
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}=0
(corresponding to a circle). It will be more convenient to change slightly its definition.

Definition 3.2
(
Scaled rotation
)
–

We define the
scaled rotation
as

\Theta\equiv\delta\alpha\frac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}^{2}}{2-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}^{2}}\,.

(58)

It can be shown that its error is well-defined for an ellipse with
\varepsilon\approx 0
, see
[
80
]
for more details. Moreover, note that the scaled rotation is a true observable:
\delta\alpha
can be measured as explained above, and the eccentricity
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}
can be determined if the ratio of the axes is known, which is certainly possible if one sees the shape of the galaxy. Using some trigonometric identities, and the definition of the shear components, we obtain the final formula for the scaled rotation

\displaystyle\Theta=\gamma_{2}\cos 2\beta_{\mathrm{o}}-\gamma_{1}\sin 2\beta_{\mathrm{o}}\,.

(59)

The expression for the rotation of an ellipse given by Eq. (
59
) is the main relation of the paper. This relation will become very important in Section
2
when we use it in a Cosmological context to study the statistical properties.

Chapter 10
Applications

In this Chapter we present two applications of the results derived previously, especially the formula for the scaled rotation. In the first Section, we apply the formalism to the Schwarzschild setup, where we can compute the shear at linear order in the mass of the lens. We present various toy models in which we estimate the statistics of the scaled rotation for different redshifts of the source, with a foreground constituted of a distribution of several Schwarzschild lenses.

In the second Section, we turn our attention to a cosmological setup to refine this model. The scaled rotation is a physical observable allowing us to probe the cosmic shear between the observer and the source. We build an observable to compute the shear correlation function and discuss the number of galaxies needed to obtain a final result with an acceptable signal-to-noise ratio.

Menu of the Chapter

1
Schwarzschild Lens

1
Setup

We consider a Schwarzschild lens, whose metric is given in the usual Spherical Coordinates by

\boldsymbol{g}=-\left(1-\frac{2m}{r}\right)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}+\left(1-\frac{2m}{r}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+r^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\Omega^{2}\,.

(1)

We defined
m=G_{\mathrm{N}}M
,
M
being the mass of the lens. The Schwarzschild radius is given by
2m
. The lens could be for example a supermassive Black Hole, or a whole galaxy.

We consider the setup shown in Fig.
1
, taken from
[
80
]
. The observer sees a galaxy (or a star) at
S^{\prime}
, while the
true
position is at
S
. The
4
-momentum of the photon is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{E}{1-\frac{2m}{r}}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}+\dot{r}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{r}+\frac{L}{r^{2}}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\varphi}\,,

(2)

\displaystyle\dot{r}^{2}

\displaystyle=E^{2}-\frac{L^{2}}{r^{2}}\left(1-2\frac{m}{r}\right)\,,

(3)

where
E
is the dimensionless energy and
L
its angular momentum, both of which are conserved. Here, a dot denotes a derivative with respect to an affine parameter
\lambda
. As usual, we set
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
. The distance between the observer and the lens is
r_{{\mathrm{o}}}
, and
\zeta
is the angle between the lens direction and the incoming direction, as seen by the observer. The impact parameter of the unperturbed trajectory with the same angle
\zeta
is
b
, while the
true
impact parameter is
r_{\mathrm{min}}
. The angle
\zeta
is given by

\sin\zeta=\frac{b}{r_{\mathrm{o}}}\sqrt{1-2\frac{m}{r_{\mathrm{o}}}}\approx\frac{b}{r_{\mathrm{o}}}\,.

(4)

We consider a family of static observers along the trajectory whose
4
-velocities are

\boldsymbol{u}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{2m}{r}}}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}\,.

(5)

The impact parameter is given by (see for example
[
168
]
for more details)

r_{\textrm{min.}}=b\left(1-\frac{m}{b}+\mathcal{O}\left[\left(\frac{m}{b}\right)^{2}\right]\right).

(6)

Figure 1:

Lensing around a Schwarzschild Lens

Left:
The position of the star is
S
and its apparent position is
S^{\prime}
.

Right:
The Sachs basis is
(\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2})
and
\boldsymbol{k}^{\mathrm{sp.}}
is the spatial part of the
4
-momentum.

Figures taken from
[
80
]
.

2
The Jacobi map

We would like now to compute the Jacobi map at linear order in the parameter
m/b
. In other words, we assume that the light is travelling sufficiently far from the lens. For example, if we assume that the Schwarzschild lens is a foreground galaxy, we require that the light emitted by a background galaxy does not pass close to the foreground one. This is certainly a good assumption: If this would not be the case, the background galaxy could not even be observed.

The Sachs basis is given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}_{1}

\displaystyle=r^{-1}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\theta}\,,

(7)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}_{2}

\displaystyle=\rho_{r}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{r}+\rho_{\varphi}\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\varphi}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{with}

(8)

\displaystyle\rho_{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{b(2m-r)}{r^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{and}

(9)

\displaystyle\rho_{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{r}\sqrt{1-\frac{b^{2}}{r^{2}}+\frac{2mb^{2}}{r^{3}}}\,.

(10)

It is straightforward to check that these choices satisfy the conditions given in Definition
3.1
. The first Sachs vector is quite easy to guess as the trajectory lies in the Equatorial plane. The Ansatz for the second vector is taken to ensure
\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{2}=0
and the specific values for
\rho_{t}
and
\rho_{\varphi}
are found using
\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{k}=0
and
\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{2}=1
. The Sachs basis is shown in Fig.
1
.

We now determine the dimensional Jacobi matrix At zeroth order in the parameter
m/b
, it is direct to shot that the solution of Eq. (
24
), Eq. (
25
) and Eq. (
26
) is

\boldsymbol{D}=-\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&\lambda\end{pmatrix}\,,

(11)

as at this order the spacetime is flat and
\Psi_{0}=\Phi_{00}=0
. At linear order, the matrix
\boldsymbol{R}
can be explicitly computed. Note that, as
\Psi_{0}
and
\Phi_{00}
are already first order quantities, it is sufficient to take the background values for
\boldsymbol{k}
and
\boldsymbol{e}_{a}
in Eq. (
31
) ans Eq. (
32
), i.e. setting
m=0
in Eq. (
2
) and Eq. (
8
). The final result is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{0}\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\mathrm{with}

(12)

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{3mb^{2}}{r^{5}}\,.

(13)

Due to the symmetry of the matrix
\boldsymbol{R}
, we pose the following Ansatz for the dimensional Jacobi matrix

\boldsymbol{D}=-\begin{pmatrix}\lambda+mf^{(1)}&0\\
0&\lambda-mf^{(1)}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(14)

The function
f^{(1)}
satisfies the following differential equation

\displaystyle m\ddot{f}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=-\lambda\Psi_{0}\,,

(15)

\displaystyle f^{(1)}_{0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(16)

\displaystyle\dot{f}^{(1)}_{0}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(17)

As the function
f^{(1)}
is a first-order perturbation, it is sufficient to use the background solution for
r
in the expression for
\Psi_{0}
. It is given by

r(\lambda)=\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+r_{\mathrm{o}}^{2}+2\lambda\sqrt{r_{\mathrm{o}}^{2}-b^{2}}}\,,

(18)

which is nothing else as the equation of a straight line starting at
r_{\mathrm{o}}
and with impact parameter
b
. The equation for
f^{(1)}
Eq. (
15
) can be solved analytically but the full solution does not give any physical insight. We will assume that the impact parameter is much small than the distances at hand

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\ll r_{\mathrm{s}}\,,

(19)

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\ll r_{\mathrm{o}}\,,

(20)

where
r_{\mathrm{s}}
is the distance between the lens and the source. In this regime, the solutions reads

mf^{(1)}(\lambda)=-m\frac{4r_{\mathrm{o}}}{b^{2}}(r_{\mathrm{o}}+\lambda)+\mathcal{O}(b^{2})\,.

(21)

Recalling the decomposition of the dimensional Jacobi matrix Eq. (
23
), we get

\boldsymbol{D}=-\lambda\begin{pmatrix}(1-\gamma)&0\\
0&(1+\gamma)\end{pmatrix}\,,

(22)

where
-\lambda_{\mathrm{s}}>0
is the angular diameter distance to the source (recall
\lambda<0
between the source and the observer). Comparing this expression with the Ansatz Eq. (
14
) yields to the final expression for the shear

\displaystyle\gamma=-m\frac{f^{(1)}(\lambda_{\mathrm{s}})}{\lambda_{\mathrm{s}}}\approx\frac{4mr_{\mathrm{o}}}{b^{2}}\left(\frac{r_{\mathrm{s}}}{r_{\mathrm{o}}+r_{\mathrm{s}}}\right)\,,

(23)

where we used that, at the background level,
\lambda_{\mathrm{s}}=-(r_{\mathrm{o}}+r_{\mathrm{s}})
. Note that all the other quantities vanish:
\kappa=\chi=\omega=0
. The fact that the convergence vanishes is a consequence of
\boldsymbol{R}=0
, see for example
[
79
]
. The net rotation
\omega
vanishes by symmetry: If you’re looking at the image with the Black Hole, say, at your right, there is no reason why the galaxy shape would rotate clockwise or counterclockwise. Last, the angle
\chi
vanishes simply because we chose
wisely
our Sachs basis.

Remark 3.5
(
Behaviour of the solution for
b=0
)
–

The final solution for the shear Eq. (
23
) seems to be divergent in the regime
b\approx 0
. However, the weak lensing regimes imposes that we observe rays coming outside the Einstein radius of the lens

\zeta\gg\theta_{\mathrm{E}}\,,

(24)

where

\theta_{\mathrm{E}}=\sqrt{\frac{4mr_{\mathrm{s}}}{r_{\mathrm{o}}(r_{\mathrm{o}}+r_{\mathrm{s}})}}

(25)

is the Einstein angle. Using the expression for
\zeta
Eq. (
4
) and for the shear Eq. (
23
), we get

\gamma=\left(\frac{\theta_{\mathrm{E}}}{\zeta}\right)^{2}\ll 1

(26)

under our assumption.

3
Statistical Analysis

General Setup

In this Section, we present a cute toy-model to show how the result derived above can be useful in a more realistic situation. Recall that the scaled rotation (in absolute value) is by
1
1

1

Here we adopt the subscript for
source
instead of
emitted
as it is more adapted to the context.

\Theta=|\delta\alpha|\frac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}^{2}}{2-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}^{2}}=|\delta\alpha|\frac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}}{2-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}}\,,

(27)

where the last equality holds as
\delta\alpha
is already a first order quantity, and we have at this order
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{o}}=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{s}}
. The rotation angle
\delta\alpha
is given by Eq. (
53
). Combining these relations with the result for the shear Eq. (
23
) yields (using
\gamma=\gamma_{1}
as
\chi=0
)

\Theta=\frac{4m}{b^{2}}\frac{D_{\ell}D_{\ell\textrm{s}}}{D_{\textrm{s}}}|\sin 2\beta_{\mathrm{s}}|\,,

(28)

where
r_{\mathrm{o}}=D_{\ell}
is the distance between the lens and the observer,
r_{\mathrm{s}}=D_{\ell\textrm{s}}
is the distance betwee n the lens and the source and
r_{\mathrm{o}}+r_{\mathrm{s}}=D_{\textrm{s}}
is the distance between the observer and the source. Recall that
\beta_{\mathrm{s}}
is the angle between the semi-minor axis and the first Sachs vector
\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
at the source position. Note that, as before, there is no need to distinguish between
\beta_{\mathrm{o}}
and
\beta_{\mathrm{s}}
as the scaled rotation
\Theta
is already a first order quantity.

We would like now to define some statistical quantities that can be measured. For example, for a given collection of sources at redshift
z_{\mathrm{s}}
(up to a the width of the bin), what is the probability to have
\Theta>\bar{\Theta}
where
\bar{\Theta}
is some arbitrary fixed value? Or what is the PDF of the statistical quantity
\Theta
? We can answer these questions but we need some statistical tools. We give here the main ingredients, and more details can be found in the Appendix
18.A
.

Statistical Ingredients

Let us consider the lens at distance
D_{\ell}
and redshift
z_{\ell}
, and a source at distance
D_{\textrm{s}}
and redshift
z_{\mathrm{s}}
. We define the cross section
\sigma(\Theta,z_{\ell},z_{\mathrm{s}},m)
as the area of the region (projected on the plane perpendicular to the line of sight and passing through the lens) in which the scaled rotation would be bigger than
\Theta
. Its is simply a circle with radius
b
whose area is given by

\sigma(\Theta,z_{\ell},z_{\mathrm{s}},m)=\pi b^{2}=\frac{4\pi m}{\Theta}\frac{D_{\ell}D_{\ell\textrm{s}}}{D_{\textrm{s}}}|\sin 2\beta_{\mathrm{s}}|\,.

(29)

The optical depth for a source at a fixed redshift
z_{\mathrm{s}}
and a scaled rotation of fixed value
\Theta
is

\tau(\Theta,z_{\mathrm{s}})=\mathcal{A}\int_{0}^{z_{\mathrm{s}}}n^{\mathrm{phys}}(z,M)\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{\mathrm{d}z}\sigma(\Theta,z,z_{\mathrm{s}},M)\,\mathrm{d}M\,\mathrm{d}z\,,

(30)

see Appendix
18.A
for more details. We used
m=G_{\mathrm{N}}M
to recover the lens of the mass. The integral spans all the potential lens between the observer (
z=0
) and the source (
z=z_{\mathrm{s}}
). The physical density of galaxies of mass
M
at redshift
z
is given by
n^{\mathrm{phys}}(z,M)
. We also integrated over the orientation of the galaxies using a flat distribution, which gives the constant prefactor

\mathcal{A}=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\;\sin(2\beta_{\mathrm{s}})\,\mathrm{d}\beta_{\mathrm{s}}=\frac{2}{\pi}\,.

(31)

Using the relation between the physical distance
r
and the redshift
z
given by

\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{\mathrm{d}z}=\frac{1}{(1+z)H(z)}\,,

(32)

and the expression for the cross section Eq. (
29
), we get

\tau(\Theta,z_{\mathrm{s}})=\mathcal{A}\int_{0}^{z_{\mathrm{s}}}\frac{1}{(1+z)H(z)}\left(\frac{4\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}M}{\Theta}\frac{D_{\ell}D_{\ell\textrm{s}}}{D_{\textrm{s}}}\right)n^{\mathrm{phys}}(z,M)\,\mathrm{d}M\,\mathrm{d}z\,.

(33)

We can relate the mass
M
of a lens inside its Einstein radius to its velocity dispersion
\sigma_{v}
(see
[
160
]
for example)

M=\frac{4\pi^{2}}{G}\sigma_{v}^{4}\frac{D_{\ell}D_{\ell\textrm{s}}}{D_{\textrm{s}}}\,.

(34)

The density of galaxies using this variable satisfies

n^{\mathrm{phys}}(z,M)\,\mathrm{d}M=(1+z)^{3}n(z,\sigma_{v})\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{v}\,,

(35)

where
n(z,\sigma_{v})
is the comoving number of galaxies (using today’s value as a reference). The factor
(1+z)^{3}
encodes the dilution of galaxies (matter) as the Universe is expanding. Combining these different relations, we can obtain the optical depth, together with the probability density function
p(\Theta,z_{\mathrm{s}})
of the scaled rotation, namely

\displaystyle\tau(\Theta,z_{\mathrm{s}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{A}}{\Theta}\mathcal{J}(z_{\mathrm{s}})\,,

(36)

\displaystyle P(>\Theta,z_{\mathrm{s}})

\displaystyle=1-\mathrm{e}^{-\tau(\Theta,z_{\mathrm{s}})}\,,

(37)

\displaystyle p(\Theta,z_{\mathrm{s}})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial P(>\Theta,z_{\mathrm{s}})}{\partial\Theta}=\frac{\mathcal{A}}{\Theta^{2}}\mathcal{J}(z_{\mathrm{s}})\exp\left(-\frac{\mathcal{A}}{\Theta}\mathcal{J}(z_{\mathrm{s}})\right)\,,\quad\mathrm{with}

(38)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(z_{\mathrm{s}})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{z_{\mathrm{s}}}\frac{16\pi^{3}(1+z)^{2}}{H(z)}\left(\frac{D_{\ell}D_{\ell\textrm{s}}}{D_{\textrm{s}}}\right)^{2}\sigma_{v}^{4}n(z,\sigma_{v})\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{v}\,.

(39)

Models for the Galaxy Number Density

To get some physical insight, we can first take the fitted values for
n(z,\sigma_{v})=n(0,\sigma_{v})
coming from the SDSS observations, see Bernardi
[
19
]
, in which the comoving density of galaxies does not take into account a potential evolution of galaxies and only depends on
\sigma_{v}
, i.e.

n(z,\sigma_{v})=n(\sigma_{v})\,.

(40)

The expression is given by

\displaystyle n(\sigma_{v})

\displaystyle=\phi^{\star}\left(\frac{\sigma_{v}}{\Sigma_{\star}}\right)^{\alpha}\exp\left(-\left(\frac{\sigma_{v}}{\Sigma_{\star}}\right)^{\beta}\right)\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\right)}\frac{\beta}{\sigma_{v}}\,,\quad\mathrm{with}

(41)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\star}

\displaystyle=113.78\mathrm{km\times s^{-1}}\approx 0.0038\,,

(42)

\displaystyle\phi^{\star}

\displaystyle=1.65\times 10^{9}H_{0}^{3}\,,\quad\textrm{and}

(43)

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=(0.94,1.85)\,.

(44)

With the galaxy number density Eq. (
41
), the integral over
\sigma_{v}
in Eq. (
39
) reads

\int_{0}^{\infty}\;\sigma_{v}^{4}n(\sigma_{v})\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{v}=(\Sigma^{\star})^{4}\phi^{\star}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{4+\alpha}{\beta}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\right)}\,.

(45)

The only integral left is

\mathcal{I}(z_{\mathrm{s}})=\int_{0}^{z_{\mathrm{s}}}\;\frac{(1+z)^{2}}{H(z)}\left(\frac{D_{\ell}D_{\ell\textrm{s}}}{D_{\textrm{s}}}\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}z=\int_{0}^{z_{\mathrm{s}}}\;\frac{1}{H(z)}\left(\frac{\chi(0,z)\chi(z,z_{\mathrm{s}})}{\chi(0,z_{\mathrm{s}})}\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}z\,,

(46)

where
\chi(z_{1},z_{2})
is the comoving distance (see Section
6
) from
z_{1}
to
z_{2}
given by

\chi(z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{1}{a_{0}}\int_{z_{1}}^{z_{2}}\;\frac{1}{H(z)}\,\mathrm{d}z\,,

(47)

and

\displaystyle D_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{\chi(0,z)}{1+z}\,,

\displaystyle D_{\ell\textrm{s}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\chi(z,z_{\mathrm{s}})}{1+z_{\mathrm{s}}}\,,

\displaystyle D_{\textrm{s}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\chi(z,z_{\mathrm{s}})}{1+z_{\mathrm{s}}}\,.

(48)

The final expression for the function
\mathcal{J}(z_{\mathrm{s}})
reads

\mathcal{J}(z_{\mathrm{s}})=\frac{16\pi^{3}(\Sigma_{\star})^{4}\phi^{\star}}{H_{0}^{3}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{4+\alpha}{\beta}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\right)}H_{0}^{3}\mathcal{I}(z_{\mathrm{s}})\,.

(49)

The factors of
H_{0}^{3}
were explicitly added to show that the various terms are dimensionless. We can also use another parametrisation for the galaxy number density
n(z,\sigma_{v})
given by the Illustris model, see
[
196
,
51
]
, and Appendix
18.B
for more details. This models can take into account the redshift evolution of galaxy density.

Results

In Fig.
2
we show the PDF
p(\Theta,z_{\mathrm{s}})
for various values of the scaled rotation
\Theta
and source redshift
z_{\mathrm{s}}
. In both cases, it is clear that the overall probability increases with
z_{\mathrm{s}}
, the further the source, the bigger the
opportunity
for the light to undergo a bigger deflection. In Fig.
3
we show the optical depth for the three models (the simple toy model, and the Illustris simulation with and without the redshift evolution). We see that for a small source redshift (up to
z_{\mathrm{s}}\approx 1
), all the models agree at
\sim 10\%
. For higher redshifts, the Illustris without evolution is different from the other two models. It is clear that the final result strongly depends on the model chosen for
n(z,\sigma_{v})
.

Figure 2:

Probability density function

Function of the scaled rotation and the source redshift, computed with the Illustris model taking into account the evolution of the galaxy density.

Left:
PDF for various redshifts. When
z_{\mathrm{s}}
increases, the PDF for large values of
\Theta
also increases as the path of the light takes more time, hence the probability of getting rotated increases.

Right:
PDF for various values of
\Theta
. A low value for
\Theta
corresponds to a higher probability as the cross section for such a rotation is bigger. Note that here too, the PDF is always increasing with
z_{\mathrm{s}}
.

Figure 3:

Optical depth

Comparison of the three models for the galaxy number density (with
\Theta=0.02
fixed)

2
Cosmic shear

1
General Idea

In this Section, we want to apply our result in a cosmological context to estimate the cosmic shear. The cosmic shear is expressed in terms of the derivatives of the lensing potential
\phi(z,\boldsymbol{n})
which is itself a stochastic quantity. This means that its exact value in a specific direction and at a specific redshift cannot be predicted from our cosmological theory. However, statistical properties of the cosmic shear can be predicted from a given model. For example, its expectation value
\langle\phi(z,\boldsymbol{n})\rangle
vanishes in most theories. More interesting, the two-point correlation functions
\langle\phi(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\phi(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle
(or equivalently its power spectrum
C_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})
) has a non-trivial expression which depends on the cosmological model or on cosmological parameters. We want to build an estimator for such a quantity, in order to compare it with theoretical predictions.

The main idea is the following. The shear has two independent components, for example
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
, but other examples are possible. From this quantity, we can build two independent correlation functions:
\zeta_{+}(z_{1},z_{2},\varphi)
and
\zeta_{-}(z_{1},z_{2},\varphi)
which we will define precisely below. They can only depend on the angle between the two directions because the cosmological background is isotropic. Each single galaxy measurement provides one value of the scaled rotation
\Theta
. The (two-point) correlation function of the scaled rotation can be schematically related with the shear correlation function as

\displaystyle\Theta

\displaystyle\sim\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}\,,

(50)

\displaystyle\langle\Theta^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\sim\Lambda_{+}\zeta_{+}+\Lambda_{-}\zeta_{-}\,,

(51)

where
\Theta^{2}
simply means here that we need two galaxies, and the coefficients
\Lambda_{\pm}
are arbitrary functions. To determine completely the independent functions
\zeta_{\pm}
, we need two such observables. In other words, we need two pairs of galaxies, each pairs being separated by a constant angle
\varphi
to determine the two independent correlation functions
\zeta_{\pm}
. The observation of such a pair is shown in Fig.
4
. Note that, in practice, the galaxies are located in bins of a given angular and redshift size.

Figure 4:

Estimation of the correlation function

A couple of galaxies in two bins separated by an angle
\varphi
allows the computation of
\langle\Theta^{2}\rangle
. To such couples are needed to estimate the two correlation functions
\zeta_{+}
and
\zeta_{-}
.

2
Theoretical Results

Coordinate Independent Shear

We want to define here the relevant correlation functions. The first guess would be

\zeta_{ab}(z_{1},z_{2},\mu)\equiv\langle\gamma_{a}(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\gamma_{b}(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle\,,

(52)

with
a,b=1,2
. We use here

\displaystyle\mu\equiv\cos\varphi=\boldsymbol{n}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{2}\,,

(53)

where
\varphi
is the angle between the two pixels. We will use a slight notation abuse and use
\varphi
or
\mu
indifferently in what follows, as one notation or the other may be more convenient.

The components of the shear are given by (see Section
2
and Section
3
)

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(z,\boldsymbol{n})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{1}\nabla_{1}-\nabla_{2}\nabla_{2})\phi\,,

(54)

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}(z,\boldsymbol{n})

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}\phi\,,

(55)

where
\phi
is the lensing potential. It is clear from this definition that the components
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
depend on the coordinate system, as the covariant derivatives are defined with respect to
(\theta,\varphi)
.

It is possible to define a coordinate independent shear, see for example
[
94
]
. The logic goes as follows: The shear can be seen as a rank-
2
tensor given by

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}=\begin{pmatrix}-\gamma_{1}&-\gamma_{2}\\
-\gamma_{2}&\gamma_{1}\,\end{pmatrix}\,,

(56)

whose components depend on the coordinate system. For a direction
\boldsymbol{n}
, one defines an orthonormal basis of the tangent space given by
(\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2})
associated with the coordinates
(\theta,\varphi)
. We define the vector in direction
\alpha
as

\boldsymbol{e}_{\alpha}\equiv\cos\alpha\,\boldsymbol{e}_{1}+\sin\alpha\,\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\,,

(57)

With this notation, the coordinate independent shear is defined as

\displaystyle\gamma_{\boldsymbol{e}_{\alpha}}=\gamma_{\alpha}\equiv\boldsymbol{e}_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{e}_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{T}}=-\gamma_{1}\cos 2\alpha-\gamma_{2}\sin 2\alpha\,.

(58)

This shear is independent of the coordinate system, provided the direction
\boldsymbol{e}_{\alpha}
is given. The independent correlation function can be defined as follows. Let two galaxies be located at
(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})
and
(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})
with
\boldsymbol{n}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{2}=\cos\varphi=\mu
. The great circle connecting them defines an
Equator
, from which the usual Spherical coordinates
(\theta,\varphi)
are easily defined. An illustration is shown in Fig.
5
.

Figure 5:

Coordinate independent shear

The black dot represents the galaxies and the black line is the Equator connecting them. This defines the basis
(\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2})
of the tangent space (purple square). The shear
\gamma_{\alpha}
is computed in the direction of the green arrow, and does not depend on the system of coordinates.

Remark 3.6
(
Ambiguity in the definition
)
–

Note that there is are a ambiguity, as one can define new coordinates
(\tilde{\theta},\tilde{\varphi})=(\pi-\theta,\varphi+\varphi_{0})
where
\varphi_{0}
is an arbitrary constant. For
\varphi
this is not problematic as only consider the angle between galaxies is relevant. For
\theta
, the only effect is to define
\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha+\pi
which leaves invariant
\gamma_{\alpha}
defined by Eq. (
58
).

Coordinate Independent Correlation Functions

When this coordinate system has been defined unambiguously, the invariant correlation functions are defined as

\displaystyle\zeta_{\mathrm{p}}(z_{1},z_{2},\mu)

\displaystyle=\langle\gamma_{0}(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\gamma_{\pi}(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle\,,

(59)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\mathrm{c}}(z_{1},z_{2},\mu)

\displaystyle=\langle\gamma_{\pi/4}(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\gamma_{5\pi/4}(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle\,.

(60)

Figure 6:

Invariant correlation functions

The dots represent the galaxies and the dashed line represents the Equator. The angle are computed with respect to
\boldsymbol{e}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\partial}_{\theta}
(towards the South).

In this specific coordinate system, one has

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\pi}

\displaystyle=-\gamma_{1}\,,\quad\mathrm{and}

\displaystyle\gamma_{\pi/4}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{5\pi/4}

\displaystyle=-\gamma_{2}\,.

Hence, the invariant correlation functions are given in this system by

\displaystyle\zeta_{\mathrm{p}}(z_{1},z_{2},\mu)

\displaystyle=\langle\gamma_{1}(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\gamma_{1}(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle\,,

(61)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\mathrm{c}}(z_{1},z_{2},\mu)

\displaystyle=\langle\gamma_{2}(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle\,,

(62)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{n}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mu\,.

(63)
