Figure 7:

Correlation functions
\zeta_{\pm}(z_{1},z_{2};\varphi)
for
z=1,\,1.5,\,2

The values for
\zeta_{+}
and
\zeta_{-}
is computed with the flat sky approximation and the power spectrum
C^{\phi}_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})
is obtained with
C
LASS. Using a relative error of the order
10^{3}
for each single measurement and an angular aperture of
5^{\prime}
, we can reach a relatively good SNR (the error being of the order of
10\%
).

Chapter 11
Conclusion

In this Part, we studied weak lensing and built a new estimator for shear correlation functions. We first reviewed the theory of weak lensing together with its main tools.

We presented two different transports of vectors in General Relativity: Parallel Transport and Lie Transport. While the polarisation vector is parallel transported, the galaxy shape is Lie Transported. We argued that, while the polarisation vector is aligned with the galaxy shape at the source position, this is not the case at the observer position. This important result was formalised with the computation of the rotation angle, quantifying the discrepancy between the polarisation and the observed galaxy shape. We showed that this quantity is a signature of cosmic shear (provided the axes of the shear are not aligned with the axes of the galaxy).

We then presented a toy-model application of our result, where we applied it to the lensing of light by Schwarzschild galaxies, using the various models for galaxy density, see
[
196
,
51
,
19
]
.

In the second applications, we estimated cosmic shear correlation functions using the rotation angle as an observable. This quantity was already used in the past, for example to study the weak lensing and the intrinsic alignment of galaxies
[
28
,
27
,
33
,
194
]
. However, our method is new in the sense that we use this rotation angle to estimate cosmic shear. We described how to obtain one estimator for the correlation functions using
4
galaxies (two pairs of galaxies). An important advantage of our method is that it is not affected by intrinsic alignment and we can be agnostic about its effects.

We also showed that, even if the error on a single galaxy measurement can be of the same order, or higher, than the signal itself, this problem can be circumvented by considering a high number of galaxies. For example, we showed that we can reach a
SNR
of order
50
even if the relative error on the shape and polarisation are of order
10^{3}
.

Future survey are expected to include polarisation measurements, for example SKA2,
[
29
]
. In this context, it will provide very useful to have different independent ways to estimate cosmic shear, in order to constraint the various Cosmological models. Hence, this method can be useful as it is different from what has already been done.

PART 4 Gravitational Waves and Effective Theory of Gravity

Clay
, Juan Manuel García Arcos

Chapter 12
General Introduction

In this Part, I present the work we did together with Claudia and Jun during my
3
months visit in London in Fall 2019. This was a lovely time for me, the visit was a total success. Also, 2019 was a nice period, confinement was a concept only relevant when talking about strong interactions, and I did not know the meaning of a PCR. Little that I knew… Anyway, let me tell you what I did in London, which resulted in
[
169
]
.

Gravitational waves emitted by colliding Black Holes were first observed by LIGO in 2016
[
2
,
1
,
96
]
. Among other results, these observations constrained the speed of gravitational waves which is very close to the speed of light, the relative error being smaller than
10^{-10}
. In this new era of Gravitational waves observations, it seems necessary to study as extensively as possible their behaviour in various theories of gravity and in various background, see for example
[
11
,
73
,
105
,
146
]
for such predictions in different contexts.

We already know from Electrodynamics that the speed of photons in a curved background can differ from unity
[
131
,
65
]
. This speed can even be superluminal. This is actually not in contradiction with causality as explained in
[
106
,
107
,
112
,
108
,
109
,
110
,
111
,
185
,
186
]
. The same analysis has been done for gravitational waves. For example, in
[
170
]
, the authors examine how the speed of the gravitational waves can depend on their frequency and argue why they should be exactly luminal at high energies probed by LIGO for example. In
[
172
]
the authors examine the propagation of gravitational waves around a FRLW background and show that the speed can differ from unity.

The general idea is that variation from unity of the speed of gravitational waves can be caused by interactions with very heavy fields or particles. To study this in details, we would need for example a theory of of Quantum Gravity, or at least of Gravity at higher energies… which we do not have! A good framework to circumvent this problem is given by Effective Field Theories of gravity. The main idea is to consider a theory valid at low-energies (for example the Einstein-Hilbert action), and to add
by hand
various higher order terms, staying agnostic about their origin and the specific value of any free parameters, even though various arguments can provide constraints on several terms, see for example
[
72
,
99
,
173
]
where they use arguments about the causality of a theory to constraint higher curvature terms. For a general introduction to Effective Theories, see for example
[
60
,
61
,
62
,
31
]
.

In our work, we consider a particular class of Effective Theories where the higher order terms are Riemann-squared and Riemann-cubed. Such theories were already considered in the framework of string theories, see for example
[
98
,
149
]
. More recently, the speed of gravitational waves and light in curved spacetimes in this framework was discussed in
[
171
]
, while in
[
15
]
they apply the formalism to probe the mechanism of inflation at high energy. In general, the effective terms are treated non-perturbatively. For an example where this is not the case, see
[
44
]
, where a full treatment can lead to interesting results, different from General Relativity.

Effective Theories can also be applied to study Gravity around Black Holes, see for example
[
38
]
for an Effective Theory with Riemann-cubed terms and the Schwarzschild metric or
[
35
,
34
,
36
]
for examples around a rotating Kerr Black Hole. Studying Effective Theories in a Black Hole context is particularly interesting as merging Black Holes are the origin of a good portion of the Gravitational waves we observe on Earth. In particular, the frequencies of the ringdown signal emitted by the final Black Hole after its formation are quantified. These are called the
QNM
, and the specific values they can take is a genuine signature both of the Black Hole parameters, and of the Theory of Gravity. The theory of
QNM
has been studied a lot since the seminal results of Regge and Zerilli
[
167
,
208
]
about the gravitational perturbations around Schwarzschild Black Holes. Since then, a lot of work has been done, see for example
[
20
,
142
,
138
,
137
,
187
]
and
[
162
,
157
]
for more complete reviews.

The structure of this Part goes as follows. Chapter
12
is the present introduction. In Chapter
13
, we review some well-known concepts of the Schwarzschild metric. In particular, we describe the parametrisation of Gravitational perturbations around this specific background and derive the so-called master equation. Then we discuss in details the concept
QNM
. Finally we present a quantitative way to estimate the speed of the gravitational waves around
SSSS
. In Chapter
14
, we present the Effective Theory we want to consider. We derive the perturbed Black Hole solution and the perturbed master equation for the Gravitational perturbations. We compute their speed, and the correction to the
QNM
, based on a method presented in
[
39
,
148
,
123
]
. Finally, we conclude in Chapter
15
.

Chapter 13
Schwarzschild Black Hole

In this Chapter, we present the Schwarzschild Black Holes and the formalism to describe
GW
therearound. We describe these using the formalism presented in Section
3
. In particular, we detail the decomposition between odd and even modes and define the master equation, a Schrödinger-like equation governing the evolution of the perturbations for each mode. We explain the concept of
QNM
and briefly present some well-known results about their values in General Relativity. Finally, we argue how the speed of
GW
can be computed using the effective metric formalism. Most of what is presented in the Chapter can be found in standard textbooks, e.g.
[
45
,
157
,
162
]
.

Menu of the Chapter

1
The Schwarzschild Background

1
The Metric

In this Section, we would like to discuss in more details the Schwarzschild metric which we briefly presented in Section
1
. The Schwarzschild metric is defined, in the usual spherical coordinates, as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{S}}

\displaystyle=-f(r)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}+f(r)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+r^{2}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\Omega^{2}\,,

(1)

\displaystyle f(r)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{2m}{r}\,.

(2)

The Schwarzschild metric is a solution of the vacuum Einstein’s Equations Eq. (
34
), i.e. they satisfy
\boldsymbol{R}=\boldsymbol{G}=0
. The constant
m
is the only parameter of this metric and is given by
m=G_{\mathrm{N}}M_{\mathrm{BH}}
, where
M_{\mathrm{BH}}
is the physical mass (the Komar mass) of the Black Hole.

This metric seems to be ill-defined at
r=r_{\mathrm{g}}\equiv 2m=2G_{\mathrm{N}}M
. This radius is the Schwarzschild radius of the Black Hole and corresponds to the event horizon (which gives the metric Eq. (
1
) the interpretation of a Black Hole!). It is tempting to think that there is some physical singularity on this spatial sphere. This is actually not the case. To check this, it is possible to compute some invariant scalar and show that they behave well at
r=r_{\mathrm{g}}
. As the Ricci and the Einstein tensors vanish, the only tensor at hand is the Riemann tensor. A typical scalar is the Kretschmann scalar given by

K\equiv\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}}\mathchoice{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=12\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}}{r^{6}}\,,

(3)

which is well-defined at
r=r_{\mathrm{g}}
. The metric seems to be singular at the event horizon, but it is possible to perform some changes of coordinates to write it in a form which also holds for
r<r_{\mathrm{g}}
.

The only physical singularity is located at
r=0
, which is in some sense the
position
of the Black Hole. As of today, nobody really knows what happens here…

2
Geodesics

We quickly present the geodesics equation in this metric. We take a slightly more general (stationary and isotropic) metric given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}=-A(r)\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}+B(r)^{-1}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+r^{2}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\Omega^{2}\,,

(4)

where
A(r)
and
B(r)
are arbitrary radial functions. Consider a particle moving in this gravitational field, with trajectory
x^{\mu}(\lambda)
.. As usually, we can define the energy
E
and the angular momentum
L
by

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=A(r)\dot{t}^{2}\,,

(5)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=r^{2}\dot{\varphi}\,,

(6)

where a dot represents here a derivative with respect to the affine parameter
\lambda
. The radial equation of motion of the particle is implicitly given by

\displaystyle\dot{r}^{2}=B(r)\left(\gamma+\frac{E^{2}}{A(r)}-\frac{L^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)\,,

(7)

with
\gamma=-1
for a massive particle and
\gamma=0
for a massless particle.

2
Gravitational Waves

1
Perturbed Einstein Equations

We use the results from Section
3
, in which we defined the perturbations around a
SSSS
. We will work in the Regge-Wheeler gauge with
2
odd perturbations (
h_{0}
and
h_{1}
) and
4
even perturbations (
H_{0}
,
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
and
K_{0}
). Moreover, as we consider a static background, we can also specify the time evolution of these variables. Hence, the only time dependence comes from the factor
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}
, and all these functions depend only on
r
. We will omit the
\ell
,
n
and
\omega
dependence and write
F(r)
, for
F=h_{0},\dots
. Explicitly, the perturbations are given by

\displaystyle(h_{{\mathrm{o}}})_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&-h_{0}\sin^{-1}\theta\,\partial_{\varphi}&h_{0}\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}\\
0&0&-h_{1}\sin^{-1}\theta\,\partial_{\varphi}&h_{1}\sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}\\
-h_{0}\sin^{-1}\theta\,\partial_{\varphi}&-h_{1}\sin^{-1}\theta\,\partial_{\varphi}&0&0\\
h_{0}\sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}&h_{1}\sin\theta\,\partial_{\theta}&0&0\end{pmatrix}Y_{\ell,m}\,,

(8)

\displaystyle(h_{{\mathrm{e}}})_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}\begin{pmatrix}-A(r)\,H_{0}\,&H_{1}\,&0&0\\
H_{1}\,&\frac{H_{2}}{B(r)}&0&0\\
0&0&r^{2}K_{0}\,&0\\
0&0&0&r^{2}K_{0}\sin^{2}\theta\,\\
\end{pmatrix}Y_{\ell,m}\,.

(9)

Note that, for the Schwarzschild metric, one has

A(r)=B(r)=1-\frac{2m}{r}=f(r)\,.

(10)

The full metric is then given by

\boldsymbol{g}=\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{S}}+\varepsilon_{2}\,\boldsymbol{h}\,.

(11)

Here,
\varepsilon_{2}
is a bookkeeping parameter without any physical meaning. It only represents the gravitational perturbations around the background metric.

The Einstein’s Equations read (in vacuum)

\boldsymbol{R}=0\,.

(12)

They are trivially satisfied by the background Schwarzschild metric. At the perturbed level, they can be written as

\delta\boldsymbol{R}=0\,,

(13)

where it is understood that this quantity is at order
\varepsilon_{2}
, coming from the perturbation
\boldsymbol{h}
. They are the
10
equations of motion we need to solve to characterise the behaviour of the perturbations. The explicit expressions can be found in
[
157
]
.

As explained in Section
3
, the odd and the even perturbations do not interact with each other. As a matter of fact, we are left with
3
odd and
7
even equations, as expected from the original number of individual degrees of freedom. If we write
\mathcal{E}_{\mu\nu}\equiv\delta R_{\mu\nu}
, the schematic form of the perturbed equations of motion is

\mathcal{E}_{\mu\nu}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{E}&\mathrm{E}&\mathrm{E}&\mathrm{O}\\
\mathrm{E}&\mathrm{E}&\mathrm{E}&\mathrm{O}\\
\mathrm{E}&\mathrm{E}&\mathrm{E}&\mathrm{O}\\
\mathrm{O}&\mathrm{O}&\mathrm{O}&\mathrm{E}\\
\end{pmatrix}\,,

(14)

where O and E stand for
Odd
and
Even
respectively.

Remark 4.1
(
Simplifications
)
–

Using the spherical symmetry of the background metric, it is sufficient to set
m=0
and
\theta=\pi/2
in the equations of motion. The
\ell
-dependence only appears through the usual variable
J
given by

\displaystyle J^{2}\equiv\ell(\ell+1)\,.

(15)

2
Master Equations

General idea

The equation of motion given by Eq. (
13
) are not very useful if considered in this form. Indeed, they are coupled second order differential equations. Hence, it is necessary to give them a very good massage to extract any relevant physical information. Let us explain qualitatively how to proceed.

The general philosophy goes as follows. We know that
GW
only have two degrees of freedom. This means that out of the
7
functions, only two are truly independent:
\psi_{{\mathrm{o}}}
and
\psi_{{\mathrm{e}}}
, where the first one corresponds to odd modes and the second ones to even modes. Note that
\psi_{{\mathrm{e}}/{\mathrm{o}}}
does not necessarily correspond to the metric perturbations. The variables
\psi_{{\mathrm{e}}/{\mathrm{o}}}
are called
Master Variables
and the equation of motion they satisfy is the
Master Equation
.

In the framework of gravitational perturbations around Black Holes, the master equation for both modes takes the form of a Schrödinger equation

\displaystyle f(r)\big[f(r)\psi_{{\mathrm{o}}/{\mathrm{e}}}^{\prime}(r)\big]^{\prime}+\big[V_{\omega}(r)\omega^{2}-f(r)V_{{\mathrm{o}}/{\mathrm{e}}}(r)\big]\psi_{{\mathrm{o}}/{\mathrm{e}}}(r)=0\,.

(16)

In this equation,
V_{{\mathrm{o}}/{\mathrm{e}}}(r)
represents an effective radial potential, which differs between the two types of perturbations and which depends on
J
and
m
and
V_{\omega}(r)
is a radial function, linked with the speed of the waves (see Section
4
). Here, the function
f
is defined above and corresponds to the Schwarzschild case, but we will see below in Remark
4.3
that other choices are possible.

Remark 4.2
(
Tortoise coordinates
)
–

Defining the
Tortoise coordinates

x
via

\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{f(r)}\mathrm{d}r\,,

(17)

the master equation Eq. (
16
) becomes

\frac{\mathrm{d}\psi_{{\mathrm{o}}/{\mathrm{e}}}}{\mathrm{d}x^{2}}+[V_{\omega}\,\omega^{2}-f\,V]\psi_{{\mathrm{o}}/{\mathrm{e}}}=0\,,

(18)

which is exactly similar (up to a constant multiplication and some redefinition) to the time-independent Schrödinger equation. For the Schwarzschild metric, the explicit coordinate transformation is given by

r+\log\left(\frac{r}{r_{\mathrm{g}}}-1\right)=x\,,

(19)

which is shown in Fig.
1
.

Figure 1
:

Tortoise coordinates

Tortoise coordinates in dimensionless units: The turtle can walk as long as they fancy, they will never reach
\xi=1
,
\zeta=-\infty
.

Remark 4.3
(
Jérémie’s trick
)
–

When we work with the metric perturbations, the master equation does not take directly the form given by Eq. (
16
), but is rather a general second order differential equation

\phi_{1}^{\prime\prime}+\alpha(r)\phi_{1}^{\prime}+\big[\alpha_{\omega}(r)\omega^{2}-\alpha_{V}(r)\big]\phi_{1}=0\,,

(20)

with arbitrary functions
\alpha(r)
,
\alpha_{\omega}(r)
and
\alpha_{V}(r)
. This expression is equivalent to

F_{1}\big[F_{1}\phi_{1}^{\prime}\big]^{\prime}+\big[V_{\omega,1}(r)\omega^{2}-F_{1}\,V_{r,1}(r)\big]\phi_{1}=0\,,

(21)

with

\displaystyle\frac{F_{1}^{\prime}}{F_{1}}

\displaystyle=\alpha\,,

(22)

\displaystyle V_{\omega,1}

\displaystyle=F_{1}^{2}\alpha_{\omega}\,,

(23)

\displaystyle V_{r,1}

\displaystyle=F_{1}\alpha_{V}\,.

(24)

Hence, any generic second order differential equation of the form of Eq. (
20
) can be cast into a master equation with a particular function
F
. Moreover, with the field redefinition

\phi_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\chi}}\phi_{2}\,,

(25)

the master equation Eq. (
21
) becomes

F_{2}\big[F_{2}\phi_{2}^{\prime}\big]^{\prime}+\big[V_{\omega,2}(r)\omega^{2}-F_{2}V_{r,2}(r)\big]\phi_{2}=0\,,

(26)

with

\displaystyle F_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{F_{1}}{\chi}\,,

(27)

\displaystyle V_{\omega,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{V_{\omega,1}}{\chi^{2}}\,,

(28)

\displaystyle V_{r,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{V_{r,1}}{\chi}-\frac{3}{4}F_{1}\frac{(\chi^{\prime})^{2}}{\chi^{3}}+\frac{1}{2\chi^{2}}(F_{1}\,\chi^{\prime})^{\prime}\,.

(29)

The bottom line here is that the master equation is not unique: It is always possible to perform a field redefinition, provided the function
F
and the potentials
V_{\omega}
and
V_{r}
are modified according to Eq. (
27
), Eq. (
28
) and Eq. (
29
). Note that if
F_{1}(r_{\mathrm{g}})=0
, then
F_{2}(r_{\mathrm{g}})=0
, which limits the potential range of the possible functions.

In practice, the equation of motion is of the general form of Eq. (
20
). It can be cast into a master equation solving Eq. (
22
), and the function
F
can be modified using Eq. (
27
). We call this process
Jérémie’s trick
, and we will see its relevance in Chapter
14
.

Odd perturbations

We discuss here the odd perturbations. As indicated in
1
1

1

Is it really an equation?
Eq. (
14
), we have three equations:
{\mathcal{E}}_{t,\varphi}
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{r,\varphi}
, and
{\mathcal{E}}_{\theta,\varphi}
and two variables:
h_{0}
and
h_{1}
. Recall that at this stage, the equation only depend on
r
(and contains remanent terms proportional to
\omega
and
J
coming from the time and angular derivatives).

These three equations are not independent, and an algebraic relation can be found between them and their derivatives, which is expected as we only have two variables. As a matter of fact, the functions
h_{0}
can be expressed in terms of
h_{1}
and
h_{1}^{\prime}
using
{\mathcal{E}}_{\theta,\varphi}
, see Appendix
1
for the explicit expression. Once this has been done,
{\mathcal{E}}_{r,\varphi}
reads

\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}-r}{4r}h_{1}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{2r-5r_{\mathrm{g}}}{4r^{2}}h_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{5r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}+rr_{\mathrm{g}}(J^{2}-6)-r^{2}(J^{2}-2)+r^{4}\omega^{2}}{4r^{3}(r_{\mathrm{g}}-r)}h_{1}=0\,.

(30)

Let us use Jérémie’s trick to massage this equation. Using the notation of Eq. (
20
), we have

\alpha(r)=-\frac{5}{r}+\frac{3}{r-r_{\mathrm{g}}}\,,

(31)

and hence it is a master equation with (up to a constant prefactor)

F_{1}(r)=\frac{(r-r_{\mathrm{g}})^{3}}{r^{5}}\,,

(32)

where we used the relation Eq. (
22
). We want to get a master equation with

f(r)=1-\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}\,,

(33)

which is possible with the field redefinition

\displaystyle h_{1}(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\chi}}\psi_{\mathrm{o}}(r)\,,

(34)

\displaystyle\chi(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{F_{1}(r)}{f(r)}=\frac{(r-r_{\mathrm{g}})^{2}}{r^{4}}\,,

(35)

where we used the transformation rule Eq. (
27
). The master equation is then

\displaystyle f(f\psi_{\mathrm{o}}^{\prime})^{\prime}+(\omega^{2}-f\,V_{\mathrm{o}}(r))\psi_{\mathrm{o}}=0\,,

(36)

\displaystyle V_{{\mathrm{o}}}(r)=\frac{J^{2}}{r^{2}}-3\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r^{3}}\,.

(37)

This is the famous result found by Regge
[
167
]
, the function
V_{\mathrm{o}}(r)
being the
Regge-Wheeler potential
2
2

2

Some authors include the function
f(r)
in the definition of the potential
. The functions
f(r)V_{\mathrm{o}}(r)
for the first
4
values of
\ell
are shown in Fig.
2

Figure 2:

Regge potential

The Regge potential (multiplied by the function
f(r)
) for the first
4
values of
\ell
in dimensionless units

Even perturbations

We would like to do the same for even perturbations. Now,
Rien ne va plus !
… The equations are more numerous, there are more variables and overall the process is more tedious. Let us try to massage them well enough to decipher their secrets.

We have now
7
equations:
{\mathcal{E}}_{t,t}
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{r,r}
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{\theta,\theta}
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{\varphi,\varphi}
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{t,r}
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{t,\theta}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{r,\theta}
, and
4
variables:
H_{0}
,
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
and
K_{0}
. Using
{\mathcal{E}}_{\theta,\theta}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{\varphi,\varphi}
, it is direct to show that
H_{0}+H_{2}=0
, which allows us to eliminate one of them. The second one of these variables can then be eliminated using
{\mathcal{E}}_{t,r}
. We are then left with
H_{1}
and
K_{0}
only. It is possible to go even a bit further: Using
{\mathcal{E}}_{t,\theta}
allows us to express
K_{0}^{\prime}
as a function of
H_{1}
and
K_{0}
. Then,
K_{0}^{\prime}
and its derivatives can be substituted in the other equations. Finally, using
{\mathcal{E}}_{r,r}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{r,\theta}
makes it possible to express
H_{1}^{\prime}
as a function of
H_{1}
and
K_{0}
. The full expressions are given in Appendix
2
. The goal now is to find the master equation. We know that we have only one physical degree of freedom for the even perturbation
\psi_{\mathrm{e}}
. The procedure is slightly more subtle than for the odd perturbations.

First, we assume that the master variable
\psi_{\mathrm{e}}
is a linear combination of the metric perturbations, i.e.

\psi_{\mathrm{e}}(r)=\frac{1}{\omega}f_{H}(r)H_{1}(r)+f_{K}(r)K_{0}(r)\,.

(38)

Recall that all the other variables can be expressed as functions of
H_{1}
and
K_{0}
, which justifies this choice (e.g. there could not be any term proportional to
H_{0}
or
K_{0}^{\prime}
). We describe here how to determine the functions
f_{H}
and
f_{K}
from scratch.

We impose the master equation

f\big[f\psi_{\mathrm{e}}^{\prime}\big]^{\prime}+\big[V_{\omega}(r)\omega^{2}-fV_{\mathrm{e}}(r)\big]\psi_{\mathrm{e}}\,.

(39)

Inserting the Ansatz Eq. (
38
) into the master equation Eq. (
39
), and using the expressions for
H_{1}^{\prime}
and
K_{0}^{\prime}
(given in Appendix
2
), we are left with an expression containing only
H_{1}
and
K_{0}
. From this expression, we can isolate the quantity

V_{\omega,r}(r)\equiv V_{\omega}(r)\omega^{2}-fV_{\mathrm{e}}(r)\,,

(40)

and extract the radial part given by

V_{r}(r)=V_{\omega,r}(r)-\omega^{2}\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{\omega}^{2}V_{\omega,r}\right)|_{\omega=0}\,.

(41)

The key point is that here, with generic functions
f_{H}
and
f_{K}
, the potential
V_{r}(r)
would still contain terms proportional to
H_{1}
and
K_{0}
, which we do not want. Hence we impose the conditions

\displaystyle E_{H}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\delta V_{r}}{\delta H_{1}}=0\,,

(42)

\displaystyle E_{K}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\delta V_{r}}{\delta K_{0}}=0\,,

(43)

This is not straightforward, and one needs to play a little bit before getting the final solution, which we provide here as a
Deux Ex Machina
(the use of
Mathematica
is recommanded!). As a matter of fact, combining
E_{H}
and
\partial_{\omega}^{2}E_{K}
allows us to eliminate
f_{H}^{\prime\prime}
. The remaining equation is algebraic for
f_{K}
and yields

f_{K}(r)=\mathrm{i}\frac{r^{2}f_{H}(r)}{r-r_{\mathrm{g}}}\,.

(44)

Substituting this solution for
f_{K}(r)
in either condition Eq. (
42
) or Eq. (
43
) leads to a differential equation for
f_{H}(r)
only, which is directly solved by

f_{H}(r)=\frac{r-r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r(J^{2}-2)+3r_{\mathrm{g}}}\,,

(45)

which implies, using Eq. (
44
),

f_{K}(r)=\mathrm{i}\frac{r^{2}}{r(J^{2}-2)+3r_{\mathrm{g}}}\,.

(46)

We are now able to define
\psi_{\mathrm{e}}
using Eq. (
38
), and the master equation reads

\displaystyle f\big[f\psi_{\mathrm{e}}^{\prime}\big]^{\prime}+\big[\omega^{2}-f\,V_{\mathrm{e}}(r)\big]\psi_{\mathrm{e}}=0\,.

(47)

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{e}}(r)=\frac{J^{2}-2}{3r^{2}}+\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r^{3}}+\frac{2}{3}\frac{4-3J^{4}+J^{6}}{((J^{2}-2)r+3r_{\mathrm{g}})^{2}}\,.

(48)

This equation is also famous. It was first derived by Zerilli
[
208
]
, using the seminal work of Regge. The potential
V_{\mathrm{e}}(r)
is the Zerilli potential, and the product
f(r)V_{\mathrm{e}}(r)
is shown in Fig.
3
for the first
4
values for
\ell
.

Figure 3:

Zerilli potential

The Zerilli potential (multiplied by the function
f(r)
) for the first
4
values of
\ell
in dimensionless units

Remark 4.4
(
Similarity of the two potentials
)
–

From Fig.
2
and Fig.
3
, it seems that the potentials are roughly equal for
\ell\geq 2
. Using the explicit expressions Eq. (
37
) and Eq. (
48
), it is direct to show that

V_{\mathrm{o}}(r)-V_{\mathrm{e}}(r)=\mathcal{O}(\ell^{-2})\,,

(49)

which implies that these potentials are equal for large values of
\ell
.

3
Quasinormal Modes

1
General Idea

In this Section, we would like to discuss and compute the
QNM
of a Schwarzschild Black Hole. Before going deep into the technical details, let us present the topic from another point of view.

Remember when you were a kid (or even as a grown-up!), you probably played with this soap bubble bottles. What happened is that the bubbles you had just produced were not perfectly spherical. Some of them were
Siamese bubbles
which merged after some point, or two different bubbles could collide and merge to form one bigger bubble.

In all these situations, the rest of the story is the same: The final bubble is not spherical and undergoes some oscillations which are damped over time. This physical process has been discuss quantitatively, see
[
126
]
.

Bulles noires
, Beatriz Alvarez

The same process happens when two Black Holes collide to produce one final, bigger, Black Hole. The latter is not completely spherical at the beginning but presents some
wrinkles
and oscillates. These oscillations emit
GW
that we can, eventually, detect. More important, and we will explain this later on, the frequencies of these oscillations are quantified. Their precise values depend on the physical properties of the Black Hole (e.g. its mass or angular momentum) and on the physical theory of gravity (e.g.
GR
). These
GW
travel through spacetime, and hence their amplitude decreases over time. They form the signal that we measure on Earth, e.g. with LIGO, and this signal is called the
ringdown
of the Black Hole. For a quantitative, yet excellent, video on the topic, see
[
195
]
by
PBS Space Time
.

Being able to compute and measure these
QNM
is very relevant as it allows us to build precise predictions to test our understanding of
GW
propagation, spacetime and Black Holes. Moreover, from a theoretical and mathematical point of view, the theory of
QNM
is very interesting in several aspects.

2
A Mechanical Example

To give a precise definition of
QNM
, let me first use a mechanical analogy. Classical Mechanics is a theory we meet in everyday life, and using examples therefrom can in general be very fruitful before diving into a more advanced and less intuitive topic. This discussion has already been presented in more details, see for example
[
142
]
and we discuss here only the general ideas.

Consider a damped harmonic oscillator of mass
m
, spring constant
k
and friction term
\gamma
. The differential equation describing the evolution of its position
x
is

m\ddot{x}=-kx-\gamma\dot{x}\,.

(50)

Using the Ansatz

x\sim\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}\,,

(51)

we find

-m\omega^{2}=-k+\mathrm{i}\gamma\omega\,,

(52)

which leads to the solution for the frequency

\omega=\pm\frac{\sqrt{4km-\gamma^{2}}}{2m}-\mathrm{i}\frac{\gamma}{2m}\,.

(53)

Plugging this into the solution Eq. (
51
) yields to an oscillatory solution
3
3

3

We assume that the damping is sufficiently small, i.e.
4km>\gamma^{2}
.
with frequency

\omega_{\mathrm{R}}=\pm\frac{\sqrt{4km-\gamma^{2}}}{2m}\,,

(54)

and with exponential damping term given by
\mathrm{e}^{\omega_{\mathrm{I}}t}
with

\omega_{\mathrm{I}}=-\frac{\gamma}{2m}<0\,.

(55)

The bottom line is that the final solution is indeed an exponential solution, as usual, but with an imaginary frequency corresponding to the damping term. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 4.1
(
Quasinormal modes
)
–

A Quasinormal Mode (
QNM
) is an oscillatory mode of the form
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}
where
\omega
is a complex number. The real part of
\omega
is the oscillatory part, while the imaginary part of
\omega
is a damping term (provided it is negative).

With this definition of
QNM
, we understand why they are ubiquitous in Physics: Any real oscillatory system possesses
QNM
. Put a banana on a table and make it oscillate slightly, or observe a swing which has just been left by a kid if you’re not convinced.

The emission of
GW
by a Black Hole is by essence a dissipative system: The waves either fall back into the horizon, or leave away towards infinity. In both cases, the amplitude of the wave is expected to decrease over time!

3
Results

We have just discussed the nature of
QNM
. The next natural question is: How to compute those frequencies explicitly, for example in our setup with Perturbation around a
SSSS
corresponding to a Black Hole. This is a field of investigation in Gravitational Physics per se, and we briefly present here the method introduced by Leaver
[
132
,
133
]
.

To compute the
QNM
, one needs an additional assumption, which we briefly mentionned. Recall that the master equation Eq. (
16
) is weirdly similar to the Schrödinger equation. In Quantum Mechanics, the energies are quantified. The mathematical origin of this comes, in principle, from the boundary conditions of the system. For example, in the usual
Particle in a Box
problem, the condition
\psi(0)=\psi(L)=0
imposes some conditions on the momentum
k
of the wave, henceforth on its energy. In our situation, we have to boundary conditions:

•

no wave is coming from infinity ;

•

no wave is coming from inside the horizon.

These two conditions lead to the quantification of the
QNM
. The details can be found in
[
132
]
(see Section 2). We present here the main ideas. It is possible to rewrite the master equation (for the odd case) as

r(r-r_{\mathrm{g}})\Psi^{\prime\prime}+\Psi^{\prime}+\left(\frac{\omega^{2}r^{3}}{r-r_{\mathrm{g}}}-J^{2}+\frac{3r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}\right)\Psi=0\,,

(56)

where
\Psi
is a master variable (different from the one we used before).

The first boundary condition, in the Tortoise coordinates, reads

\Psi(t,x)\sim\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega x}\,,

(57)

corresponding to a wave escaping at
x=\infty
. Using the definition of the Tortoise coordinates Eq. (
19
), we get

\Psi(r)\sim r^{\mathrm{i}\omega}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega r}\,,

(58)

where we used
x\approx r
as
r\gg r_{\mathrm{g}}
.

The second boundary condition reads

\Psi(t,x)\sim\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega x}\,,

(59)

corresponding to a wave falling into the Black Hole at
x=-\infty
. In radial coordinates, this yields

\Psi(r)\sim(r-r_{\mathrm{g}})^{-\mathrm{i}\omega r_{\mathrm{g}}}\,.

(60)

A solution of the differential equation Eq. (
56
) satisfying the boundary conditions is

\Psi(r)=(r-r_{\mathrm{g}})^{-\mathrm{i}\omega r_{\mathrm{g}}}r^{2\mathrm{i}\omega r_{\mathrm{g}}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega(r-r_{\mathrm{g}})}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}\left(\frac{r-r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}\right)^{n}\,,

(61)

Plugging this Ansatz in the master equation yields to the recursion formula

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\alpha_{0}a_{0}+\beta_{0}a_{0}\,,

(62)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\alpha_{n}a_{n+1}+\beta_{n}a_{n}+\gamma_{n}a_{n-1}\,,

(63)

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}

\displaystyle=n^{2}+\left(-2\mathrm{i}\omega r_{\mathrm{g}}+2\right)n-2\mathrm{i}\omega r_{\mathrm{g}}+1\,,

(64)

\displaystyle\beta_{n}

\displaystyle=-(2n^{2}+(-8\mathrm{i}\omega r_{\mathrm{g}}+2)-8\omega^{2}r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}-4\mathrm{i}\omega r_{\mathrm{g}}+J^{2}-3)\,,

(65)

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}

\displaystyle=n^{2}-4\mathrm{i}\omega r_{\mathrm{g}}n-4\,.

(66)

The argument of Leaver goes as follows: The allowed frequencies
\omega
are the ones for which the series in the Ansatz Eq. (
61
) converges. This is the case if
\omega
is a solution of the continuous fraction equation

\displaystyle\beta_{0}-\frac{\alpha_{0}\gamma_{1}}{\beta_{1}-\frac{\alpha_{1}\gamma_{2}}{\beta_{2}-\dots}}=0\,.

(67)

This condition can be truncated at a given order setting
\alpha_{j}=0
for
j\geq j_{\mathrm{max}}
. For a given values of
j_{\mathrm{max}}
, we get
2j_{\mathrm{max}}
numerical solutions for the frequency
\omega
.

We will not reproduce these computations, as they have been done several times. We only present them in Fig.
4
. The values are taken from
[
20
]
, and the same analysis can be done for even perturbations. An interesting fact: The Schwarzschild Black Hole is said to be
isospectral
, i.e. the odd and the even modes have the same
QNM
frequencies, see
[
45
,
153
]
for more explanations on this topic. The
QNM
frequencies are ordered with decreasing
\omega_{\mathrm{I}}
, the classification
n
index being the
Overtone Number
, starting at
n=0
. The first frequencies (for
\ell=2,3,4
) are given by (see e.g.
[
125
]
4
4

4

The sign of the complex part of
\omega
can differ depending on the sign convention of the exponential.
)

\displaystyle\omega_{0}=\frac{0.747343-0.177925\mathrm{i}}{r_{\mathrm{g}}}\,,

\displaystyle\quad\ell=2\,,

(68)

\displaystyle\omega_{0}=\frac{1.199887-0.185406\mathrm{i}}{r_{\mathrm{g}}}\,,

\displaystyle\quad\ell=3\,,

(69)

\displaystyle\omega_{0}=\frac{1.618357-0.188328\mathrm{i}}{r_{\mathrm{g}}}\,,

\displaystyle\quad\ell=4\,.

(70)

Figure 4:

Quasinormal modes

QNM
for the Schwarzschild Black Hole with
r_{\mathrm{g}}=2m
and for
\ell=2
. We show the first
20
modes with decreasing
\omega_{\mathrm{I}}
. Note the mode with
n=8
is exactly real,
\omega_{8}r_{\mathrm{g}}\approx-4
, see Section 5.1 of
[
20
]
for more explanations.

4
Speed of Gravity

1
Effective Metric

The second useful information we can extract from the master equation is the speed of the
GW
. We present here the general formalism and derive the main formula.

Recall that the master equation for a generic field
\psi(r)
is

F\big[F\psi^{\prime}\big]^{\prime}+\big[V_{\omega}(r)\omega^{2}-FV_{r}(r)\big]\psi=0\,,

(72)

where
F
is an arbitrary function satisfying
F(r_{\mathrm{g}})=0
, while
V_{\omega}
and
V_{r}
are arbitrary radial (recall
V_{r}
generally depends on
J
).

We would like to rewrite the master equation Eq. (
72
) in the form of a generic Laplacian, namely

Z^{\mu\nu}\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\mathcal{D}_{\nu}\Psi=0\,,

(73)

where

\Psi(t,r,\theta,\varphi)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}\psi(r)\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}(\theta,\varphi)\,,

(74)

is a generic field depending on all the variables. In the relation given by Eq. (
73
),

\boldsymbol{Z}=Z_{\mu\nu}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}{x}^{\mu}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}{x}^{\nu}

(75)

is an effective metric, and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}}
is its associated covariant derivative. The effective metric can be interpreted as the metric
felt
by the
GW
. We will assume that the effective metric has the same structure as the background metric, i.e.

\boldsymbol{Z}=-Z_{t}(r)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}+Z_{r}(r)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+r^{2}Z_{\Omega}(r)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\Omega^{2}\,.

(76)

Plugging the Ansatz for
\Phi
Eq. (
74
) in the generic Laplacian Eq. (
73
), and using the form of the effective metric Eq. (
76
) yields (setting
m=0
and
\theta=\pi/2
at the end)

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime\prime}+

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2}{r}+\frac{Z_{\Omega}^{\prime}}{Z_{\Omega}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{Z_{r}^{\prime}}{Z_{r}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{Z_{t}^{\prime}}{Z_{t}}\right)\psi^{\prime}

(77)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\omega^{2}}{Z_{t}Z_{r}}\psi(r)-\frac{J^{2}}{r^{2}Z_{r}Z_{\Omega}}\psi(r)=0\,,

(78)

which should be equal to the master equation Eq. (
72
)

\psi^{\prime\prime}+\frac{F^{\prime}}{F}\psi^{\prime}+\frac{V_{\omega}\omega^{2}}{F^{2}}\psi-\frac{V_{r}}{F}\psi\,.

(79)

Comparing the terms proportional to
\omega^{2}
gives directly

Z_{r}=\frac{F^{2}}{Z_{t}V_{\omega}}\,.

(80)

Plugging this solution into Eq. (
77
) and comparing the terms proportional to
\psi^{\prime}
leads to
5
5

5

We set the integration constants to get convenient expressions for
Z_{\Omega}
and
Z_{t}
.

Z_{\Omega}(r)=\frac{J^{2}\sqrt{V_{\omega}}}{r^{2}}\,.

(81)

Finally, comparing the last term proportional to
\psi
gives

Z_{t}(r)=\frac{F(r)V_{r}(r)}{\sqrt{V_{\omega}(r)}}\,.

(82)

Combining all these results, we get the expression for the effective metric

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}=-\frac{F(r)V_{r}(r)}{\sqrt{V_{\omega}(r)}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}+\frac{V_{r}(r)\sqrt{V_{\omega}(r)}}{F(r)}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+J^{2}\sqrt{V_{\omega}(r)}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\Omega^{2}\,.

(83)

Remark 4.5
(
Form of the effective metric
)
–

It is interesting to see that the effective metric only depends on the structure of the master equation, and not directly on the metric given by Eq. (
4
). Obviously, in fine, the potentials
V_{\omega}
and
V_{r}
do depend implicitly on the background metric and on the underlying theory of gravity, as we will see in the next Chapter.

2
Speed of the Waves

In this Section, we would like to compute the speed of a
GW
satisfying the Laplace equation with the effective metric
\boldsymbol{Z}
Eq. (
73
). We present here a heuristic derivation of the formulas. We take an Ansatz of the form

\Psi\sim\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k_{r}r}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k_{\mu}x^{\mu}}\,,

(84)

with
\boldsymbol{k}=-\omega\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t+k_{r}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}r
. Plugging this Ansatz into the Laplacian Eq. (
73
) gives

Z^{\mu\nu}k_{\mu}k_{\nu}=0\,,

(85)

or

-Z^{tt}\omega^{2}=Z^{rr}k_{r}^{2}\,.

(86)

The phase velocity is given by the condition

k_{\mu}x^{\mu}=0\,,

(87)

or

\omega t=k_{r}r\,.

(88)

Assume now that the waves travels during a small coordinate time
\mathrm{d}t
and a radial coordinate distance
\mathrm{d}r
. The relation Eq.
88
still holds at this infinitesimal level. The physical time the waves travels is given by

\mathrm{d}t_{{\mathrm{p}}}^{2}=-g_{tt}\mathrm{d}t^{2}\,,

(89)

and likewise, the physical distance is

\mathrm{d}r_{{\mathrm{p}}}^{2}=g_{rr}\mathrm{d}r^{2}\,.

(90)

Using the relation Eq. (
88
), the velocity is given by

c_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}=\frac{\mathrm{d}r_{{\mathrm{p}}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t_{{\mathrm{p}}}^{2}}=-\frac{g_{rr}}{g_{tt}}\left(\frac{\omega}{k_{r}}\right)^{2}\,.

(91)

Finally, using the condition on
k
and
\omega
given by Eq. (
87
), the final formula for the speed of the
GW
is (recall that
Z^{tt}<0
)

\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}=\frac{Z^{rr}g_{rr}}{Z^{tt}g_{tt}}=\frac{F(r)^{2}}{A(r)B(r)V_{\omega}(r)}\,,

(92)

where we used the generic form for the metric Eq. (
4
) and the expression of the effective metric Eq. (
83
).

Regarding the perturbations around the Schwarzschild metric, we have for both modes (see the master equations Eq. (
36
) and Eq. (
47
))

A(r)=B(r)=F(r)=f(r)=1-\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}\,,

(93)

and

V_{\omega}(r)=1\,,

(94)

which implies trivially

c_{\mathrm{s}}=1\,.

(95)

In other words, around a Schwarzschild Black Hole,
GW
travel at the speed of light. We already knew this, but the work we did in this Section is not useless, as we will see in the next Section that this result does not hold at the EFT level.

Remark 4.6
(
Invariance of the speed
)
–

The expression for the speed of the waves Eq. (
92
) seems problematic: The function
F
, as explained in Rem.
4.3
is not well-defined. This is actually not a problem, as the potential
V_{\omega}
is also modified when the function
F
is modified. It can be checked directly using the transformation rules Eq. (
27
) and Eq. (
28
) that the expression for the speed Eq. (
92
) is invariant under such a transformation (as the metric, and hence the functions
A
and
B
, is not modified). We conclude that the formula we found is indeed a physical observable as it does not depend on the arbitrary choice for the function
F
.

Remark 4.7
(
Dependence on the coordinates
)
–

Note that, in principle Eq. (
92
) depends on the radial coordinate. This is irrelevant for the Schwarzschild metric as it turns out that the final expression does not, but this will turn out to be very important when we consider the EFT at the perturbed level.

Remark 4.8
(
Which speed did we compute?
)
–

We computed the speed of gravity
c_{\mathrm{s}}
, but with respect to which frame? In the derivation, we used that the distances and time were computed with the metric
\boldsymbol{g}
. If we want to compare this speed with other speed (say the speed of light), we need to assume which metric is
felt
by light. We will then make the assumption that we are working in the Jordan frame. This implies that other fields (including light and matter) are minimally coupled with gravity, and follow geodesics of the metric
\boldsymbol{g}
, see Section
2
where we discussed this with more details. We can then use this metric to compute distances, times, and assume that the speed of light is
c_{\mathrm{l}}=1
in this frame.

Chapter 14
Effective Theory of Gravity

In this Chapter, we present an effective theory of gravity parametrised by Riemann-squared and Riemann-cubed terms added to the Einstein-Hilbert Lagrangian. A similar theory is considered for example in
[
38
,
149
]
. We build explicitly the Lagrangian and argue why most of the terms can effectively be neglected in our framework, as we work at first order around a Schwarzschild solution. We derived the
SSSS
Schwarzschild-like Black Hole in this theory, and derive the master equations governing the
GW
in this perturbed background. The main results of this chapter are the derivation of the speed of the
GW
, and the corrections to the
QNM
of the Black Hole following
[
39
,
148
,
123
]
.

Menu of the Chapter

1
A Perturbed Black Hole

1
General Idea

In this Section, we want to present an effective field theory of gravity. Effective field theories are a domain of research and of interest per se, and I am far from pretending that I master, not even understand, it. As usual, a lot of references exist on the topic, see for example
[
60
,
61
,
62
,
31
]
or the nice article written by Georgi
[
92
]
, which was very useful to me at the beginning of PhD.

The idea goes as follows: When we probe a physical system, we may only be able do it at low energy. For example, our detectors may not be sensitive to photons with energy higher than a given cutoff. A more intuitive example is simply friction: When you try to move forward in a swimming pool, you are not sensitive to the individual, tiny, water molecules dragging you back. Rather, you feel at the human scale a friction force which you model as
F=-\gamma v
, knowing that this is simply an approximation capturing out all the microscopical effects of the water. In this specific example, the coefficient
\gamma
is not a fundamental physical parameter, but is simply an effective coefficient in the theory.

We will follow the same approach for gravity. The Einstein-Hilbert action given by Eq. (
32
) describes our theory of gravity. But what if some very massive field are
hidden
because we are not able to produce them? As explained above, we may be able to still describe their effects on low-energy processes by adding some effective terms in the Lagrangian, as we explain in the following section.

More precisely, in this Chapter, we will consider a Lagrangian of the form

{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EH}}+\varepsilon\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EFT}}\,,

(1)

where
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EFT}}
is the effective Lagrangian coming from the effects of high energy particles, and
\varepsilon\ll 1
is a small perturbative parameter. More details are provided in the next Section. Our goal is to find a solution of this theory in the form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}=\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{S}}+\varepsilon\,\delta\boldsymbol{g}+\varepsilon_{2}\,\boldsymbol{h}\,.

(2)

In this Ansatz,
\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{S}}
is the usual Schwarzschild background given by Eq. (
1
). The second term
\delta\boldsymbol{g}
represents a correction to the Black Hole solution due to the effective Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EFT}}
, which is supposed to be small (hence proportional to
\varepsilon
). We will assume that this perturbed metric is still a
SSSS
. The second term describes the gravitational perturbations around the Black Hole, similar to what we did in Section
2
. Here,
\varepsilon_{2}
has no particular physical meaning, but is rather a bookkeeping parameter representing the gravitational perturbations.

2
The Lagrangian

Recall that the Einstein-Hilbert Lagrangian
1
1

1

We do not include in the Lagrangian the square root determinant.
is given by

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{EH}}=\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}{2}R\,,

(3)

where we used the definition of the Planck mass

\frac{1}{8\pi G_{\mathrm{N}}}=M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}\,.

(4)

We would like to estimate the order of magnitude of this Lagrangian, assuming that the metric is close to the Schwarzschild Black Hole. Obviously, the value of the Lagrangian per se vanishes, as this is a solution of the Einstein’s Equations. However, we can simply think that
R
represents a typical component of the Riemann tensor, which does not vanish.

The curvature
R
has the units of an inverse length squared. In the Schwarzschild metric, there is only one length: The Schwarzschild radius. Hence, we expect that

R\sim\frac{1}{r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}}\,,

(5)

and

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{EH}}\sim\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}{r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}}\,.

(6)

We want to build the effective Lagangian. However, we know that General Relativity and the Schwarzschild solution describe the Physics of the Solar System very well: We do not want the perturbative Lagrangian to be
big
, and hence we need to find a very small dimensionless quantity to multiply
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EH}}
therewith. Let
M
be an arbitrary mass of the same order of magnitude as the Planck mass (or higher). Then, the quantity (with
L_{\mathrm{Pl}}
the Planck length)

\frac{1}{Mr_{\mathrm{g}}}\sim\frac{L_{\mathrm{Pl}}}{r_{\mathrm{g}}}\,,

(7)

is very small if we consider a typical astronomical Black Hole whose radius is much bigger than the Planck Length. Hence, we would like to find a Lagrangian of the form

{\mathcal{L}}_{3}\sim{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{EH}}\times\frac{1}{Mr_{\mathrm{g}}}\sim\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}{Mr_{\mathrm{g}}^{3}}\,,

(8)

where the number
3
indicates the power of
r_{\mathrm{g}}
we need in the denominator. With the Riemann tensor only, we cannot get odd powers of
r_{\mathrm{g}}
, so we also need to use the covariant derivative
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
which has the units of an inverse length, or
r_{\mathrm{g}}^{-1}
. Schematically, this reads

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}

\displaystyle\sim 2\,,

(9)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}

\displaystyle\sim 1\,.

(10)

The only way to get
3
is (schematically)
\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}=3
, hence we need to construct a Lagrangian with one covariant derivatives and one Riemann component. The problem is the following: The Riemann components have an even number of indices, while the covariant derivative only has one index. It then impossible to contract all the indices with these two objects only, so we conclude that
{\mathcal{L}}_{3}=0
.

We consider the next order given by

{\mathcal{L}}_{4}\sim{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{EH}}\times\left(\frac{1}{Mr_{\mathrm{g}}}\right)^{2}\sim\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}{M^{2}r_{\mathrm{g}}^{4}}\,,

(11)

Now, things are more interesting and we have two possibilities to get a term proportional to
r_{\mathrm{g}}^{-4}
in the Lagrangian:
\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{R}
or
\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}
. With the first option, we have three possibility:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{4,1}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{R}{R}{R}{R}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\mathchoice{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\,,

(12)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{4,2}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{R}{R}{R}{R}_{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\,,

(13)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{4,3}

\displaystyle=R^{2}\,.

(14)

With the second option, the only possibility where all the indices are contracted is

{\mathcal{L}}_{4,4}=\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\mathchoice{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\,.

(15)

This term is a divergence, and hence its integral vanishes because of Stokes’s theorem, hence it can be ignored. Finally, the fourth order Lagrangian is

{\mathcal{L}}_{4}=c_{1}{\mathcal{L}}_{4,1}+c_{2}{\mathcal{L}}_{4,2}+c_{3}{\mathcal{L}}_{4,3}\,,

(16)

where
c_{j}
are dimensionless coefficients that we keep unspecified for now.

This would be the end if we did not know the definition of the Gauss-Bonnet term

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{GB}}\equiv R_{\mathrm{GB}}=\mathchoice{R}{R}{R}{R}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\mathchoice{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}-4\mathchoice{R}{R}{R}{R}_{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}+R^{2}={\mathcal{L}}_{4,1}-4{\mathcal{L}}_{4,2}+{\mathcal{L}}_{4,3}.

(17)

This term is interesting as its value in
4
dimensions is fixed and only depends on the topology of the manifold. Such a term does not contribute to the action as its variation trivially vanishes. Using then its definition Eq. (
17
), we can re-express
{\mathcal{L}}_{4,1}
and write

{\mathcal{L}}_{4}=c_{1}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{GB}}+(c_{2}+4c_{1}){\mathcal{L}}_{4,2}+(c_{3}-c_{1}){\mathcal{L}}_{4,3}=(c_{2}+4c_{1}){\mathcal{L}}_{4,2}+(c_{3}-c_{1}){\mathcal{L}}_{4,3}\,,

(18)

where we used that effectively
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{GB}}=0
as a Lagrangian in an action. The bottom line is that we only need to consider two terms in the Lagrangian and write

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{4}=c_{1}R^{2}+c_{2}\mathchoice{R}{R}{R}{R}_{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\,.

(19)

Remark 4.9
(
The coefficients
)
–

In both Lagrangians Eq. (
16
) and Eq. (
19
), we did not specify anything about the coefficients
c_{j}
, which is also part of the philosophy of the effective theory formalism. We do not really know why those terms are here or where they come from. We only know that they are allowed to exist, so we consider them with generic coefficients. This is why we also replaced
c_{2}+4c_{2}
and
c_{3}-c_{1}
by
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, as these coefficients do not posses any meaning per se. Moreover, we should have multiplied them by
M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}/M^{2}
to be consistent, but this quantity being a dimensionless number, we can just absorb it and redefine the coefficients. We will do this in what follows and we will not mention it systematically. We will present in Section
2
an example where these coefficients can be computed exactly.

We continue our journey and consider the next order

{\mathcal{L}}_{5}\sim\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EH}}\times\left(\frac{1}{Mr_{\mathrm{g}}}\right)^{2}\sim\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}{M^{3}r_{g}^{5}}\,.

(20)

This case is similar to
{\mathcal{L}}_{3}
: The odd powers of
r_{\mathrm{g}}
forces us to use an odd number of covariant derivatives, which makes it impossible to contract all the indices with the Riemann tensor. Hence, the term
{\mathcal{L}}_{5}
cannot appear.

The last term we will consider is

{\mathcal{L}}_{6}\sim\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EH}}\times\left(\frac{1}{Mr_{\mathrm{g}}}\right)^{4}\sim\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}{M^{4}r_{g}^{6}}\,.

(21)

This is more interesting: To obtain a power of
6
, we have three options :
\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{R}
,
\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}
and
\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}
. The last option ought to be of the form
\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{R}
, which is a total derivative corresponding to a boundary term, and we can ignore it.

1.

Let us focus on the terms of the form
\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}
first. We have several options.

(a)

Two Ricci scalars

This implies that both covariant derivatives must be contracted together, which leaves us with only two options :
\nabla_{\mu}R\nabla^{\mu}R
and
R\nabla_{\mu}\nabla^{\mu}R=R\Box R
. These two options are equivalent at the action level as they can be obtained through an integration by part, i.e.

\nabla_{\mu}R\nabla^{\mu}R=\nabla_{\mu}(\nabla^{\mu}R)-R\Box R\,,

(22)

and the first term does not contribute to the action. Doing this may artificially change the coefficient in front of
R\Box R
, but as explained before, this is not a problem as the various coefficients do not have any physical interpretation per se in an effective context. The first term of
{\mathcal{L}}_{6}
is then

{\mathcal{L}}_{6,1}=d_{1}R\Box R\,.

(23)

(b)

One Ricci scalar and one Ricci tensor

We cannot contract the Ricci tensor anymore, as this would lead to the previous case. Hence, the Ricci tensor should be contracted with the covariant derivatives, i.e.
R\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}R^{\mu\nu}
.
However, the Bianchi identity of the Einstein tensor Eq. (
40
) implies

\nabla_{\nu}R^{\mu\nu}\propto\nabla^{\mu}R\,,

(24)

and hence

R\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}R^{\mu\nu}\propto R\Box R\,,

(25)

which has already been considered.

(c)

Two Ricci tensors

Contracting a covariant derivative with a Ricci tensor would lead to a term with the Ricci scalar (using again the Bianchi identity). The first option is

{\mathcal{L}}_{6,2}=d_{2}R_{\mu\nu}\Box R^{\mu\nu}\,,

(26)

up to a boundary term as usual. The second option is
\nabla_{\mu}R^{\nu\alpha}\nabla_{\nu}\mathchoice{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}
. An integration by part would lead to

\mathchoice{R^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{R^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{R^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{R^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\mathchoice{R^{{{\mu}{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu}{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\disp ]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{R^{{{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}\left(\nabla_{\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\mathchoice{R^{{{\mu}{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu}{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu}{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu}{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][ e$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho\mu\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.02058pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.02058pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.90637pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.64738pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho\mu\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.02058pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.02058pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.90637pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.64738pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho\mu\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.02058pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.02058pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.90637pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.64738pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho\mu\nu}}}}R^{\mu\rho}\right)\,,

(27)

where we used the definition of the curvature. The first term simplifies because of the Bianchi identity, and the second and third terms are of the form
\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{R}
, which we consider below.

(d)

One Ricci scalar and one Riemann tensor

Such a term would necessarily contains a Ricci tensor as the Riemann tensor has to be contracted at least once, hence the case has already been considered above.

(e)

One Ricci tensor and one Riemann tensor

The only possible term would be
R^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\alpha}\nabla_{\beta}\mathchoice{R^{{{\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.9061pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.21864pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.9061pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.21864pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.9061pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.21864pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8716pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.373pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83784pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.9061pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.21864pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\nu}}}}
. However, using the second Bianchi identity Eq. (
27
), the term with the Riemann tensor can be expressed as a combination of the covariant derivative of the Ricci tensor, which has already been considered.

(f)

Two Riemann tensor

It is possible to show that a term with two Riemann tensors and two covariant derivatives can be rewritten as a linear combination of the terms above and of Riemann-cubed terms, using the same properties and the fact that
\nabla_{\mu}R^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
can be rewritten in terms of the Ricci tensor only.

2.

We now turn our attention to the terms of the form
\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{R}
. The idea is the same as above. One needs to carefully list all the possibilities, and uses all the tricks (integration by part, identity of the tensors, symmetries) to check which terms have already been considered. We do not give all the details here, but only the final result which reads

\displaystyle\sum_{j=3}^{10}d_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{6,j}=
