tyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\rho}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{\rho}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{\rho}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{\rho}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14899pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80954pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{\rho}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Bigr)\,.

The final Lagrangian and action read

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EH}}+{\mathcal{L}}_{4}+{\mathcal{L}}_{6}\,,

(30)

\displaystyle S[\boldsymbol{g},c_{i},d_{j}]

\displaystyle=\int\;\sqrt{-g}\,{\mathcal{L}}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,,

(31)

where it is implied that the
\varepsilon
factor is absorbed in the coefficients
c_{j}
and
d_{j}
.

Before turning our attention to a quantitative analysis, let us estimate the value of the small parameter
\varepsilon
introduced in Eq. (
2
). As explained above, we have three different Lagrangians, the order of magnitude of which being given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EH}}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}{r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}}\,,

(32)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{4}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{r_{\mathrm{g}}^{4}}\,,

(33)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{6}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{M^{2}r_{\mathrm{g}}^{6}}\,,

(34)

We require the two last Lagrangians to be smaller than the Einstein-Hilbert one.

For
{\mathcal{L}}_{4}
the condition reads

\varepsilon\equiv\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}{r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}}=\left(\frac{L_{\mathrm{Pl}}}{r_{\mathrm{g}}}\right)^{2}\ll 1\,.

(35)

This condition is definitely satisfied for astronomical Black Hole. For a mass of the order of one solar mass, we have

\varepsilon\sim 10^{-80}\,,

(36)

which perfectly justifies the perturbative approach we are using for such Black Holes. Only the extreme case of a Primordial Black Hole whose mass is of the order of the Planck mass (see
[
41
]
for example) would lead to

\varepsilon\sim 0.5\,.

(37)

At this length scale, the Lagrangian
{\mathcal{L}}_{4}
would not be perturbative anymore and more sophisticated approach would be necessary. Moreover, at this length scale, we can no longer assume that massive fields can be integrated out and the knowledge of a better theory is necessary.

The condition on
{\mathcal{L}}_{6}
is more interesting. It leads to

\frac{1}{M^{2}M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}r_{\mathrm{g}}^{4}}\ll 1\,.

(38)

Expressing this parameter in more intuitive variables yields

\varepsilon\sim 10^{-160}\left(\frac{M}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\right)^{-2}\left(\frac{M_{\mathrm{BH}}}{M_{\astrosun}}\right)^{-4}\ \ll 1\,.

(39)

This condition is definitely valid for astrophysical Black Holes with
M_{\mathrm{BH}}\sim 50M_{\astrosun}
, even if the integrated fields satisfy
M\sim 10^{-60}M_{\mathrm{Pl}}
. For these reasons, the perturbative dimensionless parameter is given by

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\frac{1}{M^{2}M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}}\,.

(40)

3
So many Terms…

A toy-model

In this Section, we would like to describe how the final Lagrangian Eq. (
30
) can be effectively simplified, provided we study perturbations around the Schwarzschild Black Hole. Before going into the technical details, we again present a simple mechanical toy-model. This model was proposed as an exercise about perturbation theory and Oscillations around an equilibrium position for second year students following the course
Mécanique II
during the Fall Semester 2019.

Consider a non-relativistic particle of mass
m
moving in a potential of the form

V(x)=V_{0}(x)+\varepsilon\,\delta V(x)\,,

(41)

where
V_{0}(x)
is a background potential and
\varepsilon\,\delta V(x)
is a small perturbation. The equation of motion is

m\ddot{x}=-V^{\prime}(x)\,.

(42)

Assume now that we know that
x_{0}
is a stable equilibrium position of the background potential
V_{0}(x)
, i.e.
V_{0}^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
and
V_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})>0
. In the full theory, the new equilibrium position is

\tilde{x}_{0}=x_{0}+\varepsilon\,\delta x\,,

(43)

and
\delta x
is found imposing
V^{\prime}(\tilde{x}_{0})=0
at order
\varepsilon
. The computations are straightforward and yield

\delta x=-\frac{\delta V^{\prime}(x_{0})}{V_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})}\,,

(44)

which is well-defined as
V_{0}^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})\neq 0
.

Now, we consider oscillations around the equilibrium. At the background level, we have

x(t)=x_{0}+\varepsilon_{2}\,y(t)\,,

(45)

where
\varepsilon_{2}
is the bookkeeping parameter describing the oscillations. Plugging this Ansatz into the equation of motion Eq. (
42
) yields to the very well-known result

m\ddot{y}=-yV^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})\,,

(46)

from which we can extract the frequency

\omega_{0}^{2}=\frac{V^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})}{m}\,.

(47)

At the perturbed level, the oscillatory solution reads

x(t)=x_{0}+\varepsilon\,\delta x+\varepsilon_{2}\,y(t)\,,

(48)

and the equation of motion is

m\ddot{y}=-y\Bigl(V_{0}^{\prime\prime}+\varepsilon\,V_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}\delta x+\varepsilon\,\delta V^{\prime\prime}\Bigr)\,,

(49)

where all the derivatives are evaluated at
x=x_{0}
. We can extract the perturbed frequency as

\tilde{\omega}_{0}=\frac{V_{0}^{\prime\prime}+\varepsilon\,V_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}\delta x+\varepsilon\,\delta V^{\prime\prime}}{m}\,.

(50)

Let us stress three important points about this toy-model, which will illuminate the discussion below.

1.

Two terms appear in the correction of the frequency: The first term is a consequence of the displacement of the equilibrium due to
\delta x
, and the second one comes from the perturbation
\delta V
.

2.

If the first derivative
\delta V^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
, the equilibrium position is not modified, i.e.
\delta x=0
.

3.

If, in addition to
\delta V^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
, the second derivative
\delta V^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})=0
, the frequency is not modified.

A useful trick

In the final effective Lagrangian Eq. (
30
), there are
2+10=12
independent parameters, which is very large. We can however simplify this action. To see this, let us consider a simpler version with the Lagrangian given by

{\mathcal{L}}=\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}{2}\left(R+\varepsilon\,R_{\rho\sigma}\Pi^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}\right)\,,

(51)

where
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
is an arbitrary tensor, which can potentially contains derivatives. The equation of motion are

G_{\mu\nu}+\varepsilon\left(\delta(R_{\rho\sigma}\Pi^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta})_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}R_{\rho\sigma}\Pi^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}\right)=0\,,

(52)

where the second term schematically represents the term containing
\delta g^{\mu\nu}
and the last term comes from the variation of the square root determinant.

We first require the background solution to be close to the Schwarzschild solution, i.e.

\bar{\boldsymbol{g}}=\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{S}}+\varepsilon\,\delta\boldsymbol{g}\,.

(53)

Here comes the trick: As we work at first order, in the term proportional to
\varepsilon
in the equations of motion Eq. (
52
), we can set
\boldsymbol{g}=\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{S}}
. It is direct to show that every term is proportional to the Ricci tensor, which vanishes on the Schwarzschild solution. Hence, the equation is

\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{S}}+\varepsilon\,\delta\boldsymbol{g})=0\,.

(54)

As we want the perturbation
\delta\boldsymbol{g}
to be small, the trivial solution is
\delta g=0
. In other words, the term we considered does not contribute to the modified background.

We now turn our attention to gravitational perturbations of the form (using
\delta\boldsymbol{g}=0
)
\boldsymbol{g}=\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{S}}+\varepsilon_{2}\,\boldsymbol{h}
. The equations of motion are still given by Eq. (
52
), but we will expand them at linear order in
\varepsilon_{2}
. At the
GR
level (
\varepsilon=0
), the equations of motion are simply

(\delta G_{\mu\nu})_{\rho\sigma}h^{\rho\sigma}=(\delta R_{\mu\nu})_{\rho\sigma}h^{\rho\sigma}=(\delta R)_{\rho\sigma}h^{\rho\sigma}=0\,,

(55)

where we used the relation between the Ricci and the Einstein tensor (or simply said, if one vanishes, so does the other). In the equations of motion Eq. (
52
), all the terms quadratic in
\boldsymbol{h}
can be neglected, and we are left with

G_{\mu\nu}+\varepsilon\left((\delta R_{\rho\sigma})_{\mu\nu}\Pi^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}R_{\alpha\beta}+R_{\rho\sigma}\Pi^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}(\delta R_{\alpha\beta})_{\mu\nu}\,.\right)=0\,.

(56)

As before, in the second term, we can use the
GR
solution and use that
\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{S}}+\boldsymbol{h})=0
to cancel both terms. We are left with

G_{\mu\nu}=0\,,

(57)

i.e. even at the gravitational perturbations level, the second term in the Lagrangian Eq. (
51
) does contribute.

Remark 4.10
(
Connection with the mechanical toy-model
)
–

Using the notation we introduced in the previous section, we have first
x_{0}\sim\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{S}}
,
\delta V\sim R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}
, and we want to compute
\delta x\sim\delta\boldsymbol{g}
. Schematically, we have (neglecting the indices)

V_{0}\sim R\,,\quad\delta V^{\prime}\sim R\delta R=0\,.

(58)

The linear term does not contribute when evaluated at
\boldsymbol{g}=\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{S}}
(or
x=x_{0}
). Hence, the displacement vanishes and we have indeed
\delta x\sim\delta\boldsymbol{g}=0
. At the oscillations level

\delta V^{\prime\prime}\sim R\delta\delta R+\delta R\delta R=0,

(59)

which also vanishes when the zeroth order equations of motion are used.

Many spurious terms

With this trick in mind, the Lagrangian becomes much simpler. Basically, any term containing two Ricci’s can be eliminated, so effectively speaking:

c_{1}=c_{2}=d_{1}=d_{2}=d_{3}=d_{4}=d_{6}=d_{7}=0\,,

(60)

and the Lagrangian becomes

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}=

\displaystyle\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}{2}R+\frac{\varepsilon}{M^{2}}(d_{5}{\mathcal{L}}_{6,5}+d_{8}{\mathcal{L}}_{6,8}+d_{9}{\mathcal{L}}_{6,9}+d_{10}{\mathcal{L}}_{6,10})\,.

(61)

We can actually go even further. The Lagrangians
{\mathcal{L}}_{6,10}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{6,5}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{6,5}

\displaystyle=4{\mathcal{L}}_{6,8}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{I}}f_{j,5}{\mathcal{L}}_{6,j}\,,

(62)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{6,10}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{L}}_{6,9}-3{\mathcal{L}}_{6,8}+\frac{3}{8}{\mathcal{L}}_{6,5}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{I}}f_{j,6}{\mathcal{L}}_{6,j}\,,

(63)

where
\mathcal{I}=\{3,4,6,7\}
, see
[
35
]
for the explicit formula. Replacing
{\mathcal{L}}_{6,5}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{6,10}
using these relations yields

d_{5}{\mathcal{L}}_{6,5}+d_{8}{\mathcal{L}}_{6,8}+d_{9}{\mathcal{L}}_{6,9}+d_{10}{\mathcal{L}}_{6,10}=\left(d_{8}+4d_{5}-\frac{3}{2}d_{10}\right){\mathcal{L}}_{6,8}+\left(d_{9}+\frac{1}{2}d_{10}\right){\mathcal{L}}_{6,9}\,,

(65)

where we neglected the terms corresponding to
j\in\mathcal{I}
.

To summarise, it is sufficient to make the computations with
(d_{8},d_{9})
only. The complete expressions for the observables, with the coefficients
d_{5}
and
d_{10}
, are given by substituting

(d_{9},d_{9})\rightarrow\left(d_{8}+4d_{5}-\frac{3}{2}d_{10},d_{9}+\frac{1}{2}d_{10}\right)\,.

(66)

4
The Equations of Motion

We present now briefly the equations of motion, assuming that only the terms
{\mathcal{L}}_{6,8}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{6,9}
are present. Moreover, if their respective equations of motion contains any term with a Ricci tensor or scalar, they can be neglected (the idea follows the same lines as the proof above). Hence, the equations of motion are

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{EH}}_{\mu\nu}+d_{8}\mathcal{E}^{8}_{\mu\nu}+d_{9}\mathcal{E}^{9}_{\mu\nu}

(67)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{EH}}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}{2}\left(R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Rg_{\mu\nu}\right)\,,

(68)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{8}_{\mu\nu}

(72)

5
A Spherically Symmetric Static Black Hole

In this section, we finally have all the ingredients in our hand to construct the perturbed Schwarzschild background. We assume that the metric is

\boldsymbol{g}\equiv\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{S}}+\varepsilon\,\delta\boldsymbol{g}=-A(r)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}+\frac{1}{B(r)}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+r^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\Omega^{2}\,,

(73)

and we suppose that the functions
A
and
B
are of the form

\displaystyle A(r)

\displaystyle=f(r)+\varepsilon\,\delta A(r)\,,

(74)

\displaystyle B(r)

\displaystyle=f(r)+\varepsilon\,\delta B(r)\,,

(75)

with

f(r)=1-\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}\,.

(76)

The Ansatz can be plugged into the equations of motion Eq. (
67
), recalling that in the perturbed term, the
\varepsilon
correction can be ignored. The resulting equation are not straightforward to solve, but we postulation a solution of the form

\displaystyle\delta A(r)=\alpha_{1}\left(\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}\right)^{n_{1}}+\alpha_{2}\left(\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}\right)^{n_{2}}\,,

(77)

\displaystyle\delta B(r)=\beta_{1}\left(\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}\right)^{n_{1}}+\beta_{2}\left(\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}\right)^{n_{2}}\,.

(78)

The resulting equations are schematically of the form

f_{0}(r)r^{0}+f_{1}(r)r^{1}=g_{1}(r)r^{7-n_{1}}+g_{2}(r)r^{7-n_{2}}\,,

(79)

which is solved if
n_{1}=6
and
n_{2}=7
(or vice-versa). Plugging these values, it is straight forward to find the coefficients
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{j}
and the solution reads

\displaystyle A(r)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}+\varepsilon\left(\alpha_{6}\left(\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}\right)^{6}+\alpha_{7}\left(\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}\right)^{7}\right)\,,

(80)

\displaystyle B(r)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}+\varepsilon\left(\beta_{6}\left(\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}\right)^{6}+\beta_{7}\left(\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r}\right)^{7}\right)\,,

(81)

with

\displaystyle\alpha_{6}

\displaystyle=-6d_{8}\,,\quad

\displaystyle\alpha_{7}

\displaystyle=9d_{8}+10d_{9}\,,

(82)

\displaystyle\beta_{6}

\displaystyle=36(d_{8}+3d_{9})\quad

\displaystyle\beta_{7}

\displaystyle=-33d_{8}-98d_{9}\,.

(83)

The Black Hole horizon is displaced, as
A(r_{\mathrm{g}}),B(r_{\mathrm{g}})\neq 0
. The position of the horizon is defined (at first order in
\varepsilon
) by

A(r_{\mathrm{g}}+\varepsilon\,\delta r_{\mathrm{g}})=B(r_{\mathrm{g}}+\varepsilon\,\delta r_{\mathrm{g}})=0\,.

(84)

The solution is the same for both functions and is given by

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{H}}=r_{\mathrm{g}}+\varepsilon\,\delta r_{\mathrm{g}}=r_{\mathrm{g}}\big(1-\varepsilon(3d_{8}+10d_{9})\big)\,.

(85)

In this effective theory, we have two different radii. The first unperturbed radius is related to the Black Hole mass as
r_{\mathrm{g}}=2G_{\mathrm{N}}M_{\mathrm{BH}}
, while the physical horizon
r_{\mathrm{H}}
is given by Eq. (
85
), with
r_{\mathrm{H}}\neq 2G_{\mathrm{N}}M_{\mathrm{BH}}
.

2
Gravitational Waves, the Return

1
The Magic of Perturbation Theory

We determined the
SSSS
solution given by

\boldsymbol{g}=\boldsymbol{g}_{\mathrm{S}}+\varepsilon\,\delta\boldsymbol{g}\,.

(86)

We want now to determine the master equations of the
GW
around this perturbed solution, in our effective theory. The parametrisation for the perturbations is the same as for the unperturbed case, see Eq. (
8
) and Eq. (
9
). Note that the functions
A(r)
and
B(r)
can be taken at zeroth or first order without modifying the final results.

We present here the general logic to get the master equation at the perturbed level. At the background level, we have for each modes a collection of fields
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
, which includes all the perturbations and their derivatives. For example, in our two cases, they read

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{{\mathrm{o}}}

\displaystyle=\{h_{0},h_{1},h_{0}^{\prime},h_{1}^{\prime},\dots\}\,,

(87)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{{\mathrm{e}}}

\displaystyle=\{H_{0},H_{1},H_{2},K_{0},H_{0}^{\prime},H_{1}^{\prime},H_{2}^{\prime},K_{0}^{\prime},\dots\}\,.

(88)

When we solved the background solution, we actually found a finite number of fields with respect to which all the others fields can be expressed. Mathematically speaking, there is a finite set
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{f}}
of
free
2
2

2

Here, the term
free
has nothing to do with the usual free fields in QFT.
fields and a infinite subset
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{c}}
of constrained fields such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{f}}

\displaystyle\cap\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{c}}=\varnothing\,,

(89)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{f}}

\displaystyle\cup\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{c}}=\boldsymbol{\Psi}\,.

(90)

The constrained fields can be expressed as linear functions of the free fields as

\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{c}}=\boldsymbol{\Xi}\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{f}}\,,

(91)

where
\boldsymbol{\Xi}
is a (infinite) linear operator. In our cases, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{{\mathrm{o}},{\mathrm{f}}}

\displaystyle=\{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}\}\quad

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{{\mathrm{o}},{\mathrm{c}}}

\displaystyle=\{h_{0},h_{0}^{\prime},h_{0}^{\prime\prime},h_{1}^{\prime\prime},\dots\}\,,

(92)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{{\mathrm{e}},{\mathrm{f}}}

\displaystyle=\{H_{1},K_{0}\}\quad

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{{\mathrm{e}},{\mathrm{c}}}

\displaystyle=\{H_{0},H_{2},H_{0}^{\prime},H_{1}^{\prime},H_{2}^{\prime},K_{0}^{\prime},\dots\}\,.

(93)

We now turn our attention to the first order equations. They are of the form

{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{GR}}(\boldsymbol{\Psi})+\varepsilon\,\delta{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{\Psi})\,,

(94)

in which we can use the magic of perturbation theory: The second term being first order, we can plug therein the background solutions Eq. (
91
) to eliminate all the fields
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{c}}
. The equations then read

{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{\Psi})={\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{GR}}(\boldsymbol{\Psi})+\varepsilon\,\delta{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{f}})\,.

(95)

Moreover, at the background level, we can massage the equations in a certain way to express all the constraints fields
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{c}}
as functions of the free fields
\Psi_{\mathrm{f}}
. We can apply the
same
massage to the full equations of motion to express them at first order. At each step of the process, we can reuse the magic of perturbation theory and replace
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{c}}
by
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{f}}
in the terms proportional to
\varepsilon
. The final result is an expression of the form

\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{c}}=(\boldsymbol{\Xi}+\varepsilon\,\delta\boldsymbol{\Xi})\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\mathrm{f}}\,,

(96)

from which we can fully eliminate the constrained fields and then extract the master equation, as we did for the background case.

2
Master Equations

Odd perturbations

The massage needed for odd perturbations is detailed in Section
2
. The final equation reads

\displaystyle\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}-r}{4r}h_{1}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle+\frac{2r-5r_{\mathrm{g}}}{4r^{2}}h_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{5r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}+rr_{\mathrm{g}}(J^{2}-6)-r^{2}(J^{2}-2)+r^{4}\omega^{2}}{4r^{3}(r_{\mathrm{g}}-r)}h_{1}

(97)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\big(\alpha_{h_{1}}(r)h_{1}+\alpha_{h_{1}^{\prime}}(r)h_{1}^{\prime}\big)=0\,,

and the explicit formulas are given in Appendix
1
.

Remark 4.11
(
Schwarzschild radius
)
–

Recall that in the zeroth order term
r_{\mathrm{g}}=2G_{\mathrm{N}}M
is
not
the Schwarzschild radius of the Black Hole, while at first order all these values are equal, i.e.
r_{\mathrm{g}}=r_{\mathrm{H}}=2G_{\mathrm{N}}M
.

We want to find the master equation

F(r)\big[F(r)\psi_{{\mathrm{o}}}^{\prime}\big]^{\prime}+\big[V_{\omega}(r)\omega^{2}-F(r)V_{{\mathrm{o}},1}(r)\big]\psi_{{\mathrm{o}}}(r)=0\,.

(98)

Recall Jérémie’s trick (Remark
4.3
): We are free to choose the function
F(r)
(as long as it vanishes on the Black Hole horizon). Here, for reasons that will be clear later, we take

F(r)=1-\frac{r_{\mathrm{H}}}{r}\,,

(99)

where
r_{\mathrm{H}}
is the Schwarzschild radius at the perturbed level.

These manipulations lead to the final master equation with

\displaystyle V_{\omega}(r)

\displaystyle=1+\varepsilon\big(d_{8}\,V_{\omega,8}(r)d_{9}\,V_{\omega,9}(r)\big)\,,

(100)

\displaystyle V_{\omega,8}(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{6r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r^{6}}(r^{5}+r^{4}r_{\mathrm{g}}+r^{3}r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}+r^{2}r_{\mathrm{g}}^{3}+rr_{\mathrm{g}}^{4}-4r_{\mathrm{g}}^{5})\,,

(101)

\displaystyle V_{\omega,9}(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{4r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r^{6}}(5r^{5}+5r^{4}r_{\mathrm{g}}+5r^{3}r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}+5r^{2}r_{\mathrm{g}}^{3}+77rr_{\mathrm{g}}^{4}-94r_{\mathrm{g}}^{5})\,,

(102)

\displaystyle V_{{\mathrm{o}},1}(r)

\displaystyle=V_{\mathrm{o}}(r)+\varepsilon\,\delta V_{{\mathrm{o}}}(r)\,,

(103)

where
V_{\mathrm{o}}(r)
is the Regge potential given by Eq. (
37
). The expression for
\delta V_{{\mathrm{o}}}(r)
is given in Appendix
1
. As we will see in the next Section, the potential
V_{\omega}(r)
is the same for both modes, hence we omit the subscript odd/even. Note that the Regge potential
V_{\mathrm{o}}(r)
is expressed here in terms of the Black Hole mass
M_{\mathrm{BH}}
. Inverting the relation between
r_{\mathrm{g}}
and
r_{\mathrm{H}}
yields

2G_{\mathrm{N}}M_{\mathrm{BH}}=r_{\mathrm{g}}=r_{\mathrm{H}}\big(1+\varepsilon(3d_{8}+10d_{9})\big)\,,

(104)

which will bring extra first order corrections. This will reveal important when we compute the corrections to the
QNM
.

Even perturbations

Dealing with the even perturbations is slightly more complicated than with the odd ones. The expressions and the computations are longer as the number of variables and of equations is higher. As for the odd modes, the massage is described in Section
2
. Using the background solution to simplify the first order part of the equation, we are left with
H_{0}^{\prime}
and
K_{1}^{\prime}
expressed as functions of
H_{0}
and
K_{1}
. As before, we assume that the master variable is given by

\psi(r)=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{(J^{2}-2)r+3r_{\mathrm{g}}}\left(r^{2}K_{0}(r)\big[1+\varepsilon\,\delta f_{K}(r)\big]-\mathrm{i}\frac{\sqrt{AB}r}{\omega}H_{1}(r)\big[1+\varepsilon\,\delta f_{H}(r)\big]\right)\,,

(105)

with
A(r)
and
B(r)
given by Eq. (
80
) and Eq. (
81
). One last field redefinition given by

\psi(r)=\big[1+\varepsilon\chi(r)\big]\psi_{\mathrm{e}}(r)

(106)

is necessary to bring the master equation in the same form as for the odd perturbations

F(r)\big[F(r)\psi_{{\mathrm{e}}}^{\prime}\big]^{\prime}+\big[V_{\omega}(r)\omega^{2}-F(r)V_{{\mathrm{e}},1}(r)\big]\psi_{{\mathrm{e}}}(r)=0\,,

(107)

with

F(r)=1-\frac{r_{\mathrm{H}}}{r}\,,

(108)

and

V_{{\mathrm{e}},1}(r)=V_{\mathrm{e}}(r)+\varepsilon\,\delta V_{{\mathrm{e}}}(r)\,,

(109)

V_{\mathrm{e}}(r)
being the Zerilli potential given by Eq. (
48
). Moreover,
V_{\omega}(r)
takes the same form as in the odd case (Eq. (
100
)). The explicit expressions for the other quantities are given in Appendix
2
.

Remark 4.12
(
Defining the correct field redefinition
)
–

Note that it is not straightforward at all to define the functions
\delta f_{K}
,
\delta f_{H}
and
\chi
. The general idea is to define
\psi_{\mathrm{e}}
in terms of
K_{0}
and
H_{1}
. We can isolate
\delta V_{{\mathrm{e}}}(r)
by imposing the master equation Eq. (
107
), and we impose that the final expression does not depend on
K_{0}
nor on
H_{1}
, as we did in the background case. These conditions, with an Ansatz about the form of
\delta f_{K}
and
\delta f_{H}
yield the final results.

3
Discussion

With the expression for the potential
V_{\omega}(r)
given by Eq. (
100
), we can directly compute the speed of the
GW
using Eq. (
92
). This yields

\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{s}}=1-\varepsilon\,72(2d_{9}+d_{10})\frac{r_{\mathrm{g}}^{5}(r-r_{\mathrm{g}})}{r^{6}}\,,

(110)

where we restored the coefficient
d_{10}
using Eq. (
66
). This is the main result of this Section. We define the speed difference as

\delta c_{\mathrm{s}}\equiv\frac{c_{\mathrm{s}}-1}{\varepsilon(2d_{9}+d_{10})}\,,

(111)

which we show in Fig.
1
. It clear both from the analytical expression and from the figure that the sign of the speed difference is always negative
\delta c_{\mathrm{s}}<0
.

Figure 1:

Speed difference

Speed difference as a function of the dimensionless radius
\xi=r/r_{\mathrm{g}}
. The minimum is located at
(\xi,\delta c_{\mathrm{s}})\sim(6/5,-5)
.

As a specific example, we consider three cases where the lightest massive particle which has been integrated out is either a scalar (
s=0
), a fermion (
s=1/2
) or a boson (
s=1
). The values of the coefficients
d_{9}
and
d_{10}
are taken from
[
172
,
10
,
9
]
. From the relation Eq. (
110
), the
GW
are
superluminal
if
2d_{9}+d_{10}<0
and
subluminal
if
2d_{9}+d_{10}>0
. The results are shown in Tab.
1
. We see that in the fermion case, the waves are superluminal, while they are subluminal in the scalar and boson case.

s

2d_{9}+d_{10}

Speed

0

+\frac{1}{1260}

Subluminal

1/2

-\frac{1}{630}

Superluminal

1

+\frac{1}{420}

Subluminal

Table 1:
Velocity of the gravitational in three examples

It can be surprising to see that
GW
can be superluminal in some cases. In general, subluminality is a necessary condition to ensure that the theory does not violate causality. Naively, one would expect that an effective theory with
c_{\mathrm{s}}>1
should be eliminated, and for example conclude that the lightest particle integrated out could not be a fermion. However, some authors pointed out that superluminality is not a signature of causality violation, see for example the work of Hollowood
[
106
,
107
,
112
,
108
,
109
,
110
,
111
]
or
[
171
,
173
,
46
]
.

Another important point is that see from the expression Eq. (
110
) that the speed of
GW
exactly vanishes at
r=r_{\mathrm{g}}=r_{\mathrm{H}}
(recall that these two last quantities are equal at first order). Moreover, using the expression for the effective metric Eq. (
83
), it is direct to show that

Z_{t}(r_{\mathrm{H}})=Z_{r}(r_{\mathrm{H}})=0\,.

(112)

This means that the horizons seen by the
GW
and by the photons are actually the same. This result is actually more general and is related to the
Horizon Theorem
, see for example
[
102
,
185
,
186
,
112
]
. Even if we did the computations at first order, this result should also holds at all orders.

The fact that both species see the horizon at the same location can be understood as follows. We have two metrics at hand: The usual metric
\boldsymbol{g}
, and the effective metric
\boldsymbol{Z}
. We can compute the Riemann tensors of each metric, respectively
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{g}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}_{Z}
. Let us assume that both horizons are different, i.e.
r_{\mathrm{H}}(\boldsymbol{g})\neq r_{\mathrm{H}}(\boldsymbol{Z})
. We can compute a scalar invariant, for example the hybrid Kretschmann

\Upsilon\equiv R_{g,\mu\nu\rho\sigma}R^{Z,\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,.

(113)

In our effective theory, the terms we added are finite when evaluated at
r_{\mathrm{H}}
, hence we do not expect such a scalar to be divergent when we turn on the small perturbation. However, computing
\Upsilon
explicitly leads to a divergent part of the form

\Upsilon\propto\frac{1}{[r-r_{\mathrm{H}}(\boldsymbol{g})][r-r_{\mathrm{H}}(\boldsymbol{Z})]}\,,

(114)

which is not defined on either horizon. Hence, we need
r_{\mathrm{H}}(\boldsymbol{g})=r_{\mathrm{H}}(\boldsymbol{Z})
to get a well-defined behaviour for such quantities at the horizon.

3
Quasinormal modes

1
General Method

In this Section, we compute the corrections to the
QNM
frequencies. We follow the method developed by Cardoso et al.
[
39
,
148
,
123
]
. They develop a systematic approach to compute corrections to
QNM
around Black Holes, let us summarise their method.

Let
r_{\mathrm{H}}
be the Black Hole radius and
\psi
a master variable. We do not specify if we study odd or even variables as the logic is the same. The generic master equation reads

F\big[F\psi^{\prime}\big]^{\prime}+\big[\Omega^{2}-FV(r)\big]\psi=0\,.

(115)

with

F(r)=1-\frac{r_{\mathrm{H}}}{r}\,,

(116)

and
\Omega
is a constant frequency. It can be a priori different from the frequency we measure
\omega
, we will discuss this below. The potential
V
can be decomposed as

V=V_{\mathrm{GR}}+\varepsilon\,\delta W\,,

(117)

where the first term is the
GR
contribution, while the second term is a perturbation of any kind.

An important comment should be made here: The
GR
potential
V_{\mathrm{GR}}
should be expressed in terms of
r_{\mathrm{H}}
, which brings a correction to
\delta W
as
r_{\mathrm{g}}=r_{\mathrm{H}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
. In our context, it corresponds to the higher terms in the Lagrangian, but the potential can also come from considering slowly rotating Black Holes.

The perturbed potential should then be decomposed as an infinite series

\delta W(r)=r_{\mathrm{H}}^{2}\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}\left(\frac{r_{\mathrm{H}}}{r}\right)^{j}\,.

(118)

In the article, the authors computed a set of complex numbers
e_{j}
from which the perturbed frequency can be computed as

\displaystyle\Omega

\displaystyle=\Omega_{0}+\varepsilon\,\delta\omega\,,

(119)

\displaystyle\delta\omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}e_{j}\alpha_{j}\,,

(120)

where
\Omega_{0}
is the
QNM
in
GR
. In principle, the coefficients
e_{j}
will depend on the modes we consider (e.g. odd or even) as well as on the angular number
\ell
.

To apply this method to our case, there is one subtlety. Our master equations is of the form

F\big[F\psi^{\prime}\big]^{\prime}+\big[V_{\omega}\omega^{2}-FV(r)\big]\psi=0\,.

(121)

This is not exactly equivalent to Eq. (
115
) as there is a function in front of the frequency. This is discussed in Appendix B of
[
39
]
. We present here the key ideas.

First, we can use the fact that in our cases

V_{\omega}(r)=1+\varepsilon\delta V_{\omega}(r)

(122)

both for the odd and for the even perturbations. The master equation Eq. (
121
) can be identically rewritten as

F(r)\big[F(r)\psi^{\prime}\big]^{\prime}+\left[\big[1+\varepsilon\delta V_{\omega}(r_{\mathrm{H}})\big]\omega^{2}-F(r)\left(V(r)-\varepsilon\omega^{2}\frac{\delta V_{\omega}(r)-\delta V_{\omega}(r_{\mathrm{H}})}{F(r)}\right)\right]\psi=0\,.

(123)

Hence, assuming that the potential
V
is decomposed as

V(r)=V_{\mathrm{GR}}(r)+\varepsilon\,\delta V(r)\,,

(124)

the effective perturbation
\delta W
appearing in Eq. (
115
) is then

\delta W=\delta V-\omega^{2}\frac{\delta V_{\omega}(r)-\delta V_{\omega}(r_{\mathrm{H}})}{F(r)}\,.

(125)

Moreover, in the final equation Eq. (
123
), the frequency we compute is actually a rescaled frequency
\Omega
defined as

\Omega^{2}=\big[1+\varepsilon\,\delta V_{\omega}(r_{\mathrm{H}})\big]\omega^{2}\,.

(126)

This relation can be checked by directly comparison between the generic master equation Eq. (
115
) and the master equation we obtain in our particular case Eq. (
123
). Hence, the final frequency is given by

\omega=\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon\,\delta V_{\omega}(r_{\mathrm{H}})\right)\Omega=\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon\,\delta V_{\omega}(r_{\mathrm{H}})\right)(\Omega_{0}+\varepsilon\delta\omega)=\Omega_{0}+\varepsilon\left(\delta\omega-\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{0}\delta V_{\omega}(r_{\mathrm{H}})\right)\,.

(127)

This is the correction to the
QNM
for a Black Hole with Schwarzschild radius
r_{\mathrm{H}}
. We want to compare this frequency to a Black Hole with the same mass. In order to do so we express the background frequency as

\Omega_{0}=\frac{\xi_{0}}{r_{\mathrm{H}}}\,,

(128)

where
\xi_{0}
is dimensionless complex number. Using the generic relation between the radius of the Black Hole and its mass (see Eq. (
85
))

r_{\mathrm{H}}=r_{\mathrm{g}}+\varepsilon\delta r_{\mathrm{g}}\,,

(129)

we get

\displaystyle\omega=\omega_{0}+\varepsilon\left(\delta\omega-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{0}\delta V_{\omega}(r_{\mathrm{H}})-\frac{\delta r_{\mathrm{g}}}{r_{\mathrm{g}}}\right)\,,

(130)

where we used that
\Omega_{0}=\omega_{0}
at first order and
\omega_{0}=\xi_{0}/r_{\mathrm{g}}
. As
r_{\mathrm{g}}=2G_{\mathrm{N}}M_{\mathrm{BH}}
, this relation makes it possible to compute the
QNM
for a given Black Hole mass.

2
Results

As an example, we compute the numerical corrections to the
QNM
for the first overtone number (
n=0
), i.e. the background frequencies are
\xi_{0}=0.747343-0.177925\mathrm{i}
,
1.199887-\mathrm{i}0.185406\mathrm{i}
and
1.618357-\mathrm{i}0.188328
, respectively for
\ell=2,3,4
. We define the relative perturbation as

\delta\equiv\left(\frac{\mathrm{Re}(\omega-\omega_{0})}{\varepsilon\,\mathrm{Re}(\omega_{0})},\frac{\mathrm{Im}(\omega-\omega_{0})}{\varepsilon\,\mathrm{Im}(\omega_{0})}\right)=d_{8}\delta_{8}+d_{9}\delta_{9}\,.

(131)

We show in Tab.

the numerical corrections we obtain for the first frequency. , and in Fig.

, we show the direction of the displacement in the
(\omega_{\mathrm{R}},\omega_{\mathrm{I}})
plane for the
\delta_{9}
contribution only, assuming
d_{9}>0
. Interestingly, the imaginary part increases for odd modes and decrease for even modes. Recall that the imaginary part is proportional to the damping (or inversely proportional to the lifetime of the wave).

. Obviously, the lengths do not correspond to anything. The shift in the imaginary part is negative for even modes and positive for odd modes.

It seems from the numerical results that the corrections coming from
d_{8}
are very small. As a matter of fact, these should be exactly
0
. Indeed, if we perform a metric redefinition given by

\boldsymbol{g}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}-\frac{2\varepsilon}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}\delta\boldsymbol{g},

(132)

the Einstein Hilbert action

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{EH}}=\int\;\sqrt{-g}\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}{2}R\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{4}

(133)

becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{\tilde{S}}_{\mathrm{EH}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{EH}}+\varepsilon\,\delta\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{EH}}\,,

(134)

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{EH}}

\displaystyle=\int\;\sqrt{-\tilde{g}}\left(\tilde{R}^{\mu\nu}-\frac{\tilde{R}}{2}\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}\right)\delta g_{\mu\nu}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{4}\,.

(135)

In particular, choosing

\delta g_{\mu\nu}=d_{8}\left(\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}}\mathchoice{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.65453pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.7063pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64732pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}-\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\right)

(136)

yields

\delta\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{EH}}=-d_{8}\int\;\sqrt{-\tilde{g}}R^{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\nu}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\nu}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\nu}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}{R^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.7922pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.75381pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5384pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\nu}{\nu\rho\sigma}}}}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{4}\,.

(137)

Considering the effective Lagrangian Eq. (
29
), this correction exactly cancels out the term
{\mathcal{L}}_{6,8}
.

Let us assume now that only the term
{\mathcal{L}}_{6,8}
is present in the theory. After performing the field redefinition Eq. (
136
), we are left with
GR
, where the
QNM
are known, i.e.

\tilde{\omega}=\omega_{\mathrm{GR}}\,,

(138)

where
\tilde{\omega}
corresponds to the frequency in the frame associated with
\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}
. It is direct to show that the correction given by Eq. (
136
) has the the usual time dependence
\sim\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\tilde{\omega}t}
. Hence, after performing the inverse field redefinition Eq. (
132
), the time dependence stays the same, which means that
\tilde{\omega}
is also the frequency in the original frame associated with the metric
\boldsymbol{g}
. Because of these considerations, the term proportional to
d_{8}
does not contribute to the
QNM
.

Remark 4.13
(
What about the other terms?
)
–

Do you remember all the terms we eliminated in Section
3
? We were able to do it only because we are considerations perturbation around the Schwarzschild metric. However, this comment is independent on the metric background. Moreover, it can be shown that all the terms in the effective Lagrangian except
d_{9}
and
d_{10}
can be eliminated with an appropriate field redefinition. This means that, in this effective theory of gravity, and at linear order, the corrections to the
QNM
will only depend on the combination
2d_{9}+d_{10}
(recall
d_{10}
can also be eliminated, see Eq. (
66
)). Roughly speaking, only the terms that are genuinely Riemann-cubed contribute to the
QNM
corrections.

Remark 4.14
(
What about the speed?
)
–

Could we also conclude that the speed of the
GW
also depends only on
2d_{9}+d_{10}
only, as it is actually the case in our specific example? This is actually more subtle. Performing the field redefinition will add some non-minimal interactions between the matter fields and the metric. Hence, in the new frame, the speed of light is not
1
anymore. However, we still expect the ratio of the velocities to be frame-independent, as we discussed in Part
2
.

It can be formally shown that the ratio of the speed of
GW
and of light is frame-independent, see
[
172
]
.

Chapter 15
Conclusion

In this Part, we first reviewed some well-known facts about the Schwarzschild Black Hole. We presented the formalism to study gravitational perturbations around a
SSSS
Black Hole, and its decomposition into odd and even modes.

We discussed the concept of
QNM
, which is central in Gravitational Physics and Astrophysics. Gravitational Waves have been detected in 2015, see
[
2
,
1
]
after the collision of two Black Hole. The ringdown of this signal can be decomposed in a sum of different modes, the frequencies of which are called
QNM
. The frequencies of the
QNM
are quantified, and the list of this frequency is the identity card both of the Black Hole and of the theory of Gravity governing the production and the propagation of these
GW
. Being able to predict these frequencies in various models and under different assumptions is necessary if we want to extract useful information from these gravitational waves signals.

We then quantitatively explained how the speed of a gravitational wave can be defined, provided a master equation. The definition we provided is observer invariant as it does not depend on the master equation one decides to use to perform the analysis. We developed the formalism of the effective metric, which can also be used to determine the position of the Black Hole horizon as seen by the gravitational waves.

Once these basic, yet important, concepts were defined, we turned our attention to a specific Effective Theory of Gravity. Effective Theories are very useful as they allow us to capture the effects of heavy fields, without knowing exactly the identities of those, or any high-energy Physical theory, see
[
38
,
35
,
34
,
90
,
98
,
149
,
83
,
193
,
192
]
for various examples on the topic of
QNM
. The philosophy of an Effective Theory is to stay agnostic about this unknown theory, and only consider its effects on low-energy processes by adding an arbitrary linear combination of every possible terms in the Lagrangian up to a given (mass) dimension. In our case, we considered terms of the forms Riemann-squared and Riemann-cubed. We argued that, even if one should technically consider
12
different terms,
10
of them can be actually removed if we work perturbatively around a Schwarzschild Black Hole. We were then left with only two new terms in the action, namely
{\mathcal{L}}_{6,8}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{6,9}
, parametrised by two dimensionless coefficients
d_{8}
and
d_{9}
and one mass
M
corresponding, roughly, to the mass of the lightest fields which was integrated out.

The first step of our analysis in this Effective Theory was to determine the
SSSS
correction to the Schwarzschild Black Hole. In the usual Spherical coordinates, the terms
g_{tt}
and
g_{rr}
receives corrections proportional to
r^{-6}
and
r^{-7}
. We also computed the position of the new horizon
r_{\mathrm{H}}
which is different from the usual Schwarzschild radius
r_{\mathrm{g}}
, hence the usual relation does not hold anymore in our theory
r_{\mathrm{H}}\neq 2G_{\mathrm{N}}M_{\mathrm{BH}}
.

Second, we derived a master equation for the odd and the even perturbations. We used the method discussed in
[
39
,
148
,
123
]
to compute numerically the corrections to the Quasinormal frequencies, based on a series expansion of the perturbed potential. We computed these frequencies for three different modes and showed that the contribution proportional to
d_{8}
vanishes (up to some numerical errors, especially for even modes). We explained why this was expected, as the
{\mathcal{L}}_{6,8}
term can be removed by a field redefinition, under which the frequency is not modified.

Lat but not least, we derived and expression for the speed of the Gravitational Waves around the Black Hole, which formed the main result of this work. This topic has received a lot of attention in the past, see
[
170
,
171
,
172
]
. We found that the sign of
c_{\mathrm{s}}-1
only depends on the coefficients of the Effective Theory (and not on the radial distance), and we consider examples where the gravitational waves can be either subluminal or superluminal. Even if the second could be surprising at first sight, one can actually not rule out such a theory, as was argued in
[
106
,
107
,
112
,
108
,
109
,
110
,
111
]
and
[
171
]
.

Finally, we derived the expressions for the effective metric seen by the Gravitational waves, and showed that they feel the same horizon as the other species (especially light). This is result is actually more general and is linked to the Horizon Theorem. Our analysis only holds at linear order in the perturbations, but this result should hold at any order.

One last comment: We showed qualitatively that the order of magnitude of the perturbations is extremely tiny around typical Black Holes. For example, if the Black Hole mass is around
50
Solar Mass, and if the parameter in the Lagrangian is of order
M\sim 10^{-60}M_{\mathrm{Pl}}
, the amplitude of the corrections would be of order
\epsilon\sim 10^{-40}
. This implies that no observations could distinguish between General Relativity and the theory we considered (at least considering astronomical Gravitational waves). Our work is therefore mostly theoretical and conceptual as we investigated the properties of the gravitational waves in a given Effective Theory of Gravity.

Future works could include, for example, a similar study around rotating Kerr Black Holes, as it was already done in
[
36
,
68
]
. Some other works considered the same kind of gravitational perturbations, but in a specific theory of Modified Gravity, for example in Horndesky’s theory
[
121
]
or in theories of non-linear electrodynamics
[
158
]
.

PART 5 Conclusion and Outlook

Contemplation
, Juan Manuel García Arcos

Chapter 16
General Conclusion

This is the end of our journey. For you it may have been a few hours, but for me it felt more like 4 years, probably a relativistic effect. Let us take a step back and summarise what we learned in this thesis.

The objective of this thesis was to study several topics with one idea in mind: How can we define clear observables, with the objective of testing our Cosmological models and parameters? As Cosmology becomes more and more data driven, it is very important to be able to have in our hands several different, and independent, ways to perform measurements and test our theories. Let us review the various observables presented in this thesis.

First, in Part
2
, I studied conformally related frames, where both metrics are related to each other via a spacetime dependent global factor. I considered two specific frames: The Einstein and the Jordan frames. In the Einstein frame, the Einstein-Hilbert action takes the usual form, while matter fields are non-minimally coupled to gravity. The opposite is true in the Jordan frame: The Einstein-Hilbert action is not standard as there is a coupling to the scalar field, but the matter is minimally coupled to gravity. I presented the physical interpretation of working with two different frames: This is nothing but a spacetime-dependent (local) change of units. I argued that this should, in fine, not change the predictions regarding physical observables. Then, I introduced the Galaxy Number Counts as a physical observable quantifying the angular fluctuations of the number of observed galaxies. I showed that this quantity does not depend on the frame, as expected. This shows that the Galaxy Number Counts is a genuine observable, or the other way around, that both frames are physical. This also means that computations can be performed in either frames, as long as sufficient care is taken in the interpretation of the results. The Galaxy Number Counts can itself help us to test Cosmological models, for example by inferring the convergence correlation function, whose power spectrum is directly linked to the lensing power spectrum.

Then, in Part
3
, I turned my attention to the weak lensing formalism. After briefly summarising the main concepts of lensing, I showed that images of galaxies whose main axes are not aligned with the principal direction of the shear undergo a rotation. I argued that this is a good candidate for a physical observable if the polarisation direction is also measured. Indeed, this vector tends to be aligned with the axes of the ellipse. As the polarisation is parallel transported along the path, its position defines a Sachs basis with respect to which the images of galaxies rotate. A non-zero angle between the polarisation of light and the main axes of the ellipse is a signature of shear. Its observation can be used to probe the shear along the line of sight between the observer and the source. With this idea in mind, I proposed a new way to probe cosmic shear by evaluating this angle for several galaxies in a catalogue. I explicitly showed how to estimate coordinate invariant shear correlation functions using this observable. Finally, I argued that this method can be competitive, i.e. yield low signal-to-noise ratio, if the number of observed galaxies is sufficiently high. Correlation functions, or the power spectrum, their equivalent in Fourier space, are again a good test of our Cosmological models, which motivates the will to define several independent ways to estimate them.

Finally, in Part
4
, I considered a Schwarzschild Black Hole in a Modified Gravity theory. I first recalled some well-known facts about the Schwarzschild Black Hole: The decomposition of gravitational perturbations into odd and even modes, the Master Equation and the Regge and Zerrili potentials. With these tools, I argued that we can define the speed of gravity in a non-ambiguous way, and I presented the central concept of Quasinormal Modes of Black Holes. Second, I defined an Effective Field Theory of Gravity, in which we assume that high energy fields exist but are not observable at low-energy scales. These fields still have an effect on low-energy Physics through their interaction with, for example, the metric. This interaction can be explicitly determined by integrating out these fields from the high-energy theory. I thus constructed the most general effective Lagrangian, up to the fourth power of the Riemann tensor (matter fields were not considered in this specific model). I argued that most terms in the Lagrangian are actually negligible, due to the specific Schwarzschild case I considered. Then, I proceeded with the same analysis as for the General Relativity case. I derived the background perturbed Black Hole and showed that the position of the horizon is slightly displaced. I computed the corrected speed of the Gravitational Waves and the correction to the Quasinormal Modes. Two main results were that the horizon is the same for both gravitational waves and other matter particles and that the speed of the gravitational waves can be either subluminal or superluminal, depending on the nature of the heavy particles integrated out. I argued that, in fine, this actually does not lead to any causality issues. Finally I also showed that the amplitude of the various corrections in this model are small, and that no experimental tests can be extracted therefrom. Hence, the main goal of this project was more to show some conceptual aspects of Effective Theories, rather than pretend to build realistic observables.

Chapter 17
Outlook

What’s next?

As mentioned in Chapter
I
, Cosmology has become a strongly observational science. To give only one example, the first Gravitational Waves have been observed in 2015, and the mischief was repeated several times since then! The
LCDM
model describes particularly well the observations we have made so far: Observations are in perfect agreement with the
FRLW
model and the theoretical
CMB
power spectrum.

But remember, this is not the end of the story. The Hubble tension, dark matter and dark energy are three ghosts wandering above our heads. To solve these main problems, and all the others I kept silent, we need to face two challenges. First, it is necessary to come up with new models that differ from
GR
enough so as to predict these problematic observations, without spoiling all the old results, e.g. regarding the Solar System or in accordance with the
FRLW
model. Second, we also need to find ways to perform extremely precise observations to confront these models or to be able to detect a potential candidate for the dark components.

To make a connection with the present work, let us remember that in Part
4
, we estimated that the effective theory we considered would add correction of magnitude
\varepsilon\sim 10^{-100}
, or smaller. Comparing this with the sensitivity of the LIGO experiment
\sim 10^{-30}
, this is a double failure: This theory does not make any useful prediction departing from
GR
, and we are far from having the technology to probe such perturbations. However, this is not the end of the story. Black Hole Physics is an intensely explored field nowadays and there are high hopes that we will find a theory in which departure from
GR
is detectable.

Regarding lensing, as I briefly pointed out, new galaxy surveys are planned. These will include new observational information, for example light polarisation. Be it thanks to the method I presented, or any other one, further research will doubtless sharpen our knowledge and lead to more precise estimation of the Cosmological parameters.

This is the situation that Cosmology faces now. There are, however, reasons for optimism. Until today, humankind has shown a wonderful ability to come up with new ideas and new technologies to face never ending challenges (with the little cost that came along – the destruction of our planet, LOL). This can only sound promising for the future of the field!

PART 6 Appendices

The Desk
, Juan Manuel García Arcos

Chapter 18
Rotation from Lensing

Menu of the Chapter

Appendix 18.A
Optical Depth

We derive here the results presented in Section
3
. We consider the following situation. An observer is located at
x=0
while a light source is located at
x=L
, where
x
represents a distance. The space between the observer and the source is filled with randomly distributed targets. We assume that if light passes close enough to a target, it undergoes some
modification
. We do not precise the modification we have in mind, we only assume that its intensity
\Theta
can be measured. In the example of Section
3
, this is the scaled rotation and the targets are Schwarzschild lenses. We assume that, to obtain at least a given intensity
\Theta_{0}
, the light must pass in a surface of size
\sigma(\Theta_{0},x)
around a target located at distance
x
from the observer. This quantity represents the cross-section of the targets, and we assume that it depends on the distance and on the intensity of the rotation. The dependence on
\Theta_{0}
satisfies the reasonable assumption
\sigma(0,x)=\infty
. The situation is illustrated in Fig
1
.

Figure 1:

Optical depth - Hide and seek with the fish

The observer is looking inside a forest filled with trees of various sized
\sigma
and randomly distributed with density
n
. What is the probability that the observer can see the blue pond on the left, at a distance
L
?

We want to compute
P(>\Theta_{0},L)
, the probability of observing the source with a rotation bigger than
\Theta_{0}
. To compute this quantity, we compute first
P(\leq\Theta_{0},x)
, the probability for the light ray to undergo a rotation less or equal than
\Theta_{0}
. We will go backwards and start at
x=0
to finally reach
x=L
. The initial condition is

P(\leq\Theta_{0},0)=1\,,

(1)

for all values of
\Theta_{0}
.

First, let us assume that
P(\leq\Theta_{0},x)
is known. We want to evaluate
P(\leq\Theta_{0},x+\mathrm{d}x)
. To do so, we need to consider the conditional probability

P(\leq\Theta_{0},x+\mathrm{d}x|\leq\Theta_{0},x)=1-P(>\Theta_{0},x\rightarrow x+\mathrm{d}x)\,,

(2)

where
P(>\Theta_{0},x\rightarrow x+\mathrm{d}x)
is the probability of undergoing a rotation bigger than
\Theta_{0}
between
x
and
x+\mathrm{d}x
. This is intuitively understood as follows: If you have reached
x
without problem, the probability for you to reach
x+\mathrm{d}x
without problem is almost
1
, up to the probability you get in trouble between
x
and
x+\mathrm{d}x
.

We evaluate now this last term. We assume that the light beam travels in a tube of sectional area
\mathrm{d}A
and length
\mathrm{d}x
. The number of targets is

\mathrm{d}N=n\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}A\,,

(3)

where
n
is the density of targets, which can depend on
x
and
\Theta_{0}
, but we do not write it explicitly to not clutter the notations. The probability of hitting a target is the ratio of the targets area
\mathrm{d}A_{\sigma}=\sigma\,\mathrm{d}N
with the total area
\mathrm{d}A
. Here, we assumed that the length
\mathrm{d}x
is sufficiently small to avoid overlapping targets (recall the surface of an individual target is
\sigma
). This probability is then

P(>\Theta_{0},x\rightarrow\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{\mathrm{d}A_{\sigma}}{\mathrm{d}A}=\frac{\sigma\,\mathrm{d}N}{\mathrm{d}A}=n\sigma\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

(4)

Using Bayes’ Theorem, we can express the conditional probability in Eq. (
2
) as

\displaystyle P(\leq\Theta_{0},x+\mathrm{d}x|\leq\Theta_{0},x)

\displaystyle=\frac{P(\leq\Theta_{0},x|\leq\Theta_{0},x+\mathrm{d}x)P(\leq\Theta_{0},x+\mathrm{d}x)}{P(\leq\Theta_{0},x)}

(5)

\displaystyle=\frac{P(\leq\Theta_{0},x+\mathrm{d}x)}{P(\leq\Theta_{0},x)}\,,

(6)

where we used the obvious fact that

P(\leq\Theta_{0},x|\leq\Theta_{0},x+\mathrm{d}x)=1\,,

(7)

i.e. if you reach your endpoint without problem, we can be sure you did not get into trouble anywhere before. Combining the various relations Eq. (
2
), Eq. (
4
) and Eq. (
6
) yields

P(\leq\Theta_{0},x+\mathrm{d}x)=P(\leq\Theta_{0},x)(1-n\sigma\,\mathrm{d}x)\,.

(8)

The solution of this differential equation, together with the initial condition Eq. (
1
), is

\displaystyle P(\leq\Theta_{0},x)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\tau(\Theta_{0},x)\right)\,,

(9)

\displaystyle\tau(\Theta_{0},x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{y}\;n(\Theta_{0},y)\sigma(\Theta_{0},y)\,\mathrm{d}y\,,

(10)

where we explicitly wrote the dependence on
\Theta
and on the position. Furthermore, we can assume that the targets have also internal parameters
\chi
(e.g. the mass in our example) over which we integrate, and in a cosmological setup, the redshift
z
is a better parameter than the distance. This gives the final relation

\displaystyle P(\leq\Theta_{0},z_{\mathrm{s}})

\displaystyle=\exp(-\tau(\Theta_{0},z_{\mathrm{s}}))\,,

(11)

\displaystyle\tau(\Theta_{0},z_{\mathrm{s}})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{z_{\mathrm{s}}}\;n(\Theta_{0},z,\chi)\sigma(\Theta_{0},z,\chi)\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{\mathrm{d}z}\,\mathrm{d}\chi\,\mathrm{d}z\,.

(12)

Note that here the density
n
should be the physical density of targets. Moreover, the inverse probability is given by

P(>\Theta_{0},z_{\mathrm{s}})=1-P(\leq\Theta_{0},z_{\mathrm{s}})

(13)

and its PDF
p(\Theta,z_{\mathrm{s}})
satisfies

\displaystyle P(>\Theta_{0},z_{\mathrm{s}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Theta_{0}}^{\infty}\;p(\Theta,z_{\mathrm{s}})\,\mathrm{d}\Theta\,,

(14)

\displaystyle p(\Theta,z_{\mathrm{s}})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial P(>\Theta,z_{\mathrm{s}})}{\partial\Theta}\,.

(15)

Appendix 18.B
Illustris’ Simulation

We present here the parametrisation for the galaxy number density
n(z,\sigma_{v})
discussed in Section
3
, based on the Illustris’ hydronamical simulation, see
[
196
,
51
]
. The cumulative galaxy number is

\displaystyle\log_{10}\tilde{N}(>{\tilde{\sigma}}_{v},z)=

\displaystyle A(z)+\alpha(z)\left(\log_{10}\tilde{\sigma}_{v}-\gamma(z)\right)

(16)

\displaystyle+\beta(z)\left(\log_{10}\tilde{\sigma}_{v}-\gamma(z)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle-\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{v}\times 10^{-\gamma(z)}\right)^{1/\ln(10)}\,,

and the comoving galaxy number density is given by

\tilde{n}^{\mathrm{com}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{v},z)=-\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{N}(>\tilde{\sigma}_{v},z)}{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\sigma}_{v}}\,,

(17)

where we defined the dimensionless quantities

\displaystyle\tilde{N}

\displaystyle=N\times$1\text{\,}\mathrm{Mpc}$\,,

(18)

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}_{v}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{v}}{$1\text{\,}\mathrm{km}$}\,,

(19)

\displaystyle\tilde{n}^{\mathrm{com}}

\displaystyle=n\times$1\text{\,}\mathrm{Mpc}$\times{$1\text{\,}\mathrm{km}$}\,.

(20)

In Eq. (
16
) the functions
A
,
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
take into account a redshift evolution in the model. Their expressions are

\displaystyle A(z)

\displaystyle=a_{0}+a_{1}z+a_{2}z^{2}\,,

(21)

\displaystyle\alpha(z)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}z+\alpha_{2}z^{2}\,,

(22)

\displaystyle\beta(z)

\displaystyle=\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}z+\beta_{2}z^{2}\,,

(23)

\displaystyle\gamma(z)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{0}+\gamma_{1}z+\gamma_{2}z^{2}\,.

(24)

The values for the different parameters are given in Tab.
1
.

i

i=0

i=1

i=2

A_{i}

7.391498

5.729400

-1.120552

\alpha_{i}

-6.863393

2.852083
