Coefficients for the redshift evolution parametrisation of the Illustris’ simulation.

We show in Fig.
2
a comparison between Illustris’ and the Bernardi’s model.

Figure 2:

Galaxy number density, comparison between two models

Left:
Comparison of the galaxy number density between Illustris’ and Bernardi’s models, evaluated at
z=0

Right:
Illustris’ model, evaluated for three different sources redshifts

It is clear that the Illustris’ simulation contains more galaxies. Using one or the other model can lead to very different results. Moreover, we see that the Illustris’ simulation contains more galaxies at high redshift.

Appendix 18.C
Estimators for the Correlation Functions

The estimators for the correlation functions defined in Section
3
are

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{+}(\mu,z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=\Xi\,F_{1}(\beta_{1}^{\prime},\beta_{2}^{\prime},\beta_{1},\beta_{2})+\Xi^{\prime}\,F_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{1}^{\prime},\beta_{2}^{\prime})\,,

(25)

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{-}(\mu,z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=\Xi\,F_{2}(\beta_{1}^{\prime},\beta_{2}^{\prime},\beta_{1},\beta_{2})+\Xi^{\prime}\,F_{2}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{1}^{\prime},\beta_{2}^{\prime})\,,

(26)

where the functions
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are

F_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{1}^{\prime},\beta_{2}^{\prime})=\frac{\cos(2(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}))}{\cos(2(\beta_{1}^{\prime}-\beta_{2}^{\prime}))\cos(2(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}))-\cos(2(\beta_{1}^{\prime}+\beta_{2}^{\prime}))\cos(2(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}))}\,,

(28)

and

F_{2}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{1}^{\prime},\beta_{2}^{\prime})=\frac{\cos(2(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}))}{\cos(2(\beta_{1}^{\prime}+\beta_{2}^{\prime}))\cos(2(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}))-\cos(2(\beta_{1}^{\prime}-\beta_{2}^{\prime}))\cos(2(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}))}\,.

(29)

Appendix 18.D
Flat Sky Approximation

1
General Description

We briefly present here the flat sky approximation. Some details can be found in
[
113
,
30
,
200
,
201
,
18
]
. Surprisingly enough, I did not find any reference covering the topic in a systematic and rigorous way.

The main idea if the following: For large values of
\ell
(roughly for
\ell>100
), if we observe a small patch of the sky around a fixed direction
\boldsymbol{n}
, we can assume that it is a plane. To simplify the expressions, we assume that we observe the North Pole, but the final results are general (especially they hold even if we perform the computations on the Equator, as we did in Section
5
). Around this point, we cannot use the usual spherical coordinates, and we use the Cartesian coordinates. More precisely, a point
P
is given by

P=(x,y,\sqrt{1-x^{2}-y^{2}})\,.

(30)

We will work at linear order in
x
and
y
, as we consider a very small patch around the North Pole. We project the point
P
on the plane
z=1
and obtain

P^{\prime}=\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}-y^{2}}},\frac{y}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}-y^{2}}},1\right)\approx(x,y,1)\,.

(31)

This is the definition of the flat sky coordinates of a point, in order words, we define the vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
on the plane by

\boldsymbol{\alpha}(P)=(\alpha^{1},\alpha^{2})=(x,y)\,.

(32)

Moreover, it can be shown directly that the norm of the vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is given by the usual angle
\theta
(which is also true at the Pole), and that the polar angle
\varphi_{\alpha}
in the
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
coordinates is also the usual polar angle
\varphi
. This setup is shown in Fig.
3
.

Figure 3:

Representation of the flat sky approximation

Left:
A point
P
with coordinates
(\theta,\varphi)
on the sphere is mapped onto a point
P^{\prime}
on the flat sky plane.

Right:
The flat sky plane with coordinates
(\alpha^{1},\alpha^{2})
seen from above. The point
P^{\prime}
is located at
(\alpha^{1},\alpha^{2})=\theta(\cos\varphi_{\alpha},\sin\varphi_{\alpha})
, with the simple relation
\varphi_{\alpha}=\varphi
.

Let us consider a real field
X
on the sphere with the usual decomposition

\displaystyle X(\boldsymbol{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell,m}X_{\ell,m}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}(\boldsymbol{n})\,,

(33)

\displaystyle X_{\ell,m}

\displaystyle=\int\;X(\boldsymbol{n})Y^{\star}_{\ell,m}(\boldsymbol{n})\,\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}\,.

(34)

Using the coefficients
X_{\ell,m}
, we want to define a flat sky field
{\mathcal{X}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
on the
2D
-plane. First, we define its Fourier coefficients. Let
\boldsymbol{\ell}
be a
2D
-vector of norm
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
defined as

\boldsymbol{\ell}=\ell(\cos\varphi_{\ell},\sin\varphi_{\ell})\,.

(35)

The Fourier coefficients in the plane are defined by

{\mathcal{X}}(\boldsymbol{\ell})\equiv\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\ell+1}}\sum_{m}\mathrm{i}^{-m}X_{\ell,m}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}m\varphi_{\ell}}\,.

(36)

We can extend analytically this field to all values of
\boldsymbol{\ell}
. In this definition,
m
goes from
-\ell
to
+\ell
and
\varphi_{\ell}
is the polar angle of
\boldsymbol{\ell}
in the
2D
-Fourier plane. The inverse transformation is given by

X_{\ell,m}=\sqrt{2\ell+1}\,\mathrm{i}^{m}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\varphi_{\ell}}{2\pi}\;\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}m\varphi_{\ell}}{\mathcal{X}}(\boldsymbol{\ell})\,,

(37)

where
\boldsymbol{\ell}=\ell(\cos\varphi_{\ell},\sin\varphi_{\ell})
has norm
\ell
. We define then the flat sky field as

{\mathcal{X}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\int\;\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\ell}^{2}}{2\pi}{\mathcal{X}}(\boldsymbol{\ell})\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\ell}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,.

(38)

It is direct to show that the power spectrum agree, i.e.

\displaystyle\langle X_{\ell_{1},m_{1}}X^{\star}_{\ell_{2},m_{2}}\rangle

\displaystyle=C_{\ell_{1}}^{X}\delta_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\delta_{m_{1},m_{2}}\,,

(39)

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{X}}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1}}{\mathcal{X}}^{\star}_{\boldsymbol{\ell}_{2}}\rangle

\displaystyle=C_{\ell_{1}}^{X}\delta(\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\ell}_{2})\,.

(40)

Moreover, it can be shown (but it is neither direct nor easy) that the field
{\mathcal{X}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
is locally the original field, i.e.

{\mathcal{X}}(\alpha^{1},\alpha^{2})=X(\theta,\varphi)\,,

(41)

where
\theta
and
\varphi
depend on
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
as explained above.

2
Dictionary

Technically, to use the flat sky approximation, we should use Eq. (
34
), Eq. (
36
) and Eq. (
38
) to replace
X(\boldsymbol{n})
by
{\mathcal{X}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
at the beginning of any computation, and then use Eq. (
40
) to compute correlations. However, we can use Eq. (
34
) and Eq. (
38
) and impose

\displaystyle X(\boldsymbol{n})

\displaystyle\rightarrow{\mathcal{X}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,,

(42)

\displaystyle X_{\ell,m}

\displaystyle\rightarrow{\mathcal{X}}(\boldsymbol{\ell})\,.

(43)

to get the golden rule

\displaystyle\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}(\theta,\varphi)\rightarrow\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\ell}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,,

(44)

which is what we do in practice. In the computations, sums and integrals are replaced by

\displaystyle\int\;\dots\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\Omega^{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\int\;\dots\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,,

(45)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell,m}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\int\;\dots\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\ell}\,,

(46)

where we stress that both
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{\ell}
are
2D
vectors. In this approximation, the slashed derivatives become

\displaystyle\not{\partial}

\displaystyle\rightarrow-\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha^{1}}-\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha^{2}}\,,

(47)

\displaystyle{\not{\partial}^{\star}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow-\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha^{1}}+\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha^{2}}\,,

(48)

\displaystyle(\not{\partial})^{2}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}(\theta,\varphi)

\displaystyle\rightarrow-\frac{\ell^{2}}{2\pi}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}\varphi_{\ell}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\ell}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,,

(49)

\displaystyle({\not{\partial}^{\star}})^{2}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}(\theta,\varphi)

\displaystyle\rightarrow-\frac{1}{2\pi}\ell^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}\varphi_{\ell}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\ell}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,,

(50)

\displaystyle\nu_{\ell}\,{{}_{\pm 2}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow-\ell^{2}\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathrm{e}^{\pm 2\mathrm{i}\varphi_{\ell}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\ell}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,.

(51)

3
Explicit Computations

We use this
dictionary
to compute the correlation functions of Section
2
. We consider two directions
\boldsymbol{n}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{n}_{2}
with
\boldsymbol{n}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{2}=\cos\theta
(Note that in Section
2
, the angle between the two direction is denoted
\varphi
as computations are made in the equatorial plane). For example, the first correlation function (Eq. (
73
)) is given by

\displaystyle\langle\gamma^{+}(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\gamma^{-}(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\ell,m}C^{\phi}_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})\nu_{\ell}^{2}{{}_{+2}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.70505pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.5036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m}}}}}(\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\,{{}_{+2}Y^{\star}_{\ell,m}}(\boldsymbol{n}_{2})

(52)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\int\;C^{\phi}_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})\ell^{4}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\ell}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\ell}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\ell}\,.

(53)

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}=0
. Using the results of the previous section, we get that the norm of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}
is
\theta
. We rotate the coordinates such that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}
is in the first direction. The integral over the angle
\varphi_{l}
can be computed first and reads

\int\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\boldsymbol{\ell}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}\varphi_{\ell}=\int\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\ell\theta\cos\varphi_{\ell}}\,\mathrm{d}\varphi_{\ell}=2\pi J_{0}(\ell\theta)\,,

(54)

where we used the definition of the Bessel function of order
n

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\;\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}n\varphi_{\ell}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}y\cos\varphi_{\ell}}\,\mathrm{d}\varphi_{\ell}=2\pi\mathrm{i}^{n}J_{n}(y)\,.

(55)

Using this, the sum in the correlation function Eq. (
74
) becomes

\displaystyle\langle\gamma^{+}(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\gamma^{-}(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int\;C^{\phi}_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})\ell^{5}J_{0}(\ell\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\ell\,.

(56)

The correlations functions
\langle\gamma^{+}(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\gamma^{+}(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle
and
\langle\gamma^{-}(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\gamma^{-}(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle
are obtained following the same steps. The only differences is that the phases do not cancel, and we are left with a phase
\pm 4\mathrm{i}\ell\varphi_{\ell}
, leading to

\displaystyle\langle\gamma^{+}(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\gamma^{+}(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int\;C^{\phi}_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})\ell^{5}J_{4}(\ell\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\ell\,,

(57)

\displaystyle\langle\gamma^{-}(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\gamma^{-}(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int\;C^{\phi}_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})\ell^{5}J_{4}(\ell\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\ell\,.

(58)

Recall the relations

\displaystyle\gamma^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}\pm\mathrm{i}\gamma_{2}\,,

(59)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\mathrm{p}}(z_{1},z_{2},\theta)

\displaystyle=\langle\gamma_{1}(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\gamma_{1}(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle\,,

(60)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\mathrm{c}}(z_{1},z_{2},\theta)

\displaystyle=\langle\gamma_{2}(z_{1},\boldsymbol{n}_{1})\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\boldsymbol{n}_{2})\rangle\,,

(61)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\pm}(z_{1},z_{2},\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\zeta_{\mathrm{p}}(z_{1},z_{2};\mu)+\zeta_{\mathrm{c}}(z_{1},z_{2},\theta)\right)\,,

(62)

where the second and third relations hold in the preferred coordinates system described in Section
2
(see also Eq. (
61
) and Eq. (
62
)). Combining all of this yields

\displaystyle\zeta_{+}(z_{1},z_{2},\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\;\ell^{5}\,J_{0}(\ell\theta)\,\frac{1}{4}C^{\phi}_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\ell\,

(63)

\displaystyle\zeta_{-}(z_{1},z_{2},\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\;\ell^{5}\,J_{4}(\ell\theta)\,\frac{1}{4}C^{\phi}_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\ell\,,

(64)

As an example, we compute the correlation function for the lensing potential using the full sky formula given by

\zeta^{\phi}(z_{1},z_{2},\ \theta)=\sum_{\ell}\frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi}C^{\phi}_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})P_{\ell}(\cos\theta)\,,

(65)

and the flat sky approximation

\zeta^{\phi}(z_{1},z_{2},\ \theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\;\ell\,C^{\phi}_{\ell}(z_{1},z_{2})J_{0}(\ell\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\ell\,.

(66)

Figure 4:

Correlation function for the lensing potential, comparison between the full sky formula and the flat sky approximation (with
z_{1}=z_{2}=1
)

The full sky formula is given by Eq. (
217
), and the flat sky approximation can be obtained following the same steps as above (see also
[
66
]
). We show in Fig.
4
the correlation function of the lensing potential (with
z_{1}=z_{2}=1
) using the full sky formula and the flat sky approximation. We see that the relative error is of order of the percent for
\varphi<200\,\mathrm{arcmin}
. Both formulas were truncated at
\ell=25^{\prime}000
.

Appendix 18.E
Spherical Harmonics

1
Explicit Formulas

We give the explicit expressions for the Spherical Harmonics for up to
s=2
and
\ell=2
in Tab.
2
and Tab.
3
. The missing expressions can be deduced from the conjugation relation Eq. (
203
).

m

\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.11337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.79526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.11337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.79526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.11337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.79526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1,m}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.71188pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.11337pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.79526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1,m}}}}(\theta,\varphi)

2
Addition Theorem

The addition theorem of Spin Weighted Spherical Harmonics is useful to compute correlation functions (for example in Section
2
). For generic spins, it reads

\sqrt{\frac{4\pi}{2\ell+1}}\sum_{m^{\prime}}{{}_{s_{1}}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.9205pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.9205pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.16754pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.9661pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m^{\prime}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.9205pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.9205pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.16754pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.9661pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m^{\prime}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.9205pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.9205pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.16754pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.9661pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m^{\prime}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.9205pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.9205pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.16754pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.9661pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,m^{\prime}}}}}}(\theta_{2},\varphi_{2}){{}_{-s_{2}}Y^{\star}_{\ell,m^{\prime}}}(\theta_{1},\varphi_{1})=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}s_{1}\gamma}{{}_{s_{1}}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.70122pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.70122pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.94965pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.32118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,s_{2}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.70122pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.70122pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.94965pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.32118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,s_{2}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.70122pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.70122pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.94965pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.32118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,s_{2}}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.70122pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.70122pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.94965pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.32118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,s_{2}}}}}}(\beta,\alpha)\,.

(67)

In this formula, the angles
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
are defined via

R_{\mathrm{E}}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=R_{\mathrm{E}}(\varphi_{1},\theta_{1},0)^{-1}R_{\mathrm{E}}(\varphi_{2},\theta_{2},0)\,,

(68)

and
R_{\mathrm{E}}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
is the rotation matrix with the Euler angles
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
given by

\displaystyle R_{\mathrm{E}}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=

(69)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\cos\alpha\cos\beta\cos\gamma-\sin\alpha\sin\gamma&\ &-\cos\gamma\sin\alpha-\cos\alpha\cos\beta\sin\gamma&\ &\cos\alpha\sin\beta\\
\cos\beta\cos\gamma\sin\alpha+\cos\alpha\sin\gamma&\ &\cos\alpha\cos\gamma-\cos\beta\sin\alpha\sin\gamma&\ &\sin\alpha\sin\beta\\
-\cos\gamma\sin\beta&\ &\sin\beta\sin\gamma&\ &\cos\beta\end{pmatrix}\,.

The only relevant example is

\displaystyle\theta_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}\quad

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad

(70)

\displaystyle\theta_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}

\displaystyle=\varphi\,,

(71)

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=\varphi

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi}{2}\,.

(72)

3
The Functions
\tilde{Q}
and
\tilde{P}

In order to compute the shear correlation functions, it is useful to define the functions
\tilde{P}_{\ell}(\mu)
and
\tilde{Q}_{\ell}(\mu)
as

\displaystyle\frac{2\ell+1}{16\pi}\tilde{Q}_{\ell}(\mu)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-\sqrt{\frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi}}\;_{+2}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.55719pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.55719pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.6639pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.90279pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,+2}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.55719pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.55719pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.6639pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.90279pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,+2}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.55719pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.55719pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.6639pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.90279pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,+2}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[17.55719pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[17.55719pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.6639pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.90279pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,+2}}}}(\theta,\pi/2)\penalty\ =\penalty\ -\sqrt{\frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi}}\;{{}_{-2}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.48611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.34721pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-2}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.48611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.34721pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-2}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.48611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.34721pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-2}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.48611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.34721pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-2}}}}(\theta,\pi/2)}\,,

(73)

\displaystyle\frac{2\ell+1}{16\pi}\tilde{P}_{\ell}(\mu)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-\sqrt{\frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi}}\;_{+2}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.48611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.34721pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-2}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.48611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.34721pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-2}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.48611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.34721pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-2}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.48611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.34721pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-2}}}}(\theta,\pi/2)\penalty\ =\penalty\ -\sqrt{\frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi}}\;_{+2}\mathchoice{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.48611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.34721pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-2}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.48611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.34721pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-2}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.48611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.34721pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-2}}}}{Y^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.11273pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.48611pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.34721pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\ell,-2}}}}(\theta,\pi/2)\,.

(74)

From the orthonormality condition of the Spin Weighted Spherical Harmonics Eq. (
202
), it follows that

\int_{-1}^{+1}\;\tilde{P}_{\ell_{1}}(\mu)\tilde{P}_{\ell_{2}}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{-1}^{+1}\;\tilde{Q}_{\ell_{1}}(\mu)\tilde{Q}_{\ell_{2}}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\frac{32}{2\ell_{1}+1}\delta_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\,.

(75)

General Relativity

1
Odd Perturbations

The relation between the metric perturbations for the odd modes defined in Section
2
is

h_{0}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}(r_{\mathrm{g}}-r)}{r^{3}\omega}\left(r_{\mathrm{g}}h_{1}+r(r-r_{\mathrm{g}})h_{1}^{\prime}\right)\,.

(1)

Even Perturbations

The relation between the metric perturbations for the even modes defined in Section
2
is

\begin{pmatrix}H_{0}\\
H_{1}^{\prime}\\
K_{0}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\boldsymbol{\Pi}\begin{pmatrix}H_{1}\\
K_{0}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(2)

with

\boldsymbol{\Pi}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\mathrm{i}\left(J^{2}r_{\mathrm{g}}-4r^{3}\omega^{2}\right)}{2r\omega\left(\left(J^{2}-2\right)r+3r_{\mathrm{g}}\right)}&\frac{-2\left(J^{2}-2\right)r^{2}+2\left(J^{2}-3\right)rr_{\mathrm{g}}+4r^{4}\omega^{2}+3r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}}{2(r-r_{\mathrm{g}})\left(\left(J^{2}-2\right)r+3r_{\mathrm{g}}\right)}\\
&\\
\frac{\left(4-3J^{2}\right)rr_{\mathrm{g}}+4r^{4}\omega^{2}-6r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}}{2r(r-r_{\mathrm{g}})\left(\left(J^{2}-2\right)r+3r_{\mathrm{g}}\right)}&\frac{2\mathrm{i}r\omega\left(\left(2-J^{2}\right)r^{2}+\left(J^{2}-4\right)rr_{\mathrm{g}}+r^{4}\omega^{2}+\frac{9r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}}{4}\right)}{(r-r_{\mathrm{g}})^{2}\left(\left(J^{2}-2\right)r+3r_{\mathrm{g}}\right)}\\

\frac{\mathrm{i}\left(2J^{2}(r_{\mathrm{g}}-r)+J^{4}r+4r^{3}\omega^{2}\right)}{2r^{2}\omega\left(\left(J^{2}-2\right)r+3r_{\mathrm{g}}\right)}&\frac{\left(J^{2}-6\right)rr_{\mathrm{g}}-4r^{4}\omega^{2}+6r_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}}{2r(r-r_{\mathrm{g}})\left(\left(J^{2}-2\right)r+3r_{\mathrm{g}}\right)}\end{pmatrix}\,,
